#8502. Thankful Even When Your Wealth And Health Are Both Down

I don't know what's worse. Feeling incredibly sick or losing a ton of money in a very short time. What say you?

Lucky for me, I'm both sick and my public investments are down ~15% from their peak. In about 45 days, the market wiped out over three years of our living expenses. Getting beat up both ways hurts.

First, there's feeling dumb for not reducing more risk before the correction. I've been doing this for over 30 years. I should know better. Greed is a hell of a drug, especially when the 10-year bond yield is at ~5%. And the pain is probably not over.

Second, there's not being able to function at full strength. Every task takes twice the effort and gets half the result. It's hard for me to breathe and move at the moment. My lungs are bruised from all the dry coughing. Then there's the achy bones.

If you're thinking about having both spouses retire early, think thrice. When the market tanks and your health falters, having one spouse who still brings in a paycheck and subsidized health insurance is incredibly comforting.

But I'm still alive and somewhat kicking. So this Thanksgiving, I'm thankful.

A healthy man wants a thousand things. A sick man wants only one: to feel better.

When you're sick long enough, your mind starts wandering to dark places.

What if I don't get better? What if I only get worse? Thank goodness both my wife and I have matching 20-year term life insurance policies to get our kids through to adulthood.

Then my mind wanders to the market. What if this correction turns into a full-blown bear market as Congress throttles AI growth, which may lead to a recession? Anything is possible, which is why having an appropriate net worth allocation by age is important.

For the longest time, I've kept public equities between 25% and 35% of my net worth. That's the range I'm comfortable with for handling all the funny money volatility.

But I got lazy and let my exposure drift up to about 45%. Then I got hammered.

If you're feeling down and out, it's worth going through a list of things you're thankful for. It will make you feel better. Here are some off the top of my head.

The highlight of the week came on Monday. I felt just strong enough to take the kids and wife to Alcatraz on a gorgeous, sunny San Francisco day, right before the rainstorm rolled in. Tuesday was when my energy began to plummet.

It was their first time visiting the island, and their first time ever on a boat funny enough. Watching them stand at the railing as the city skyline got larger on our return was a lovely moment.

There's something fitting about touring a prison while feeling trapped by your own body and a falling market.

I spent extra time just sitting in one of the cells, taking in what it would like to be locked up and constantly being told what to do. It brought back old memories when I got in trouble the summer after high school for prank calling. Prison living is the opposite of FIRE living.

The visit also turned into a real-life lesson for the kids about the consequences of bad decisions. Standing in one of the dark solitary confinement cells will hopefully help them appreciate their nice rooms and the importance of being a good person. That said, my almost 7-year-old admitted she was a little scared by the end.

Monday reminded me that time with the people you love is the best antidote to a falling portfolio and failing health. It's also the one asset that never comes back once it's gone.

If you've lost a lot of money and are dealing with a health issue, or any other personal setback, here's what's helping me. The number one goal is to hang on until the good times return.

1) Health is the real net worth. No amount of money makes you feel good when you're sick. If you're healthy right now, you're richer than you think, regardless of what your brokerage account says.

2) Measure your losses in time, not dollars. Losing a big chunk of your portfolio sounds catastrophic. But ask how far back the losses take you. Often you're only back to where you were a year or two ago during a correction or bear market. However, if you're sick of work, then a more conservative portfolio adjustment should be made.

3) Don't make big decisions while you're sick or distraught. Selling everything at the bottom feels like relief in the moment. It's usually the most expensive relief you'll ever buy. Give yourself a cooling-off period before any major move. Leg out or into positions. No wholesale decisions.

4) Focus on what you can control. You can't control the Fed, AI spending, a CEO's escapades, or a virus. You can control your spending, your asset allocation, your sleep, and how you treat the people around you. Lean into spending time with people and things that bring you the most joy.

5) Do one thing that gets you outside your head. For me, it was taking the kids to Alcatraz and painting my downstairs hallway. For you, it might be a walk, a phone call with an old friend, or cooking a meal. Action beats rumination.

6) Write down your gratitude list. When your mind keeps spiraling toward worst-case scenarios, putting the good things in writing is a way to fight back.

Perhaps what I'm most thankful for is the opportunity to try again.

We've been through many corrections and bear markets before. None of them ever felt good. But over time, the stock market recovered, and we moved on with our lives.

The great thing about being at a low point is that there's likely more upside than downside. Yes, things can always get worse. But unless my illness turns fatal and the stock market falls more than 60%, forcing us to return to work, we'll be OK.

Finally, I can't talk well enough to record a podcast right now. But I can still move my fingers and write. So I'm thankful for the ability to keep going, no matter what.

Readers, what are you thankful for this year despite the market correction? Have you ever had to deal with a big financial loss and a health setback at the same time, and how did you get through it? Do you find that losing money makes you more or less grateful for what you have? And what's one thing you plan to do this holiday season to get out of your own head?

#8501. Everest and Ama Dablam Climbs Resume Despite Cyclone

The second cyclone to hit Nepal’s mountains this season caused heavy snowfall last weekend, but its briefer stay has affected climbers and trekkers less. As the sun returned this afternoon, fresh snow hampered smaller teams on 6,000m and 7,000m peaks, but the highly supported expeditions on Ama Dablam are proceeding with barely a hitch.

No cyclone seems enough to deter the Russian team on Everest for very long. Leader Andrei Vasiliev told us yesterday from Kathmandu that he, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov will return to the mountain in one or two days. The trio is well aware of the challenge they face on Everest’s West Ridge, completely alone and without oxygen.

The climbers’ latest foray up Everest’s West Ridge route. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

Vasiliev mentioned he is under some pressure due to unidentified “unsolved matters” but still determined to tackle the climb. In his own words:

We know what needs to be done; the path is clear, and we must attempt to follow it. We’ve fallen ill. A massive amount of snow has fallen. You adapt quickly to new conditions. Once we’re back in the snow, the cold, and the cramped tent, it will feel as though things were never any other way.

The new snow dump has thwarted some teams attempting Baruntse (7,129m) in the Khumbu. Jan McManus of Ireland was among them, and he has called off his expedition after failing to reach Camp 1 due to deep snow:

It is unclear whether other climbers on the peak may still be trying. There were six large teams totaling 65 foreign climbers with permits for Baruntse.

On Himlung (7,126m), teams hurried to summit and get down and safely away before the cyclone hit. During the previous snowstorm at the end of September, a massive avalanche buried Base Camp, killing 16 staff members. Despite the tragedy, one of the deadliest in Nepal’s mountaineering history, the peak remained open, and guided teams came in large numbers to the mountain a couple of weeks after the accident. A total of 130 foreigners had permits. Teams summited on October 25 and 26, and then left the area.

Ama Dablam Base Camp is also covered in snow, but the commercial teams will nevertheless resume climbing soon. According to Jana Schneider of Germany, climbing with 14 Peaks Expeditions, a Sherpa team is on its way to reopen those sections where fresh snow has buried the ropes. “If all goes well, my summit push begins Nov 4 until Nov 6,” she wrote.

So far, 410 foreigners have applied for a permit to climb Ama Dablam. A Sherpa team first fixed the route on October 17, and dozens have summited since then. Despite the break forced by bad weather over the weekend, summits should continue throughout November.

One climber has perished on the mountain so far, and there have been some close calls. Paul Clarke of Australia and Xenia Santha of Hungary climbed Ama Dablam after attempting Dhaulagiri earlier this season. Their summit push was delayed as they found a 25-year-old climber in a serious situation at Camp 2. Santha and Clarke, who climbed without Sherpa support, gave the climber dexamethasone, spent the night with him, and helped organize an emergency airlift for the following morning. They continued to the summit one day later.

A high camp on Ama Dablam. Photo: Xenia Shanta

#8500. Video: Bird Dog Falls 60 Feet into Basalt Chasm, Prompting Special Rescue Mission

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 3:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Two unbelievable sights in one spot: a dog trapped in a deep fissure in the ground, and a crew lowering a man into that fissure to save her.

This scene unfolded in southern Idaho on Nov. 8. After a traveling bird hunter’s pointer fell some 60-feet into a basalt crack while hunting wild birds, he had to call in a professional rescue team for help.

“1,200 miles from home hunting a new bird,” wrote Kyle Stokke in an Instagram post on Nov. 12. “[I] was naively unaware of the unique dangers in these new environments.”

A post shared by Kyle Stokke (@kyle.stokke)

Stokke posted a four-minute rescue video last week. Earlier posts show him hunting upland birds with two dogs: a German shorthaired pointer and a Labrador retriever. The trio camped while traveling 1,200 miles cross-country from the Midwest to Idaho. Trouble started when the GSP, Esther, fell into a deep fissure.

“Good gracious!” an unseen rescuer says in the video. “She is down there a freaking long ways.”

Esther fell at least 60 feet. The fissure went deeper than that, but she landed on a ledge above the true bottom. In the video, you can hear her whines echo up the crack as Stokke, filming from above, reassures her.

“It’s okay Esther,” he says. “You’re a good girl.”

Magic Valley Paramedics has a dedicated unit called the Special Operations Rescue Team. SORT is a highly trained, 20-person volunteer crew specializing in low- and high-angle rope rescues.

“The biggest challenge was finding exactly where she was,” Chad Smith, SORT team leader, tells Outdoor Life. “Sound travels differently in the canyon. You could hear her so she seemed close, but she wasn’t. How sound travels in there is really deceiving. It threw us off a little bit.”

SORT averages 20 rescues a year in Twin Falls and Jerome counties. In Smith’s 15 years with SORT, the team has rescued four dogs from fissures, including another bird dog stuck in a similar crack during a previous hunting season. Esther’s rescue happened to fall on the first day of SORT’s semiannual, three-day training exercises.

“The rescue truck is pulled out and everyone is practicing anchors when the supervisor asks if we want to rescue a dog,” reads a post by the organization. “Who can say no to a dog? Training shifts gears real quick.”

The video shows the team straddling a 2-foot-wide black void hidden in flat sagebrush country.

“Wow! I can see her,” says someone offscreen as one rescuer, Trevor Churchman, is slowly lowered into the tight divide on a harness, ropes, and straps rigged to a large tripod set over the opening.

“Tell Kyle she’s doing good,” says Churchman. “She’s walking around and she’s wagging her tail.”

A few minutes later, both man and dog are hoisted out of the crack, and Esther, apparently unharmed, is handed to Stokke. He’s visibly shaking in the video and sounds emotional, but manages a “thank you” for the crew and a “whoa” to keep Esther steady, since everyone is standing near the edge.

“Okay guys, remember there’s an edge there,” a rescuer says. “We don’t have a lot of you guys on ropes.”

The two-hour rescue took place in terrain shaped by prehistoric volcanic activity. Tunnels, lava tubes, cliffs, caves, and crevices run through much of the area’s undeveloped public land. (If that’s hard to picture, think of the better-known volcanic landscape at Craters of the Moon National Monument and Preserve, 90 miles north of Twin Falls.)

Water filters through the porous basalt rock formations, creating seeps, springs, and waterfalls that feed the Snake River, which has carved a deep canyon that’s home to the dramatic spray of Shoshone Falls.

“When hiking around the canyon, cracks in the rock can be quite deep,” the rescue team warns. “This one was deeper than 45 feet. We could not see the bottom, and they can fill with tumbleweeds making them look like level ground. So watch your step.”

Read Next: Idaho Wildlife Commissioner Finally Resigns as Plea Deal in Elk Poaching Scandal Draws Hunter Outrage

It’s rough, remote country that attracts extreme adventurers like BASE jumpers. And it can surprise even experienced bird hunters, such as Stokke, who assume they’re hunting in relatively flat country.

Kris Millgate is a multimedia journalist based in eastern Idaho. She’s a fly fisher, a bird hunter and an avid seeker of awkward moments. Read and watch more of her work at tightlinemedia.com

#8499. One Dead, Two Injured as Two Helicopters Crash Near Everest Base Camp

An Indian passenger died and two more were injured when two helicopters crashed while landing near Everest Base Camp this morning.

The helicopters were operated by Fishtail Air and Air Dynasty, two of Nepal’s largest companies. The crash occurred at the helipad on the outskirts of the village of Pheriche (4,371m). It’s a typical spot for helicopters to stop and refuel when on their way to and from Everest Base Camp.

As shown in the horrific video below, the two helicopters came from different directions, and both tried to land at the same time. Somehow their rotor blades collided. The inexplicable crash occurred in a wide, flat area with good visibility.

Vasanth Kumar, an Indian trekker, died on the spot, The Tourism Times reported. Shailesh Kishan from New Zealand and Rabindra Prajapati, a senior captain, were seriously injured. Both have been airlifted to hospitals in Kathmandu. They were all on board the Fishtail Air helicopter. The Air Dynasty chopper was carrying cargo.

Even if you use the value of gold in 2020, or 2021, or 2022, the results are not drastically different.

Gold purchased in 1975, 1985, or 2015 has fared quite well. Gold purchased in 1995, or 2005 has fared exceptionally well. Yes, it has large swings. It is something to keep in mind if you are purchasing gold.


If you choose a recent low year for gold to compare to the past. Gold was at a relative low in 2015. Gold purchased in 1975 more than kept up with inflation, as did gold purchased in 1985. Gold purchased in 1995 or 2005 fared exceptionally well.

#8495. Simms Rip Line PRO Review: We Tested This Heavy-Duty Fishing Rain Gear in Downpours and High Winds

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 3:54 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Simms has long been an industry staple for fishing apparel, most notably waders and outerwear. I’ve tested and reviewed many Simms products, including its Challenger lineup of rain gear. While that gear has always performed well, its target market is clear: dedicated recreational anglers. I’ve never bothered to test how Simms outerwear handles the trials and tribulations of everyday use by commercial deckhands or saltwater captains because, frankly, why would I? That’s not the audience it’s intended for, and I wouldn’t expect rain gear designed for landlocked fly anglers to hold up for someone used to hauling crab traps in salt spray and freezing rain all day.

Enter Simms’ new Rip Line Pro series of rain gear, the company’s first attempt at a truly hardcore jacket and bibs designed specifically for watermen. The Rip Line Pro series forgoes much of the aesthetic and, shall we say, fanciness of Simms’ standard recreation-oriented rain gear in favor of pure, rugged functionality. From the moment you see the Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs, it’s clear they aren’t meant for a hike down to your favorite blue-ribbon trout stream. Pick them up and feel them in hand, and that point is driven home. This is not casual rain gear, and it doesn’t sacrifice performance for looks. It’s designed for abuse of the highest order, the kind dished out by Mother Nature on the open sea.

So, how does it handle that? The look and feel right out of the box make a promise, but does the Rip Line Pro series deliver? To find out, I spent a few weeks putting this gear through the wringer in one of the best testing grounds I could think of: the Chesapeake Bay in late fall and early winter. Read on to see how the Rip Line Pro performed.

When it comes to testing gear, sometimes the timing doesn’t line up. Given the monthslong drought in the Mid-Atlantic leading up to my test, I was worried that might be the case here. As it turned out, that worry was misplaced. Exactly one day after the Rip Line Pro gear arrived, the forecast called for an epic deluge paired with extreme wind and storms. It was the perfect blend of miserable fishing conditions for testing rain gear. Lucky me.

The forecast proved accurate, and the weather was extreme, almost to a fault. I spent the first few hours of the morning sheltering from lightning under a dockhouse on the water. The Rip Line Pro gear will protect you from a lot, but I don’t think “lightning strike” is on the list. Thankfully, the electrical portion of the storm passed, and I had the rest of the day to fish in a constant downpour and gusting wind.

After a few hours of fishing from a kayak in the rain and getting sprayed with salt water, the first thing I checked was the areas where most rain gear performs worst: the wrists, neck, and ankles. These are typically the hardest spots to protect because they offer a direct path for water to get beneath the rain gear, and they still need to allow some comfort and range of motion. The Rip Line Pro did an outstanding job of keeping moisture out of all three. The wrists and forearms were the most impressive. They feature an extended 1-millimeter neoprene cuff beneath the outer layer that lets this jacket perform almost like a full dry top. Not only does it keep rain out, but the neoprene cuff creates a seal that kept water from flowing down the sleeve even when my arm was fully submerged. I was equally impressed by the neck. While it doesn’t have the full neoprene seal of the sleeves, the hood design and zipper-and-Velcro combination created an impressively form-fitting seal that kept water from dripping down my back or chest. Finally, the ankle cuffs on the bibs did an excellent job of keeping spray and rainwater away from my feet. I was wearing ankle-height deck boots, and the leg cuffs paired perfectly with them to create an angled seal where no water could enter.

Next, I inspected the storage, namely the various dry pockets on both the jacket and bibs. Each has a well-designed, easily accessible exterior pocket sealed by a watertight zipper and tucked beneath a Velcro storm flap for extra protection. Exterior pockets on rain gear are always a gamble, but accessible waterproof storage is important. The pockets on the Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs performed exceptionally well, even after I opened and closed them many times throughout the day to reach various items. Nothing seeped through the pockets to the interior of the suit, and the pockets kept everything inside them dry and functional.

Dry storage and adjustable cuffs matter, but ultimately the most important job of rain gear is keeping you dry all day long. While I was impressed with how well the Rip Line Pro kept water out of various areas throughout the day, the real test would be how wet and cold I got after fishing in rain and wind for upward of 12 hours.

The answer? Not very. For me, the most impressive part of this gear is the overall protection it offered from the elements. After we returned to the boat ramp and loaded our gear, it was time to strip off the rain suit and inspect the damage. No matter how dry you think you are, a bit of unforeseen moisture usually gets through any rain gear after a long day. Apart from a few wet spots where my shirt had been exposed during the day, I was basically bone-dry. The jacket and bibs also did a great job of blocking wind chill. I had underestimated how well that was working during the day, but looking back, I realized how much the gear kept the wind from affecting my comfort. Not that I needed confirmation, but watching my fishing partners shiver miserably and wring out sopping undershirts provided a sense of warm relief, both physically and emotionally.

Protection from the elements, plain and simple. I’ve worn many bibs and rain jackets over the years, some designed for comfort and others for function. The Rip Line Pro does the best job I’ve found of marrying the two, though function is still clearly the priority.

Once you’re fully suited up, this rain gear makes you feel practically invincible, protected by an impenetrable shell of armor. I’m not saying it’s bulletproof, but I could feel an unrealistic sense of confidence building while I wore it. The materials not only kept me bone-dry through a full day of torrential rain but also provided exceptional wind protection during gusts upward of 30 knots. When it comes to protection from extreme water and wind, the Rip Line Pro series is as good as it gets.

With extreme weather protection as the focus, it’s easy to understand why some other features take a back seat. That said, there are still a few areas I’d like to see Simms improve.

The most noticeable is the lack of mobility. The four-layer construction and fully waterproof material make this gear weatherproof, but they also make it rather cumbersome. Simms added articulation points and adjustable areas where possible, but there’s still room for improvement. At times, I felt a bit like a piece of vacuum-sealed meat, or a video game character bolted into a mech suit. Yes, I felt invincible, but I also had a harder time than I care to admit squatting to grab a sandwich from the cooler.

Insulation is another area that could be better. The Rip Line Pro gear does an incredible job of protecting you from water and wind, but it doesn’t have much interior insulation for true winter use. That’s easy to offset by layering proper cold-weather clothing underneath, but I could see a built-in cold-weather liner helping. Perhaps Simms will release a “winter” edition of the Rip Line Pro in the future with a wool liner or something similar.

The Rip Line Pro series of rain gear is not for everyone. In fact, I would say the target market for this gear is fairly narrow. Most casual anglers and outdoor enthusiasts can get by with less extreme, less expensive rain gear and never have an issue. While this gear is fantastic at keeping you dry, I could see it being overkill for an after-work outing during a light summer shower.

However, what the Rip Line Pro series is designed to do, it does extremely well, and the people who need this type of gear will appreciate it. The Rip Line Pro series is built for serious anglers and people who spend as much time on the water as on dry land. As with any gear in the fishing space, the more purpose-built something becomes, the less universal appeal it tends to have. That said, if you fish as much as I do, serious rain gear becomes less a matter of convenience and more a matter of necessity. The Rip Line Pro jacket and bibs offer the type of performance and functionality I value, and I’m sure many other anglers, deckhands, and charter captains who try them will agree.

#8493. New Route Attempt on Lunag Ri Falls Just Short of Summit

Between October 23 and 27, Alexander Parfenov and Alexander Semenov of Russia opened a demanding new route on 6,895m Lunag Ri in the Rolwaling Himal on the Nepal-Tibet border. They turned back short of the summit, which some sources list as 6,907m.

The pair climbed a previously unexplored southeast-facing buttress of the Lunag Ri massif. The line would have ended on the southeast summit. It involved mixed and rock climbing to M6, aid sections of A2–A3, ice up to WI5–WI6 in places, and long stretches of steep snow with limited protection.

They placed a piton on the final part of the pillar and began rappelling from there, reaching the base of the wall on October 28, according to gore-ljudje.net. The duo turned back short of the summit due to a coming storm and health issues. The complex summit area — multiple closely linked peaks and ridge features — also left some uncertainty about how close they came to the main summit.

Even without summiting, this was a significant exploration of a major, unclimbed feature on a remote peak.

The new line opened by the Parfenov and Semenov. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

Lunag Ri, the highest peak in the Lunag massif, has long stood as one of Nepal’s highest unclimbed summits. Early attention focused on the broader massif after Joe Puryear and David Gottlieb made the first ascent of the neighboring 6,778m Jobo Rinjang via its South Face in 2009.

In the same year, a Swiss-led team under Stephane Schaffter attempted lines in the area, including approaches to Lunag I, though their claims about summits on the massif were later met with skepticism in the American Alpine Journal.

In the autumn of 2010, a French team that included Max Belleville, Mathieu Detrie, Mathieu Maynadier, and Sebastien Ratel climbed the southeast face of Lunag Ri. Their route, Close the Door (1,200 m, IV/5 F5), was completed in a three-day alpine-style push with two bivouacs. They reached a distinct southeast top (6,812m to 6,830m) on the long ridge connecting Lunag I to Jobo Rinjang. By this time, they were exhausted and did not continue the short remaining traverse to the higher main summit. This remains the only major completed route on the Lunag I massif before the first ascent of the true summit.

From the recent climb on Lunag Ri. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

Attention then shifted to the west ridge and southwest face of the main peak. In November 2015, Conrad Anker and David Lama’s alpine-style attempt reached a high point roughly 300m below the summit before retreating due to the unexpected complexity of the ridge.

They returned in 2016 to try a similar line. On the first day, at about 5,800m, Anker suffered a heart attack and was evacuated by helicopter. Lama subsequently continued alone on a variation that moved farther right onto an icefield. He established two bivouacs and pushed 50m higher than the previous year’s high point before turning around 250m shy of the summit.

In late October 2018, Lama returned solo. Over three days (October 23-25), with two bivouacs, he climbed the southwest face and northwest/west ridge (1,500m of vertical gain, terrain to 90°). He moved mostly ropeless, with limited self-belaying on the harder mixed sections. Lama negotiated the previously unclimbed headwall and made the final exposed traverse onto the main summit.

This was the first ascent of Lunag Ri’s highest point. He descended largely by rappelling down the line of ascent. Lama was awarded a Piolet d’Or for this solo first ascent of Lunag Ri.

From the recent climb on Lunag Ri. Photo: Alexander Parfenov

KrisAnnapurna is a writer with ExplorersWeb.

Kris has been writing about history and tales in alpinism, news, mountaineering, and news updates in the Himalaya, Karakoram, etc., for with ExplorersWeb since 2021. Prior to that, Kris worked as a real estate agent, interpreter, and translator in criminal law. Now based in Madrid, Spain, she was born and raised in Hungary.

#8491. Floating Alone Down the Brahmaputra on a Bamboo Raft

Indian mountaineer and adventurer Shekhar Bordoloi has just started a solo expedition down the Brahmaputra River. He set off on a homemade on a bamboo raft from the Dhola–Sadiya Bridge on November 1 and aims to finish at Dhubri, 891km later. The route covers the entire Brahmaputra within India and should take 22 to 25 days in all. The river begins in Tibet and continues beyond India into Bangladesh, covering 2,900km in all.

This won’t be Bordoloi’s first time down the river. In 2020, he and two friends took a similar bamboo raft down a 400km section of the Brahmaputra. Earlier, in 2015, Bordoloi was one of 11 who tackled another part of the river over 11 days under the auspices of the Assam Mountaineering Association. This expedition, however, will test his endurance, pitting him against river conditions for longer, and with less support, than ever before.

Bordoloi on a previous raft expedition on the Brahmaputra. This raft took Bordoloi and friends from the world’s largest river island, Umananda, to its smallest, Majuli. Photo: Shekhar Bordoloi/Instagram

Bordoloi’s raft was hand-built from bamboo poles carefully lashed together over several days. Such craft are a traditional way of navigating rivers across the region, and are still used by local communities for fishing and transportation.

Though the river can be temperamental, Bordoloi’s first two days have been relatively calm, taking him down the Lohit, one of the Brahmaputra’s many tributaries and channels. Navigating the complex river system alone will be a challenge. The Brahmaputra is one of the largest river systems in India, the ninth-largest in the world by discharge, and is prone to flooding and powerful currents.

But according to Bordoloi, those obscure, winding corridors are at the heart of this project. Many sections of the river remain underexplored, and he believes there is hidden potential for the people in his home region of Assam. If he can map routes for river tourism and model environmentally responsible river navigation through those routes, he hopes to draw attention to this important riverine ecosystem and the economic opportunities for the people living along it.

The logistical challenges of a long expedition will further complicate progress. On previous expeditions, Bordoloi traveled with minimal supplies or no food at all, relying entirely on foraging and knowledge of the natural landscape. But this adventure will take over twice as long and may put those skills to their limits.

Bordoloi is documenting his daily progress on Facebook and Instagram.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8485. Historic Drought Looms Over Arkansas Duck Opener. What Few Ducks Show Up Face Heavy Pressure

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 4:45 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

If you asked any serious waterfowl hunter to name their dream duck-hunting destinations, Arkansas would be at the top of nearly every list. Its classic flooded hardwoods and bayous along with the state’s rich rice and grain fields, lure massive flights of puddle ducks and geese to its hallowed waters every year. This has made Arkansas stand out above other southern states as the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway.

Yet in 2026, Arkansas’ waterfowl season is suddenly looking bleak. A historic drought has many duck hunters worried ahead of the Nov. 21 opener, as the serious lack of water could bring duck hunting to a standstill. Duck hunters on both public and private land may have some serious decisions to make to safeguard ducks and future duck seasons.

For 14 months Arkansas has been without any serious rainfall, leaving rich croplands and, more importantly for ducks, wetlands, drier than they’ve been in decades. Every corner of the state is under a severe drought status, with some areas being more than 20 inches behind in cumulative rainfall totals.

To put the drought in perspective, this is slated to be the state’s third-driest October through January since record keeping began in 1895.

This leaves the Arkansas Game and Fish Commission with very little water to spare, to the point where the agency expects it will only be able to flood around 40 of its more than 70 moist-soil public-land hunting units.

“In my 50 years of duck hunting in Arkansas, I don’t remember it ever being as dry as this,” says Brent Birch, founder of Arkansas’ Greenhead magazine. “It’s been this way all summer and well into the fall. And even with the prospects of a Super El Niño, we have nothing the forecast that says it’s going to resolve anytime soon.”

Without an incredible amount of rainfall in a short amount of time, Birch and other waterfowlers around the state are worried not only about not having places to hunt, but that the ducks may not even show up.

“So far, we are seeing a few groups of gadwalls, pintails, and other early migrating ducks but not in anything close to their usual numbers,” Birch says. “And when it comes to incoming mallards, you can pretty much forget it. It’s just too dry for them. The fact is the closer it gets to 2027 under these conditions, the worse the entire duck season is going to be. And if we don’t start getting some wet weather soon to push ducks down here, we’re looking at a poor outlook for the entire season.”

The reason that Arkansas waterfowl hunting is so good is that it lies at the north end of the historic feeding grounds for ducks and geese in the Mississippi Flyway. Once the cold sets in around their spring and summer breeding grounds in Canada, the birds come south to binge on Arkansas’ rich wetlands throughout the winter. However, with the current drought, the fear is that the incoming ducks will be forced to fly elsewhere.

“Ducks coming south to Arkansas aren’t like ducks in northern states,” says Ducks Unlimited Arkansas regional biologist Jake Spears, who lives in Jonesboro. “Northern birds are more apt to feed in dry fields so long as they have a body of water to roost on nearby. However, in the Southern U.S., they stop feeding in fields and primarily feed in water. They look for things like water plants and waste grains in flooded fields, which Arkansas currently just doesn’t have.”

Spears worries that many ducks are going to fly to Arkansas and then be forced to turn northward again or keep driving south.

“The ducks need food so they may go to Louisiana, or Kansas, or Missouri — wherever they can find that food source,” says Spears. “Which is going to leave both public-land duck hunters and even most hunting clubs just completely out of luck.”

Private-land duck-hunting clubs are a popular option for many Arkansas hunters and visiting nonresidents, as even during droughts, most clubs can pump water to create duck habitat and attract birds. The 2026 drought has been so bad, however, that many clubs won’t even have the water to pump.

This is especially troubling to some public-land waterfowl hunters, who worry that the private hunting clubs that can still pump water may end up attracting what few ducks do make it to Arkansas. This could result in a season that benefits the haves over the have-nots.

“I think hunting clubs are the only thing that’s going to make Arkansas appealing for ducks right now,” Spears says. “Most of the smaller rivers, sloughs, and backwaters we have on public land can be walked across in flip-flops right now. So the clubs that have the option to flood fields and create habitat are where the birds are going to be. But with the water table so low, clubs with the ability to pump will be few and far between, so any ducks that do show up during the season are going to have to be babied quite a bit.”

The fact is that if the drought continues in Arkansas, duck hunters on both public and private land alike are going to have to weigh their priorities. Drought conditions are going to be tough on ducks that migrate through Arkansas, and hunting pressure will make surviving the migration harder even for birds that aren’t killed.

This leaves duck hunters with a choice to make.

“It’s going to come down to responsible management on all sides because if we want to maintain the ducks we have, we have to be careful with them,” Birch says. “There are several components to management. It’s not just hunting and the noise of guns, we must be careful disturbing the ducks in any capacity. Your vehicle is an issue because you’re going to bump ducks going in and out of your hunting area, and you’re going to have to be real careful with how many days you hunt and how many hours you hunt in a day, because you’re going to have to let the birds rest.”

With Arkansas’ duck season lasting a total of 51 days with two relatively short splits, hunters looking to limit out consistently are going to be putting serious pressure on what will already be a limited resource.

So instead of hunting hard, both Birch and Spears recommend self-imposed bag limits and time on the water so that the resource can continue to both be conserved and shared.

“My suggestion to any duck hunter in Arkansas is that if you have ducks on your property, take care of them,” says Spears. “With the limited resources, they aren’t going to be able to handle a lot of pressure. You’re going to have to limit how much you hunt and how many birds you shoot, and not be afraid to cut your hunt short. Get a few birds and then leave so the ducks can get back to the water to feed.”

Read Next: Has Hot Cropping Ruined Duck Hunting? Here’s What the Data and Biologists Say About How Flooded Corn Affects Waterfowl Migration

While there is hope that conditions will change, the fact is that the whole of Arkansas’ 2026 duck season may be a nonstarter for many hunters. That short-term pain, however, could help future duck seasons look a little brighter. Spears and Birch are hopeful for a future where water and the waterfowl will eventually return, filling the skies and waterways like flowing veins and once again making Arkansas the beating heart of the Mississippi Flyway itself.

“We need to be good stewards of the resource,” urges Spears. “Hunters should do everything they can to keep [ducks] around and not shoot them off what water we do have.”

#8483. Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Filling Your Tag After Opening Day

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 5:30 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

These days, a buck can’t grow to trophy proportions on hard-hunted public lands by folding up under the pressure exerted by the opening-day crush of hunters. A whitetail lives the 3½ years necessary to grow a bragging set of antlers by learning to cope with the annual influx of hunters during the season’s first days. Every opening day that the buck survives increases his chance of surviving the ensuing openers, as well.

Some big bucks live in locations that allow them to avoid the opening weekend crowd by slipping into the sanctuary offered by state parks, game refuges or private posted land. Others crawl into the most inaccessible and gosh-awful cover their “territory” has to offer — the kind of place hunters walk around, rather than through — and stay there until the crowds leave. And on more than one occasion, I have found a bruiser buck in a tiny, secluded patch of isolated cover — the kind of place most hunters never think to look in.

But the whitetail is a homebody at heart, and he is most comfortable on his loosely defined home turf. You can bet that once the steady crack of opening day rifles slackens to only an occasional pop, those big bucks will be right back to business as usual. And that is the precise reason why your best chance for bagging a wall-hanger occurs after the opening day madness has subsided.

Where you begin your search for that after-the-opener buck hinges upon one factor: the rut. Many states plan their firearms seasons to coincide with the rut, offering their hunters every opportunity to harvest the surplus bucks. Other states, bound in tradition, open the season after the main rut has ended. If you are planning to travel out of your home state to hunt whitetails, it makes sense to hunt a state where the season coincides with the rut, all other criteria being equal.

A mature whitetail buck is a different character during the rut than he is after it. A buck’s sole purpose at that time is to procreate, and this he does with obvious enthusiasm and the stamina of a teenager. There are stories abounding in every deer camp in the country of the crazy antics performed by bucks under the spell of the “rutting moon,” such as lovesick bucks strolling across open fields in broad daylight. Others tell tales of bucks putting their noses to does’ tracks and following those tracks, even after being shot at and missed several times. Rut-crazed bucks, while under the influence of a powerful rush of hormones, have even been known to either pick fights with or attempt to mate with assorted farm livestock.

So rampant are the stories that I’m afraid there exists a whole contingent of deer hunters laboring under the false assumption that all a hunter needs do to down one of these crazy buggers is to walk into the woods, lean up against a stump and wait for one of the poor, sex-addled dummies to saunter on by. Although such tactics work just often enough to keep the myth perpetuated, plenty of hunters have been sitting against stumps for more unsuccessful years than they will probably admit, and they’re still waiting for a buck to wander by.

Hunting whitetails during the rut and after the opening weekend of the season isn’t easy. You must employ all of the skill and woods savvy you have. Any breaks you receive because of a buck’s overactive glands should be considered a bonus.

After the opener, I refine my hunting strategy for big bucks by determining what stage the rut is in. This is important because the stage of the rut will determine where I concentrate most of my midweek hunting efforts.

The whitetail buck is ready to breed long before the first doe enters estrus. During this period, the buck runs off excess energy by beating up saplings, sparring with bushes and, on occasion, duking it out with other bucks. In the process of making rubs on bushes and trees, the buck deposits scents that tell the rest of the whitetail community who was there.

Scrapes begin to show up in increasing numbers as the level of testosterone builds in the buck’s system. The buck paws out scrapes and leaves his scent both in the bare earth of the scrapes and on overhanging branches above the scrapes. Again, this is a means of communicating with other whitetails. The message to does is that the buck is now standing at stud, and it tells other bucks that they had best move along if they know what’s good for them.

A buck will paw out most of his scrapes in areas where they are most likely to be seen or smelled by does. Openings in woods, old logging roads and the edges of woodlots are common places to encounter scrapes. A buck may make a dozen or more scrapes, and he’ll check some of them daily, making sure they are free of debris and liberally doused with musk/urine. These are the scrapes to hunt over.

However, once the does begin cycling into heat, the dominant bucks that handle the bulk of the breeding chores are often so busy accompanying, tracking and servicing does that they neither have the time nor the need to freshen their scrapes. But if you find a string of scrapes that have not been freshened for several days, it doesn’t necessarily mean that the rut is over or that the buck that made the scrapes is hanging from someone else’s game pole. It may mean that the buck is out doing his job.

The best way to determine what stage the rut is in is to spend a day or two in the woods prior to the hunt. If fresh scrapes abound, you will be hunting the pre-coital period, and a stand near a scrape line is going to be hard to beat. If, on the other hand, you find that more than half of the scrapes are old and abandoned, it’s a good bet that the big bucks are out taking care of the does, while the lesser bucks are still hanging around their scrapes, waiting.

If you just don’t have time to get into the woods prior to the season, you can get a good idea about the stage of the rut by speaking with area game managers, milk haulers, rural school bus drivers, postal carriers, loggers, trappers, farmers, grouse hunters, bowhunters or others who spend most of their time in deer country. If the bucks are chasing the does, chances are these people will have observed some of the activity.

Why all of the fuss over determining what stage the rut is in? Because it makes a big difference when you have to decide how you want to hunt after the opening weekend of the season.

When the season begins, if the actual breeding season has not yet begun but bucks are busy pawing out new scrapes and freshening old ones, chances are the bucks will curtail this activity during the high-pressure days at the beginning of the season, resuming their rounds once the hunters thin out. I like to have at least a half-dozen scrape lines pinpointed prior to the opener, and I check them after the opening weekend for any sign of activity. I then concentrate my hunting efforts near the “hottest” scrapes.

If your pre-season scouting and snooping indicates that the actual breeding season is in progress, then hunting over scrapes may not be your best option for tagging a really good buck. Remember, it is often the big, dominant bucks in the area that handle the bulk of the breeding chores. Even in an area with an excellent buck-to-doe ratio, a breeding buck can be kept so busy that he has little time or need to tend his scrapes. Often, a buck will spend 24 hours with the same doe, breeding her several times before her cycle ends. At this time, it is the doe, not the buck, that determines where the pair will travel. Though I would not totally rule out hunting in the vicinity of inactive scrapes at this stage of the rut, there are other options that I’ve found give me a better chance at tagging a big buck.

One of my favorite methods during this period is to concentrate my hunting efforts in areas where I have encountered good numbers of does in the past. Does and fawns tend to hang around together, and they routinely congregate in specific areas, often bedding as a loosely knit group in a favorite swale or sunny hillside. I can’t direct you to these “ladies-only bedrooms,” but I’ll bet there is such a place in the chunk of woods you hunt.

You can locate these places that hold special favor with the does by simply noting those areas where you encounter the most doe activity. After a few years of hunting the same ground, a familiar pattern becomes apparent.

On several occasions, I have located a hotbed of doe activity by simply listening to other hunters. When you hear a hunter complain that he sat on his stand and saw 15 does but not a single buck, find out where he was sitting. You can bet that when the time is right, there will be a buck or two sniffing around the vicinity.

A dominant buck ranges well out of his home area during the actual breeding season. I don’t think he goes out of his way looking for does so much as he is drawn out of the territory when following the tracks of a doe that has visited his scrapes or just wandered through the area. A buck engaged in this activity moves with a single-minded purpose, shuffling along, nose to the ground, seldom looking up, intent upon finding the maker of the tracks he is following. I once missed a buck that trailed a doe past my tree stand in this manner, my arrow slicing harmlessly over the buck’s back only to skitter noisily through the carpet of dry oak leaves a few yards beyond. The buck lifted his head and looked in the direction of the commotion. Apparently satisfied that whatever had made the racket was not what he was looking for, he then ambled on his way while I frantically fumbled around trying to nock another arrow.

When a buck finally tracks down a doe, as often as not, she will then lead the buck on a little chase that, in all likelihood, will take the buck still farther from his home base. By the time the buck actually mates with the doe, he may be miles from his original starting point. On the way back to his core area, it is not unlikely that the buck will cut the track of another “hot” doe and repeat the scenario.

All of this rambling makes it impossible to pattern a buck during the height of the breeding season, prompting many knowledgeable whitetail hunters to claim that taking a good buck during the peak of the rut is mostly a matter of luck. I disagree.

Hunting near a known “doe hangout” can help. And you can stack the deck in your favor even more by hunting a funnel area near a concentration of does. A funnel is simply any natural or man-made feature of the terrain that restricts the movement of deer. An hourglass-shaped section of woods is the classic funnel. Deer moving from one wide portion of the hourglass to the other must pass through the narrow waist. In farm country, the funnel may be a brushy fenceline connecting two woodlots. In the rugged country of the West, where whitetails are becoming increasingly common, a jutting jaw of sheer rimrock can force whitetails into a confined passage. Funnels work well because even though a buck with a snootful of “hot” doe scent wouldn’t care if he was standing in the middle of Madison Avenue, the does do not lose their heads. Does will stick to cover when on the move, even when there is a grunting, panting Romeo dogging their every move.

In many whitetail states, hunters are left with no choice but to hunt well after the main rutting period is over. At first, hunting after the opening weekend under these circumstances appears to be a futile endeavor. Without the promise of the rut to keep the bucks on the move, and with the absence of hunters to jar deer loose, what chance does the midweek hunter have of scoring on a decent buck? He has a pretty good one, I would say.

First, although the bulk of the breeding chores are completed in about a two-week period, there is still some activity after the main rut is over. Does that were not impregnated will cycle back into heat 28 days after their first estrus cycle ends. In some states, this is just about the time the rifle season rolls around. In addition, in some of the best whitetail areas, it is not uncommon for female fawns of the year to breed. This often applies to as many as 40 percent of them. Frequently, these young breeders experience their first estrus cycle later than older does. So, don’t strike out the possibility of the rut working in your favor even though the peak has passed. In that part of Minnesota where I live, the bulk of the breeding is completed by mid-November, yet each year, I find active scrapes late into November and even December. Last season, during an especially mild December, I found a pair of bushel-basket-size recently freshened scrapes while hunting grouse on New Year’s Eve day. If you can find these “late scrapes,” concentrate your hunting efforts near them.

But even if you fail to find any of these scrapes later in the year, you still stand a good chance of tagging a buck during the middle of the week if you let the after-effects of the rut work in your favor. During the rut, a buck takes little time out to eat. By the end of the breeding season, a buck that began the rut in peak condition can be drawn and thin, actually haggard looking. Some bucks lose up to 20 percent of their body weight during the course of the rut. Across the Northern states and Canada, where winter-kill is an annual problem, the big, breeding bucks follow the fawns in death each winter simply because they run off all of their reserve fat during the rut and are often unable to replenish it before the onset of winter snows.

Although the largest bucks are often nocturnal in their feeding habits at other times of the year, when the rut is over, dominant bucks cannot afford to limit their foraging to the hours of darkness alone. In order to regain their strength and put on the layer of fat necessary to see them through the winter, they must forage during the day. This gives the after-the-opener hunter the chance he needs to find bucks up and about during shooting hours.

This need to feed heavily does not necessarily mean that a big buck will be found sauntering around an alfalfa field at high noon, scarfing down the last frost-tinged greens of the year. It does mean that rather than tucking himself safely away in a thicket long before the morning sun ever peeks over the ridge, a buck will now tend to linger at the feeding grounds longer in the morning. Does and fawns often feed for an hour after sunrise, and now, bucks will begin to do the same. In the evening, when they normally would not begin feeding until complete darkness had fallen, bucks will now arrive at the feeding sites earlier — often in that last precious minute of shooting light.

Even at midday, the big boys will not be content to simply lay in their beds and chew their cuds. I have noticed that once the rut is over, bucks are much more likely to rise from their beds and spend an hour or two at midday — often between 11 a.m. and 2 p.m. — browsing near their bedding sites.

All of this daylight activity is a big plus for the after-the-opener hunter who must depend upon natural deer movements, rather than other hunters, to put a buck in front of him. The natural inclination is to hunt from a stand near the feeding area, but this is a mistake when you’re after mature bucks. A big buck is constantly alert, using each of his senses to detect the slightest hint of danger. Never is a buck more alert than when he is feeding during shooting light because he knows he is most vulnerable then. The bucks I have observed feeding during the season have been wound-up tighter than a watch spring and ready to snap at the slightest provocation. One miscue on your part, whether it is movement or scent or whatever, will send him running. I try to avoid known feeding areas, and I concentrate my efforts near the bedding areas.

Hunting near the bedding area means that any shot I take will most often be attempted in good light. That’s important for a clean kill. A buck may not arrive back at his bedding area until the sun is an hour high in the morning, and he may leave his bed to begin a dawdling trip to the feeding area in late afternoon. Instead of risking a poor shot at a shadowy form in the gray gloom of dusk or dawn, I take my shot when the light is in my favor.

Also, by concentrating my efforts in the vicinity of the buck’s daytime haunts, I have a better chance of catching him up and about during the midday hours. If I am forced to move into the bedding area and take a stand at mid-morning, I risk detection by the buck. But if I move into my stand in the dark and set up quietly, I double my chances of scoring. If I fail to take the buck as he returns from breakfast, I will be there waiting when he gets up for lunch or dinner.

But you don’t have to confine yourself to a stand. If conditions allow for quiet stillhunting, I like nothing better than to either prowl the edges of a buck’s daytime haunts or simply tread my way along a ridge littered with the acorns of the white oak, moving only a few yards at a time and stopping often to look, listen and feel. Often after the crowds of opening day are gone from the woods for another year, it is just me and the deer — one-on-one. And that’s the way I like it best.

This story, “After Opening Day,” appeared in the November 1987 issue of Outdoor Life. Read more from this series:

#8482. Here’s Proof That You Don’t Need to Break the Bank for a Hardside Deer Hunting Blind

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 10:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Growing up, I never really hunted out of a blind. Here in the Northeast, we traditionally hunt from treestands or just pick a good spot on the ground with natural cover. Sure, I watched plenty of hunting shows where they sat in a hardsided blind over a food plot, but that just wasn’t an option for me. That is, until recently, when I started planting my own food plots here in Pennsylvania.

Once I figured out the planting cycle, I knew it was time to get my hands on a hardsided blind. But the absurd prices of some of the higher-end models really turned my stomach. Then, I heard about the Maverick XL-V and knew it’d be a great fit for my new food plot.

This six-panel blind provides a near 360 degree view with large, vertical windows that are perfect for archery hunting. It’s also quite spacious, easily allowing two people to sit and hunt comfortably. But the best part is, it’s a fraction of the price that you’d pay for some of the competition out there. Here’s what you need to know about this versatile hunting blind.

After tending to my food plot for the better part of the summer, the clover was flourishing and my Maverick XL-V had arrived just in time to build it a month or so ahead of the season. This allowed the deer to get used to the blind’s presence prior to the season getting underway in October.

The first true test of the blind was setting it up. Maverick talked up its six-panel lock-in system, and I was excited to see how it all came together. Unfortunately, the first shipment I received was missing the door panel. After a quick call to customer support, the missing door panel arrived about a week later, but that panel was missing the window. Rather than wait any longer, I opted to simply replace the window with a piece of plexiglass cut to fit.

The good news is that the lock-in system works flawlessly. The panels all fit together quickly and seamlessly, making it easy to get the blind placed on our homemade platform with just two people. (For some of the bigger, spendier hard-sided blinds, you need a tractor or forklift.) Once it was set up, all that was left was to wait for our archery season to open.

On the opening weekend of archery, my girlfriend and I had an amazing first evening sitting in the XL-V blind. With several does and a few small bucks under 20 yards away, they never had any idea we were there. It was spacious enough for two blind chairs, our hunting packs, and our bows to fit with plenty of room to spare.

Since then, I’ve spent several weeks in and out of the blind, with deer in the plot almost every time. I have yet to see a target buck arrive in daylight, but I have a feeling the skunk will be knocked out of the blind in no time.

After spending several weeks in the XL-V, I can say I’ve grown quite fond of this blind. The windows offer a great view and operate on friction hinges, with three easy-to-use latches to keep them shut. Thus far, the windows open and close silently, which is obviously incredibly important to avoid spooking any deer in range.

The door offers two easy-to-use handles, too, and is large enough to fit through without having to contort my body to squeeze into the blind. Aside from the missing components with delivery, the set up was easy and straightforward. The panels with the lock-in system were intuitive and functioned as expected. I honestly expected to struggle getting it set up on our homemade platform, but was pleasantly surprised to be proven wrong.

Obviously, the issues I experienced with the missing door and then the missing window were not ideal. It’s hard to say if that’s a one-off and I simply got unlucky, or if there’s a larger issue at play there. Aside from that, there are a few other areas I think the XL-V could improve.

First, the near-360 degree view is great, but it leaves you backlit on sunny days. I’d love to see included covers for the windows behind you to avoid the backlighting issue. On top of that, this blind is not airtight, meaning you need to be aware of where your scent will be going just like hunting from a treestand.

While the XL-V might have some room for improvements, it’s worth keeping in mind how affordable this hardsided blind is compared to others on the market. It might lack some of the high-end features those blinds offer, but it still gets the job done for a fraction of the price.

Once getting it set up and deployed in the plot, I’ve thoroughly enjoyed every sit. It’s provided me with some incredible hunts, plenty of room to stretch my legs throughout some long sits, and ensure I have all of the gear I need. If you’re in the market for a budget hunting blind, don’t overlook the Maverick XL-V hunting blind.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

#8481. Details, Video of Jugal II First Ascent

The Spanish team is back in Kathmandu after their first ascent of Jugal II via a highly difficult 2,000m route. Yet, the climbers insist that the best part of the experience was climbing part of the route with Italians Nives Meroi and Romano Benet. Jugal II was Plan B for the Italian pair after flooding washed out access to their primary target, Tilicho Peak in the Annapurna region.

The peak, northeast of Kathmandu on the Nepal-Tibet border, has been a coveted goal since it opened to climbing teams in 2023. In the end, it was the skill and smart strategy of Mikel Zabalza, Ruben Perez, and Mikel Saez de Urabain that put them on the unclimbed 6,518m summit after an alpine-style push via the east-southeast face.

Route topo, including a special mention of Nives Meroi and Romano Benet.

The team said that the greatest technical difficulties came high on the route. “From the upper bivouac, the climb goes on pretty vertical mixed terrain, with sections of poor-quality rock,” Zabalza explained. “It’s sustained M5 with some even harder sections, until 6,450m. Then the rest of the way to the summit follows along a 55˚-60˚snow slope.”

A tough, vertical mixed section. Photo: Mikel Zabalza

They reached the summit on October 29, at 4:00 pm. The weather was clear but very cold, with some evening humidity.

“We made three bivouacs on the route, two on the way up and one more on descent, and all of them were rather precarious,” Mikel Zabalza told ExplorersWeb from Kathmandu. “Luckily, we had a tarp to enlarge them.”

One of the climbers on the summit of Jugal II. Photo: Mikel Zabalza

The Spanish team, all highly experienced alpinists, had been studying the route for months. This was key to their success.

The situation was different for Benet and Meroi of Italy. Since they had not planned to do Jugal II, their approach to the route was more improvisational. They had no previous information about what they would find. To make matters worse, the third member of the team, Tine Cuder of Slovenia, had to abandon the expedition due to health problems.

One of the Spanish team’s bivouacs, with a tiny ledge enlarged by a fabric tarp. Photo: Romano Benet

There was no competition between the two teams, who happily joined forces for the summit push. However, the Spaniards’ strategy proved essential on the highly steep ramps covered with unstable snow.

Nivel Meroi loaded with bivy gear on highly steep mixed ramps on Jugal II. Photo: Romano Benet

“We set off on an adventure, having never seen the mountain before and not knowing what kind of ascent awaited us, but we gave it a try,” Romano Benet reported from Kathmandu. “We decided to turn back to avoid a third bivouac. ”

The two teams joined forces and were in good spirits on the route. Photo: Romano Benet

Benet explained that they lacked the necessary gear (specifically, a tarp like Zabalza’s group) to pitch bivvies “on this type of face, where it’s impossible to carve out a ledge.”

Otherwise, the two groups returned as friends. “Nives and Romano are exceptional people,” Zabalza said.

“We are back from the mountain with new experiences and ideas but, most of all, with three new fantastic friends with whom, hopefully, we will share more adventures in the future,” Romano Benet concluded.

The Spanish team sent us the video below summarizing the climb.

#8479. Huge Crowds Trek to Everest Base Camp

The image of an endless line of trekkers heading to Everest Base Camp this week seemed like deja vu. Why are conga lines similar to the infamous ones filmed every May on Everest’s Hillary Step, now repeating at the base of the mountain? We asked the local trekking guide who shot the video.

Ngima Sherpa of Osprey Adventure explained that this is not the norm, but the delay caused by Cyclone Anwar, which hit Nepal last weekend, has created a huge backlog.

“Even though there are always a lot of trekkers heading to Everest Base Camp, this kind of traffic is not usual,” Ngima Sherpa explained. “This video was shot on November 1, after three days of continuous snow and rain…During that time, all the trekkers were stuck in Dingboche, which is two trekking stages away from Everest Base Camp.”

When the weather finally cleared, all those trekkers set off for EBC at the same time, causing heavy traffic on the route from Dingboche to Gorakshep.

The recent cyclone is also the reason why the surroundings are so heavily covered in snow. Usually, the entire trail to Everest Base Camp is dry.

The fresh snow on Nepal’s peaks is having consequences for those planning to summit trekking peaks. Conditions on otherwise straightforward snow slopes have become problematic, as climbers sink into deep, heavy snow.

Satori Adventures had to retreat after swimming in snow on the flanks of Mera Peak, in the Khumbu. “Four to five feet of fresh snow made it impossible to move forward above high camp,” leader Furba Sherpa reported.

Ramesh Gurung noted today that he has retreated from an attempt on 6,119m Lobuche East, another popular Khumbu 6000’er. However, Gurung said he will try again with another group soon.

Conditions will change only as the snow transforms under the dimming sun and freezes in the approaching winter temperatures.

On Ama Dablam, Sherpa climbers struggled to reopen the route to Camp 1 on Tuesday but managed to complete it. The route above Camp 1 follows a steep, mixed ridge with less accumulated snow. Several teams are now on their summit rotations and expect to top out in the next few days.

Tenzing Sherpa of Gokyo Namaste Lodge has shared some useful information for trekkers who plan to cross the huge Ngozumpa Glacier between Gokyo and Dragnag. The recent snowfall makes the usual route less safe, and he recommends following local people along an easier route between Longbanga Tso (1st Lake) and Taujung Tso (2nd Lake). See Tenzing’s sketch of the route:

Alternative route between Gokyo Lakes, suggested by Gokyo Tenzing Sherpa.

Tenzing also noted that the route from Thame to Gokyo is open, as the Gokyo Lodge Association cleared and reopened the trail from the Gokyo side yesterday. However, conditions on the glacier may change quickly.

The route across Gokyo is one of the most popular side trips in the Khumbu, for those wanting to enrich their Everest Base Camp trek. The area features stunning scenery, with high mountain lakes, glaciers, and a view of the formidable South Face of Cho Oyu.
