#8477. The Hunt for the Jameson Buck, a 250-Inch Kansas Legend That Lived in an Old Strip Mine

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 1:41 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

This story, “Ghost Buck,” appeared in the November 1991 issue of Outdoor Life. The Bruce Jameson buck ultimately scored 248 1/8 inches after deductions, according to Boone and Crockett. Although it was one of the top-scoring archery bucks of all time when it was killed, it’s listed as the 16th largest nontypical ever taken in Kansas. Though the story mentions Pope and Young, Jameson’s name isn’t listed among its records. The rack is now owned by Bass Pro Shops.

The big whitetail moved steadily, head lowered, nosing a course through a lunarlike landscape of earth mounds, dark puddles and hair-thick brush. Strip mining had turned the area raw and made hunting supremely difficult. Yet this giant of a buck moved on. Something compelled him, an urge perhaps. It was mid-November, the peak of the rut.

Then abruptly the buck froze, as if gripped by an old instinct for caution. From atop a rise, he surveyed the area.

To bowhunter Bruce Jameson, who knelt against the base of a cottonwood 50 yards away, it was now clear that all of the speculation about the buck had been true. Somehow the hundreds of hunters before him who had failed to get a close look at this ghost buck had emerged with an accurate picture — perhaps because it was nearly impossible to exaggerate in describing the animal. Everything about the buck was massive. His long, heavy body seemed to melt into a neck that was a barrel of solid muscle from supporting his towering, forearm-thick antlers. The bowhunter clutched his compound Pearson Spoiler and saw his knuckles turn a splotchy white.

This buck, so well-known but seldom seen, had drawn Jameson into the woods 20 of the past 21 mornings. He had been taunted by a combination of too much distance and brush. Day after day, there had been no clear shot. Still, the bowhunter felt a kind of jubila­tion as he watched the world-class whitetail lower his head and fade into the brush. After all, he had just accomplished something no other hunter had — he had seen the ghost buck while hunting.

At the time, the walls of Jameson’s Pittsburg, Kan­sas, bowhunting and taxidermy shop were lined with big-game mounts. Most were whitetail bucks of envi­able size. Still, to a mount, they were overshadowed by one buck in particular, which, once noticed, instantly took your eyes away from the rest. Hunters had come from all parts of southeastern Kansas to marvel at this huge non-typical buck. His main beams were nearly as thick as a softball where they rose from the mount, and they thickened as they climbed into a crown of spike­ like points. When displayed at a trophy deer show, people had crowded around, labeling it the most im­pressive non-typical to come from a state that’s been dominating the record books for a quarter-century.

Equally provocative was the fact that the deer was still in the wild. The display cape was that of another buck. The gargantuan antlers had been found after the ghost buck had shed them the previous winter. It wasn’t the first time, either.

Deer hunters around town got their first hint of an exceptional horned buck when John Jameson, Bruce’s father, happened upon a shed antler during a 1987 bowhunt. Though bleached from the sun and pocked by rodents, there was no mistaking that it came from a trophy buck. The single antler was unusually massive and sported eight points.

Local hunters were surprised that no one had ever seen the buck that carried it. Many speculated that the beast had already died a natural death. The following spring though, Jameson’s nephew, Doug Kratz, discovered that the ghost was still in their presence when he happened across a massive, freshly shed antler. A few weeks later the other side, an eye­ opening set, also turned up. Together the two antlers carried a total of 22 points.

The presence of the big buck in the Pittsburg area was particularly exciting to the Jamesons. Bruce Jameson is one of 10 children who grew up hunting and fishing with their father. In fact, all of the Jamesons, including their father, still bow hunt together regularly. Yet, the Jameson’s home range, southeastern Kansas, is the only area of the state not known for producing exceptional whitetail racks. Hunting pressure is high and the habitat fair at best for producing big bucks.

Like scores of other hunters, Jameson planned on hunting the ghost buck hard during the 1988 season, but his new taxidermy and bowhunting shop was gaining popularity with local hunters. He couldn’t get away.

Jameson was determined, however, to keep his ears open while he manned his Main Street shop. The progressing season started out as a repeat of the past: no sightings, and no signs of a huge whitetail — no big tracks, massive rubs or huge scrapes. Still, the attraction of this buck, and his mystique, seemed to build with each passing week.

There probably isn’t a deer camp or small hunting town in America that doesn’t have a legendary buck — an animal so massive, so elu­sive, so remarked upon, that his image is forever associated with something beyond the ordinary. The ghost buck is an idea more than a form, and as such, it has a special invincibil­ity. Yet Bruce Jameson was determined to change that.

Luck helped. Soon, Jameson’s good friend Dennis Scales accomplished something that instantly made him a local celebrity — he glimpsed the ghost buck. It was still early in the morning when Stales spotted a doe and a small buck feeding across a field of milo stubble.

From his truck, Scales could easily watch as the unimpressive pair fed across the expanse of knee-high cover. Then it happened. Scales, an avid deer hunter, watched in wonder as the big non-typical rose from the scant cover and hurried toward the little buck and doe. As soon as the monster buck reached the doe he in­stantly flattened out, totally out of sight in what looked like open cover. It was a ritual that Scales witnessed three times before the passing of a distant pickup truck spooked the big whitetail into the woods. No one else saw the famous buck during the gun or bow season.

Those who’ve driven the southeastern Kansas area can understand the amazement sur­rounding the notion of a giant buck in flatlands country. With a decided absence of wilder­ ness, every mile of land is evenly gridded by gravel roads that are often traveled by small­ game hunters and fishermen, farmers and com­muters.

Despite Scales’ sighting, and the discovery of another huge shed antler, Jameson was not encouraged. “My heart would start pounding when I would look up and think of myself in a tree with him coming down the trail,” he mused. “But I knew it was going to be tough to pick one buck, a buck that you know is at least 5 1/2 years old, in an area with heavy pressure and heavy cover. I also knew that I’d hunted this area all of my life and never seen the buck. I couldn’t help but occasionally wonder if he was still alive.”

One summer evening, Jameson found out. “I do quite a bit of looking in August,” he said. “The bucks will be hanging together, and because they haven’t been pressured you can sometimes catch them coming out to feed in the evenings.”

Along with a cousin, Bruce drove to a soybean field in his hunting area. Most sum­mertime bucks are calm and tolerant of distant vehicles, but one buck in particular melted into the nearby woods when Jameson was still 150 yards away. That was close enough.

“I didn’t need binoculars to know it was him,” Jameson recalled. “I was in a state of awe. I just turned to my cousin and said, ‘You have no idea what you just saw — he’s one of the best in the state, probably one of the best in the nation.’ It was good to know that I’d at least get a chance to hunt him.”

Jameson also knew that he wouldn’t be alone in his quest. The lad who’d found the most recent sheds had disclosed the buck’s location. Going into the season about a dozen bowhunters had sworn to hold out for this buck, which seemed now more flesh than ghost. In the weeks before the opening of deer season, a continuous cloud of dust hung over the dirt roads as hunters and non-hunters alike cruised along, hoping for a glimpse of the legendary buck. Then on October 1, bowhunters swarmed into the woods, but again it was as if the buck had turned to mist.

Meanwhile, Jameson bided his time, hop­ing the upcoming rut would draw the buck out. Though Jameson had a good idea where the deer lived, he couldn’t get permission to enter the property. Instead, he set up 1 1/2 miles away and waited.

“I figured that once the rut started he’d start covering more ground,” Jameson rea­soned. “Even then I knew he’d be tough be­ cause of the terrain.”

Deer hunting in this part of Kansas can be, well, the pits. This is coal country. In previous decades huge chunks of ground were clawed open to get at coal deposits. The strip-mined areas look like moon surface.

Piles of dirt from eight to 30 feet high lay interspaced with water-filled pits. Where there is dirt there is dense brush. The jungle — and swamplike strip pits offer some of the most difficult hunting in the nation.

Jameson spent his first week in a tree at the edge of “the pits” near a slough that would offer the buck the easiest and most direct route through the area. He saw only a small buck and some fawns.

Frustrated, he moved to an area that held a small green wheat field and a scattering of hedge trees. Though he sighted and passed up a number of lesser bucks, there was no sign of the ghost buck. The unseasonably warm and dry weather that ushered in the November rut­ — hunters in the area saw more mosquitoes than snowflakes — further tilted the odds against Jameson.

Finally, Jameson got a much needed break in the form of a light shower, which would allow him to pick up tracks. He started slowly traveling the back roads.

“We often look for what we call ‘shortcut’ trails in the dust on the shoulders of roads,” Jameson explained. “They’re basically trails that big bucks use to cover a lot of ground and to get from section to section without being seen.”

The veteran hunter observed that these shortcut trails seem to be utilized only by big bucks, seldom by does or lesser bucks. “You’ll usually only find the tracks of one or two bucks,” Jameson added. “We check the road edges because the trails are tough to see or follow in the woods, they get used so little.”

A few years earlier, in just such a place, one of his brothers had arrowed a trophy with a Boone and Crockett Club non-typical score in excess of 200 points.

Jameson soon found tracks coming and going from the section he’d hunted earlier in the season. There were two sets of buck tracks: one big, the other less so. Experience told the hunter that the larger set probably belonged to a dark-racked buck he’d seen and passed up near the wheat field.

But what about the other tracks? They weren’t overly impressive, and he couldn’t remember seeing them before. But it was their location, not their size, that excited Jameson. It was a prime spot for an old, wary buck to travel. “Maybe,” he thought, “just maybe …”

Fired by a hunch, Jameson took a rare morning off from his treestand vigil and planned to move from his wheat field location to the slough at the edge of the pits where he’d first started.

Jameson had just started the walk into the slough when something caught his eye. “I looked across the field and there he was,” said Jameson. “I could tell it was him no problem. He had his nose down bird-dogging it back and forth. He was looking for a doe.”

Jameson’s hunch was confirmed; the buck had gone right through the slough where he’d wanted his stand. But rather than bemoan his bad timing, Jameson decided to make the best of the situation as he watched the non-typical disappear into the timber.

“A lot of hunters make the mistake of seeing a big buck and then not doing anything about it,” noted Jameson. “We’ve taken sev­eral trophy-class bucks by swinging around and getting ahead of them. Sometimes it works, sometimes it doesn’t, but I figured it was worth a try.”

The buck had been heading north, possibly toward the shortcut trail Jameson had found a few days earlier. The bowhunter wasted no time circling ahead of the buck. He ran down the trail that bisected the two sections, jumped a fence, and sprinted to the top of one of the many dirt mounds.

It was a guess — hoping the buck would cross 30 yards to his right — but it was Jameson’s only chance. Even if the buck came his way he knew that the thick underbrush would make a shot next to impossible.

Jameson had been in place only five min­utes when he caught movement. It was the ghost buck, passing at a steady clip. The range was too long and the brush too thick. Jameson’s heart was drumming against his rib cage. He tried to sneak a few steps closer. but he couldn’t get within 50 yards.

Then, for reasons unclear to the hunter. the whitetail suddenly stopped and raised his head. In the next moment, he was gone. disappearing over the edge of a mound. Jameson found himself staring at the scene as it was just minutes ago — that is, without the ghost buck­ — and he felt a kind of pleasurable sadness. “It was a strange feeling.” he remembered. “I’d actually figured out where he’d go, but I was also disappointed. You simply don’t get many chances at a deer like him. He sure had proven that over the past several seasons. I had to wonder whether I’d ever get another chance.”

Feeling almost electrified, Jameson waited a few minutes before sneaking back carefully to the spot where he”d last seen the whitetail. At least, he reasoned, he could get a look at the tracks and perhaps figure out once again where the buck was heading.

When he saw them, he instantly recognized the tracks — they were the smaller set he’d seen on the shortcut trail. Jameson walked in the direction the buck had taken and paused at the top of the next mound, wondering why he hadn’t seen the deer cresting one of the sea of little ridges.

Without warning, there was a loud crash. Jameson twisted around and saw the buck of his dreams jump from a hiding place 35 yards away, staying to a course that kept distance between him and the bowhunter. Instinct took over. It seemed Jameson’s hundreds of hours of indoor and outdoor shooting had somehow led him to this shot — he was ready. “I drew, got ahead of the buck, and when the time was right, I released the arrow without actually thinking about it.”

Jameson watched his Anderson 365 mag­num broadhead enter the buck as the animal stepped through a tiny clearing. Ironically. it was one of the rare occasions when this sharp­ shooting bowhunter had been unsure of his arrow placement. He watched the buck pause briefly atop the next mound, then move out of sight as if not seriously wounded.

At the thought of a bad hit on such a magnificent animal, Jameson fell to his knees and was sick. Long a man of deep conviction, he did what came naturally in a time of crisis — he said a quick prayer for the animal’s fast and humane death. “I call upon God anytime I feel I need help.” he said. Then he thought to go get his father, who was waiting nearby.

An hour later Jameson, his father, John, and his brother Richard were back in the area. The trio quietly surveyed the scene of the shot. There was blood. They soon found the arrow. but it was floating in a nearby pit of water, wave-cleansed of any tissue or hair.

After deciding the trail looked good, the trio inched in the direction the ghost buck had taken. They saw that the buck had stopped and changed directions. The father and sons found the huge whitetail at the top of the next dump­ — barely 70 yards away from where it had been hit. Clearly, the ghost buck had succumbed within a matter of a few seconds.

“When he saw the deer,” Jameson re­called of his father, “tears came to his eyes and he hugged me around the neck. He’s always had a great sense of pride in each of his six sons and our accomplishments. I’ll never forget it.”

What will forever be known as the “Jameson buck” will go down as one of the most impres­sive whitetail bucks of all time. The mass of the rack was almost indescribable and, along with the skull plate, weighed an eye-opening 12 pounds.

After the 60-day drying period, the mam­moth rack scored 253 6/8 Pope and Young Club points — the tally may well go higher when scored by the club’s official panel. As it stands, the “Jameson buck” is the third largest non­ typical whitetail arrowed in history.

Read Next: I Shot the Third Biggest Elk of All Time

A close look at the ghost buck revealed how he had lived virtually undetected by sportsmen for years. Though he field-dressed at 243 pounds, his feet sported surprisingly small hoofs. Jameson also theorized that the jungle of points on the buck’s rack may have kept him from rubbing on big trees.  “When you really start looking at the rack you realize that he couldn’t rub all of his velvet on a big tree,” said the hunter. “He probably had to rely on small trees or maybe even brush. It’s possible we saw his rubs and just dismissed them as those of a small buck.”

#8476. Animal Attack Roundup, Nov. 4

While they predominate the media reports, most animal attack victims are not tourists unwisely attempting to take selfies with wildlife. People who live and work every day among animals are the ones who most often face the consequences.

As expanding populations push human settlements further into wildlife habitat, we can expect to see more conflicts. Here are some recent incidents.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Tisha Mukherjee, CC BY-SA 4.0

In Indonesia, 24-year-old Selviana Ambu Kaka was attacked by a crocodile while washing clothes in the Bondo Kodi River. Her fiancé and his sister witnessed a large crocodile emerging from the water, biting Ambu Kaka on the leg, and dragging her into the river. They immediately alerted nearby relatives and authorities, and a search commenced.

The people of Southwest Sumba, the closest town, organized and combed the area with nets. When they found a large crocodile matching witness descriptions, they hauled it onto shore and killed it. The animal measured nearly 4.9 meters long.

Early on the morning of October 24, Ambu Kaka’s remains were found approximately 30m from the attack site. She is one of many such victims in Indonesia, which sees more saltwater crocodile attacks than any other country.

Habitat destruction is largely behind the massive number of human-crocodile conflicts. It’s an understandably hard sell, however, for conservation workers to convince locals that preventing attacks means protecting the perpetrators.

The victim had recently returned from Jakarta to become formally engaged on October 30.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/ Rohit Gangwal, CC BY-SA 4.0

A 65-year-old farm laborer, Tulsi Rama Sawar, was killed by a leopard in state of Maharashtra, just north of Mumbai, on November 1. The noise of the attack summoned help from the locals. By the time they arrived, the leopard had fled, but Sawar was deceased. Her case comes less than a month after a leopard killed a 52-year-old woman in the same area.

Town residents are demanding immediate government action to capture the animal. If their demands for cage traps and cameras are not granted, locals threaten collective protest. The attacks represent a threat to their lives, but also their livelihoods, as farmers are unable to work the fields out of fear of attack.

Elsewhere, in Uttar Pradesh near the Nepal border, a 45-year-old farmer named Kennu fought off an attacking leopard, sustaining serious injuries. The man was working in a sugarcane field when the predator leaped out from hiding. Kennu fought back, and the animal retreated long enough for him to flee home, where family members brought him to medical care. Forestry officials have urged agricultural workers to avoid working in the fields alone.

Farm workers are not the only leopard victims, however. Early in the morning of November 4, a 15-year-old girl was dragged from her home in the Shahdol district of central India.

Saraswati Singh Gond was sleeping in bed beside her sister on their veranda, which did not have a door. A leopard entered during the night, not even waking her sister. Singh Gond’s body was found approximately 300m from the house. The search for the leopard continues.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Shadow Ayush, CC BY-SA 4.0

Leopards are only one of the wildlife threats that rural Indians face. A 55-year-old woman was gathering mushrooms in Utterakand in northern India on October 30 when she was attacked and killed by a wild elephant. Devaki Rana was lifted up and thrown to the ground, her son reported.

The incident occurred in one of India’s elephant corridors, which allow highly endangered Asian elephant herds to move between their fragmented habitats. The corridors are essential to conservation, but also present a concentrated area where attacks are more common.

Another fatal encounter took place in the state of Chhattisgarh in east-central India. A man of between 35 and 40 years of age, traveling by motorcycle with his nephew, came upon an elephant standing in the road. It immediately charged them, killing the uncle, while his nephew managed to hide beneath a parked truck. Eventually, the elephant left, and he was able to escape.

Locals believe that the elephant in question is one known as Kabra Kaan, which they see frequently and which has been connected with earlier incidents. The Forestry Department, however, has not confirmed this.

Elephant corridors allow them to pass between the remaining forested areas. Photo: Wildlife Trust of India

In Japan, brown bear attacks have been rising for several years. As in many places, human settlements are encroaching on brown bear territory. A decrease in farmland has left no boundary between the urban and the forested worlds. The government has responded with controversial bear culls, despite the concerns of conservation workers.

The latest incident is very different from the increasingly common attack stories. Late on the night of October 30, an express train running on Hokkaido’s Soya Line collided with a large brown bear. The body of the bear was trapped under the train, and the wheels derailed.

A brown bear became trapped beneath the wheels of Sarobetsu No 3. Photo: Hokkaido Railway Company

None of the crew or 32 passengers were injured in the crash. However, fearing more bears were in the area, officials kept everyone on the train overnight, until they could be safely extracted the following morning. Company officials have confirmed that this is the first bear-collision derailment in their nearly 40-year history.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8470. Young Climbers Bag New Routes, Including First Ascent, in Rolwaling

An energetic foursome of young French climbers had a busy time recently in Nepal’s Rolwaling Valley, east of the Khumbu. Eric Bazon, Pierre Girot, Sophie Jacob, and Kilian Moni did two new routes, including a first ascent of a 5,205m rocky spire and one very difficult line. They then climbed another peak on a “rest” day.

As soon as the climbers reached Rolwaling, they had to change their primary goal, which was in poor condition. Their acclimatization was also progressing more slowly than expected. Instead, they focused their attention on a ridge they saw from their base camp every morning. On October 20, Bazon, Jacob, and Girot set off toward the summit, while Moni waited for them at the base.

“Before long, the climbing turned vertical, and the ridge became razor-sharp and exposed,” Bazon wrote. The climb took three days on the ridge, bivvies included, and led Bazon, Sophie Jacob, and Pierre Girot to the west summit of ChekiGo (6,128m). It took another full day and one more bivouac to reach base camp.

Cheki Go’s main summit was first climbed in 2005 by Bruce Normand of the UK and Oliver Von Rotz of Switzerland via the west face. A Spanish team led by Mikel Zabalza first reached the west summit via a direct route up the west face in 2017. The team had just returned from bagging the first ascent of Jugal II.

“It was quite an intense experience for a ‘secondary’ project: one that combined rock climbing on mediocre granite, improvised protection, and steep snow ridges, all while hauling our ropes on our backs,” said Bazon.

The ridge was technical enough to be a good start: 5c on rock and M4 on mixed terrain. In Alpine grades, the team assessed it as TD (very difficult).

Since the weather forecast looked good for another four days, the team decided to continue climbing. They split into two teams: Bazon and Girot headed for a peak previously climbed by elite American alpinists Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey in 2014: Chukyima Go (6,259m). Like their predecessors, the French team approached the west face from the Naa Valley in Rolwaling.

“There was this direct, narrow, and obvious couloir that simply begged to be climbed,” Bazon reported.

The climbers at an open bivy. Photo: Eric Bazon

The next morning, they found the west face in great condition, which allowed them to climb a direct route to the summit along rock cracks, steep ice, and varied mixed terrain.

It took them two days (October 27-28), with one cold bivy on the face with their feet in their backpacks, to complete the route’s 23 pitches. Their West Face Direct route is 900m long, and the team assessed it as ED (extremely difficult), with M6 on mixed sections, and an Alpine ice grade of AI5.

The photo below shows that the new French route, TchikitaMa Go, runs to the right of the previous line opened by the two Americans.

Left: the new route opened by the French team, compared, right, to the 2014 line by Domen Kastelic and Sam Hennessey. Photo: Eric Bazon/PlanetMountain

Meanwhile, Sophie Jacob and Kilian Moni went for an unclimbed peak that also rose right in front of their base camp: 5,205m Janjmu. The pair completed the first ascent on an aesthetic 600m line up the north face. They called the route The Eternal Stars (“Les etoiles eternelles”) after some friends who had recently perished.

“It felt just like being at home in the Alps, with the added challenge of high altitude,” the climbers reported. “We pushed ourselves to the limit, both physically and mentally, across demanding yet incredibly logical pitches.”

But this was still not enough for the pair. They squeezed in a final climb of Yalung Ri in what they called “a contemplative, active recovery” day. They then finished the trip by paragliding from the summit.

Kilian Moni flies from the top of Yalung Ri. Photo: Kilian Moni

#8468. ‘I Tried Not Looking at the Rack.’ Indiana Hunter Tags 199-Inch Buck on State Park Land

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 21, 2026 6:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Avery Fisher of Delphi, Indiana, and his best friend since high school, Nathan Van Dokkenburg, put in for an Indiana parks draw hunt on the recommendation of a buddy. Both drew the two-day Nov. 16-17 firearms season.

Indiana has special limited-draw, short-term deer hunts on about 25 public parks around the state. The hunts help manage the burgeoning deer herds, and most allow sportsmen to take three deer per hunt, with a limit of one buck. The number of hunters drawn for each park varies, but about 150 hunters are typically allowed on a park for each two-day hunt.

Neither Fisher nor Van Dokkenburg had ever walked the park they drew to hunt, which they declined to name. But they spent a lot of time figuring out where they should be on the property well before the hunt.

“We studied a lot of Google Maps and onX to get an idea where we should hunt far back in the park well away from other hunters,” Fisher tells Outdoor Life. “It’s rolling hills with big creek drains and draws … I’ve hunted that kind of land previously, and I had a good idea where to go early that Nov. 16 opening morning.”

The hunters checked in when the park opened at 5 a.m. and were prepared for an all day sit with packs, gear, and food.

“We got far back into the park, about one and a half miles from the road,” says Fisher, 25, who works as a financial advisor. “We stayed on ridgetops, and about one mile in, Nathan and I split up.

Fisher went about another half mile, hiking by the light of his headlamp.

“In the dark I spotted a tree line that had a lot of buck rubs. Then I saw a doe, and I figured it was a pretty good place.”

He climbed about 30 feet off the ground and settled into his saddle with his coffee and snacks his favorite .44-cal Ruger, a gift from his father years ago.

It was cold early that morning, and he heard something in a nearby ravine around 7:45 a.m.

“I thought I saw a buck and put my binoculars on it, and it was one with a good spread and nice tines about 100 yards away.”

He raised his rifle and rested it against the tree for support while looking for the buck in his scope.

“But my breath kept fogging the scope,” he says. “I kept cleaning it with my gloves, and the deer kept getting closer … I tried not looking at his rack as he turned uphill and then I saw his sticker points as he was rubbing trees.”

Fisher found the shoulder in his scope and squeezed the trigger. The buck ran uphill past Fisher, wobbling as it went. When the buck stopped at 60 yards, Fisher shot again. The buck dropped.

Fisher texted Van Dokkenburg and told him he’d shot a nice buck, but to keep hunting because they were allowed to shoot up to three deer apiece.

“A group of does came in at about 9 a.m., and I shot one and it fell,” Fisher says. “I decided not to shoot any more deer that morning.

“We had to bone and pack out two deer like we were on a Western hunt. I figured that was enough for us to lug out of there that day.”

Van Dokkenburg saw nothing to shoot and met up with Fisher about 11 a.m., filming Fisher as he first walked up to his buck and realized how huge it was.

“I was speechless at the size of his rack,” says Fisher. “I knew he was big, but not a rack like that. I’m grateful and thankful to the Lord.”

Using a wheeled cart and backpacks, the two hunters hauled both deer back down to their vehicle.

“I think my pack with deer and gear weighed over 100 pounds, and I had to stop and offload more from my pack to the wheeled cart,” Fisher says.

They then drove to check in their deer with park personnel.

Fisher’s 13-point buck has been measured with an unofficial green score of 199 5/8 inches. It turned out Fisher’s second shot had been unnecessary, since his first had struck the heart.

Read Next: ‘Complete Shock.’ Long-Haul Trucker Hunter Tags 227-Inch Nontypical During State Park Hunt

The two buddies returned to the park to hunt the next day, though Fisher says they were exhausted from the previous day’s hunt.

“We saw some deer, including another far-off buck, but we didn’t shoot another deer that second day,” he says. “Nathan is drawn for another two-day park hunt later this year, so he still has a good chance of taking deer, maybe even a huge buck.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8466. Duo Completes Baffin Island Circuit By Skiing, Hiking, and Packrafting

Caleb McDaniels, 25, and Henry Penfold, 22, have completed their impressive expedition around Baffin Island. The Americans combined skiing, hiking, and packrafting to almost circle the world’s fifth-largest island. McDaniels told ExplorersWeb they did about 20% skiing and the rest was evenly split between backpacking and packrafting.

The pair began and ended in Iqaluit, Nunavut’s capital. The final section of their trip was also the longest, a 1,130km stretch with no villages after Igloolik to resupply. They made light work of it, knocking it out in 40 days and bringing their total distance to 3,262km over 145 days, from their late May start to their return to Iqaluit in mid-October. By then, the oncoming Arctic winter gave them few hours of light to work with.

The pair’s completed route around Baffin Island. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

With no resupply points, the pair left Igloolik with 82kg of food. They hoped to do it in seven weeks but brought even more food, because they weren’t sure how long that giant giant section would actually take, explained Caleb McDaniels.

“With no way to carry that much weight in our packs, we loaded up our packrafts and paddled the first 400 miles [644km]. Then, for the final 300 miles [483km], we heaved on our packs to walk overland,” they explained.

The paddling portion went quickly, aided by some ingenuity. “Much of the time, we were able to sail using our trekking poles as masts and panels cut out of an old tent for sails. Speeding along at over five mph, we did some incredible downwinding, spray flying from our bows.”

Using makeshift sails. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They also effectively halved the 480km trek by packrafting over two large lakes, including Nettilling, the world’s largest lake on an island. Yet there was no way to ease the final 160km slog into Iqaluit.

Postholing through early winter snow back to Iqaluit. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

“It was more of a postholing session — we slowly picked our way across snow-covered boulder fields and partly frozen marshlands. Constantly breaking through thin ice over the tundra marshes, we walked in our drysuits to keep our legs and feet dry. Each morning required half an hour of chipping ice and melting out our shoes, which invariably froze solid,” they said.

McDaniels and Penfold had planned a roughly circular route around the island, but “we weren’t trying to circumnavigate Baffin,” McDaniels told ExplorersWeb. Two previous parties had circumnavigated the island by dogteam, but McDaniels and Penfold’s route was driven by aesthetic considerations rather than to satisfy a catchy trip definition.

Slogging overland. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They chose their route after looking at satellite images and enjoyed both spectacular scenery and some special wildlife sightings, including at least one that was a little too close for comfort.

“One morning, as I went to fill our water bottles, I stumbled upon a sleeping polar bear hidden behind a low rise. Only a few yards away, it lifted its massive white head and glared at me for a moment before returning to its nap in the sun,” McDaniels wrote. “I carefully retreated behind the rise and yelled over to Henry to get the shotgun and flares out just in case. We quickly packed our boats and got on the water.”

In Foxe Basin, they almost collided with a sleeping walrus, and on Nettilling Lake, they met one of the resident seals. “He was immensely curious and not at all afraid. He swam around our boats for half an hour, playing with our stern lines and swimming back and forth between us. At one point, I put my hand into the water, and he brushed it with his nose!”

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

#8462. Americans Bag Major First Ascent in Pakistan

Americans Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler, awarded with a Piolet d’Or in 2025 after the first ascent of Yashkuk Sar, have done a similar monster 1,800m-long new route on the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m). This time, Grant Stewart joined them for this off-season climb in the Karakoram.

This first ascent of the peak’s north face included difficulties up to M7, WI5. The climbers had to overcome several obstacles before even setting foot on the face. Like many other alpine-style teams this season, they were originally planning to climb another peak. But instead of adapting because of washed-out trails, they had bureaucratic issues. Their permit, obtained 10 months earlier, was revoked some weeks before their departure. They had to quickly find a different goal.

Meanwhile, the airline misplaced Winckler’s baggage — every adventurer’s nightmare. Somehow, the outfitter sent it to base camp some days later. Then, Steadman had to overcome serious Acute Mountain Sickness in base camp. Their acclimatization was hampered by freak spells of bad weather. Finally, the line on Sosbun Brakk they initially chose was too hard and risky because of a hanging serac they only spotted after they began.

The team’s lonely base camp. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Back at BC, it was time to rest, regroup, and contemplate a totally different line that we’d seen from on the wall, which gave us new hope,” Steadman recalled.

Off to the north face. Photo: Dane Steadman

On October 1, the three climbers set off on that potential new line. “Instead of angling left up hard mixed ground above the cone as before, we made an arcing traverse right, across an ice apron, to gain runnels on the right side of the face,” Steadman explained.

The team gained 800m on that first day and set their first bivouac on a snowy arete, just in time to be slammed by a violent storm that kept them in their tent the whole next day.

Steep mixed terrain. Photo: Dane Steadman

A tarp to protect the team’s bivouac from falling spindrift. Photo: Dane Steadman

“In the morning, we made a gamble for the top by leaving the tent and starting up a massive gully system,” Steward wrote. “At its top, we reached another dead end in a huge serac barrier. To escape this, Dane committed to a visionary pitch, casting off up a blank slab to eventually reach an ice smear.”

Chaotic, difficult rocky sections. Photo: Dane Steadman

“A huge serac to our right and smooth slabs out left posed a serious question; eight meters of sideways teetering on tiny gneissic edges and a miraculous rock thread were the answer,” said Steadman.

“We continued up the massive hanging glacier to reach the hallucinatory final headwall of steep ice/mixed climbing and mushroom-weaving lunacy,” Stewart continued. “One final traverse with 1,800m of exposure under our feet and a magically appearing, dead-vertical ice tongue led us to the summit ridge.”

A totally vertical section. Photo: Grant Stewart

“The wild terrain on the upper mountain took longer than we wanted,” Steadman admitted. By the time they finally reached the summit, it was 4 pm, the storm was coming the following day, and they still had a long way (and many rappels) down.

Summit picture on Sosbun Brakk. Left to right, Dane Steadman, Grant Stewart, and Cody Wrincker. Photo: Dane Steadman

“The sun would set around 6 pm, so we threaded the first of 35 rappels and started down. Long after dark, we finally arrived back at our bivy ledge at around 5,800m,” Steadman recalled.

The storm was not supposed to start until noon the following day, but it was already snowing at 8 am, with still 1,200 vertical meters of descent to go.

Rappelling down the route. Photo: Dane Steadman

“Before long, we were caught in a proper storm,” Stewart explained. “We committed to a faster and hopefully safer descent by rappelling down the west rib to the Sokha/Sosbun col. By the time we reached the col, we could barely communicate over the screaming southwest wind.”

After some scary rappels in high winds down the serac band, the climbers finally reached a sheltered section of the face. They still had to deal with heavy spindrift coming from above until finally, at around 4 pm, they touched down on the glacier. And yet, the adventure was not over — especially for Grant Stewart:

Following Dane, I took maybe 10 steps when I felt the ground under me crack. Instinctively, I jumped and landed on my stomach as a huge crevasse swallowed the ground behind me. Soaking wet, we began postholing into the wan light of a mesmerizing white out.

[We stuck] to the center of the glacier as avalanches rumbled down all around in the whirling snow. After a strange eternity, the ragged colors of a tent appeared and we were greeted by ecstatic dancing, big hugs, and endless cups of chai from (our BC crew) Ibrahim and Idrees.

Happy climbers and base camp crew after a safe return from the summit. Photo: Dane Steadman

The team didn’t report their success and the details of the climb until they returned home. And then, the first words of appraisal were not for the route itself, but for the mountains and people of Gilgit-Baltistan.

“It’s a unique place…where continents and cultures collide. Trips there, while often difficult, are rewarding in ways hard to describe simply,” Steadman said. This was his third trip to the Karakoram, a place he has fallen in love with.

#8461. 15 Scent Control Strategies for Deer Hunting That Actually Work

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 22, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

A heavy-antlered 12-point buck materializes on the trail. He’s only five steps from bow range, but then he throws his nose in the air, goes rigid with nervousness, and waves the white flag. Game over. That’s all, folks.

We all know that any successful deer hunt starts with not getting busted by a whitetail’s incredibly powerful sense of smell. And today there’s a whole collection of gear that’s marketed as being able to beat a deer’s nose. Many of the scent control gear and strategies are gimmicks, but some are not.

With careful scent control, you can give yourself a slight edge that just might mean the difference between getting busted and getting a shot. Here are scent-reduction efforts that work, with 15 steps that hunters can use to minimize scent before, during, and after the hunt.

Hunters must understand they will never completely neutralize the scent they emit. Furthermore, they will never completely beat a whitetail’s nose. However, a hunter can minimize their scent.

This won’t keep a deer from smelling you, but it might buy you a little extra time or a few more yards. It can also make all the difference in a non-target deer just moving along rather than alarm snorting.

You don’t have to use all of the following strategies just to shoot a deer. But, implementing at least some of them will certainly make you more effective in the stand.

Deer hunters should consider a long-term scent control mentality. Rather than just preparing the day of the hunt, start mitigating scent long before. During hunting season, consider cutting the smelly shampoos, soaps, colognes, deodorants, and aftershaves out of your routine. This will help keep your clothes smelling more natural, too.

Washing your hunting clothes is the next step. Treat with scent-reduction laundry detergent. Such laundry detergent doesn’t remove all scent, but it knocks out a lot of it and it doesn’t add unnatural scents. (Also, on a deer vision note, ensure that the detergent does not contain UV brighteners.)

Once your hunting clothes are clean, store them in a dedicated container. A portable plastic tote that has a place at home, but easily loads into a vehicle, is the best choice. This should help minimize odors that might contaminate the garment fibers. A lot of hunters like to put a pine branch in their tote to keep clothes smelling fresh and piney (and not like plastic).

In addition to hunting clothing, hunters should also clean their other gear. Backpacks and boots absorb the scents around them. So, store these and other hunting items in areas that aren’t stinky. Use a dedicated storage bin for this gear, too.

The day of the hunt, take a shower with shampoos and soaps that are designed to reduce human odor without adding artificial scents. This should help neutralize some (but not all) of the existing surface scent on your hair and skin.

Brush your teeth, but not with a super-smelly toothpaste. Instead, use something with a natural scent, or no scent at all. Then, eat an apple to help further mitigate scent that might originate from the mouth and throat. Avoid strong-smelling drinks and foods, such as coffee or spicy grub which lead to bad breath.

A lot of hunters use ozone in the field (like Ozonics), but it’s also applicable for treating hunting clothing. Some products are designed to work in conjunction with boxes to clean clothing and remove unwanted scent.

Used improperly, Ozone can destroy clothing. So Wildgame innovations recently introduced a ZeroTrace tote and device that is designed to treat gear without destroying it.

Read Next: Does Ozonics Actually Work? Here’s What 5 Independent, Diehard Deer Hunters Say After Field Testing

To minimize unwanted scent particles from making it onto your hunting clothes, consider dressing once in the field. This keeps away scent from handling pets, visiting gas stations, or other stops between home and the hunt. Getting dressed in the field during a cold or rainy day can be a hassle, but it’s worth it.

Rubber boots can contain some scent, but they only achieve this if kept uncontaminated from foreign scents. Don’t wear your deer hunting boots around town where they might pick up unwanted odors.

Hunters have many reasons for field wipes. But in terms of scent control, wiping off sweat with a field wipe will certainly help. Just make sure the version you use is scent-free.

Even better than wiping away sweat, is to not break a sweat at all. Sweat just adds an entire fresh new layer of scent. In the field, do everything possible to prevent or reduce sweating. Wait to put on your coat until you get to your stand.. Walk slowly. Remain in the shade. Take more time to get settled in the treestand.

Perhaps the most important aspect of scent control is managing the direction that your scent travels. This means choosing good entry and exit routes, and ensuring your wind doesn’t carry over into areas that deer frequent (especially bedding areas and core feeding areas).

Those who hope to minimize scent even further should deploy hunting blinds that are completely enclosed. Those which have completely sealed doors and windows can hold most, if not all, scent within the blind. Keeping windows closed until it’s  time to shoot is an excellent way to bottle up scent.

Some hunters might cleverly use wind blockers, which are land features that keep deer from getting downwind. Examples include hunting along the edge of a bluff, river, hinge-cut, or lake. Similarly, you can make a mock scrapes on either side of your scent cone, which might stop a buck just long enough before he gets downwind of you.

The rising and falling thermals can be helpful or a huge pain in the butt. When used wisely, and with the right stand locations, thermals will carry scent up and away from deer. For example, hunting up high on a ridge when morning thermals carry scent upward makes it very difficult for deer to smell hunters. Likewise, hunting down low in a bottom when afternoon thermals carry scent downward can achieve the same result.

Remember, no matter how far you take scent control strategies, deer will eventually smell you. It’s important to take all precautions, and implement each step outlined above. Ultimately, careful stand selection that puts the wind in your favor will be the most important step for not getting busted.

But don’t get discouraged when the inevitable happens. Just keep hunting. After all, you can’t really beat a deer’s nose, and it wouldn’t be fun if we won every time anyway.

#8458. Weekend Warm-Up: Assynt

Assynt, subtitled “Home is where the croft is,” is a short documentary that brings viewers to the Scottish Highland community of Assynt, where residents live on traditional crofts.

The croft is a uniquely Scottish term, describing the smaller, less fertile plots of land that poorer people were forced onto, following the infamous Highland Clearances of the 18th and 19th centuries. Crofters lived on their crofts and farmed for subsistence, grazing sheep on shared land. Unable to support themselves on the croft alone, most also worked in fishing, kelp, or peat to support themselves.

It was a very hard life. But Lewis MacAskill, whom the viewer is soon introduced to, doesn’t see it that way. He is one of just over a thousand residents of Assynt, who chose to stay and continue the crofting tradition.

“I regard the croft as an extension of who I am as a person,” MacAskill explains.

The small community of Assynt. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill introduces us to some of the other members of the community. Anna is a sheepdog owner and trainer, whom we see herding sheep using the same techniques people here have used for centuries. Stuart is a passionate fisherman, who shares his love of Assynt’s natural landscape with his young son.

We also meet some of the creatives who have, MacAskill says, been drawn by the unique landscape and material culture. We see a potter using local materials, a fiddler, and a spinner on her spinning wheel.

The spinning wheel came to Europe from the Middle East by the 13th century, and was widely adopted by the 18th century, replacing the older drop spindle and distaff. Photo: Screenshot

MacAskill moved to the croft a decade ago, and doesn’t regret it. Having grown up visiting every summer, he has always felt drawn to the land and knows others feel the same. But a small, isolated community like Assynt is constantly in danger of depopulation. MacAskill hopes that people keep choosing to move back and stay, heeding the same call he did.

The film ends on this note, with a montage of scenes from life in Assynt and the landscape it sits in, while an elderly crofter sings a traditional Scottish song of homecoming.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8454. ‘No Dad, This Is Your Deer.’ Hunter Gets a Second Chance at a 200-Inch Blackpowder Buck

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 22, 2026 2:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

During Virginia’s muzzleloader season last year, Mike Shifflett got a chance at a giant buck.

“I was worried I might have hit him,” Shifflett tells Outdoor Life. “But we looked hard and found no evidence he was hit. I sat in a blind later looking for him and eventually spotted him on trail cameras and he was fine. I completely missed him.”

Virginia had a very harsh winter last year, says the 50-year-old farm manager from Crozet, and he was concerned the buck wouldn’t survive. But he started seeing the whitetail again on trail cameras — then found its sheds.

“I’m sure glad I missed him, because when I started getting trail camera photos of him this summer, his rack had just exploded. His antlers had added at least 30 inches.”

Shifflett monitored the buck through summer and early autumn on trail cameras in the rolling green hills of northern Virginia. He concluded the deer was very much a loner.

“He wasn’t aggressive at all,” Shifflett explained. “He completely avoided other bucks. If there was another buck in a field edge I hunted, he’d never come out.”

He started hunting the deer during this blackpowder season, which opened Oct. 31 and is Shifflett’s favorite time to chase whitetails.

He got trail camera photos of the buck and saw him once, far away at dusk.

On Nov. 6, Shifflett waited for his 16-year-old daughter, Chloe, to get home from school, then they rushed to their blind. The hardsided redneck sat  on a field edge near the top of a mountain, with deer trails feeding into the field.

A little after 5 p.m., the buck stepped out at 200 yards. There were some does in the field, and he began working toward them.

“He worked a scrape and kept walking a few steps at a time toward the does. He was locked on those does, but it took him at least 15 minutes to get to them.

Then the buck, now at 90 yards, started pushing one doe around.

“He was in range and I offered my gun to my daughter to shoot him, but she said ‘No Dad, this is your deer.’”

Shifflett raised his scoped .50-cal T/C and fired.

“The air filled with smoke when I shot, and when it cleared the buck had its tail up, and went into the woods. It was surreal, like he wasn’t hit at all.”

But Chloe had filmed her dad shooting the buck, and the video showed the deer mule kick at the shot.

“But I still didn’t like the way the deer acted as it left the field and into the woods,” says Shifflett.

The two quietly climbed out of the blind and left, planning to return the next morning to track the buck. They returned early the following day with Shifflett’s wife, his son, and a few other helpers.

The search party didn’t initially locate much of a blood trail. But 40 yards into the woods the blood became easy to see. Soon they found the buck dead about 100 yards from the hit site. Shifflet’s bullet had passed through both lungs.

“There was a respectful, silent reverence among us — almost like being in a church.”

Once they got the buck home, Shifflett and his buddies measured its rack.

“I’m no trophy hunter, and when we started scoring him at home I realized it was a really big buck,” he says. “I had entered a local big buck contest earlier this year and they scored my deer at 194 inches. It’s leading the contest with a $1,000 gift card as top prize.”

He got in touch with Virginia Buckmasters scorer Tyler Turner, who measured the 16-point whitetail slightly bigger (Buckmasters has a different scoring system than some traditional record keeping organizations, including Boone and Crockett and Pope and Young). His spread was 19.6 inches and taped out at 202 ⅛ inches.

“Tyler says it’s the biggest Virginia buck in their ‘Regular Deer’ category that’s ever been taken by bow, blackpowder, or gun. He also told me it’s the ninth largest Buckmasters deer in that rack category nationwide.”

It’s been a whirlwind of congratulations since news of his great buck spread. But one thing he says he’ll always remember is when he and his family walked up to the buck that morning.

Read Next: ‘I Tried Not Looking at the Rack.’ Indiana Hunter Tags 199-Inch Buck on State Park Land

“When we found him, everyone became real quiet,” he says. “There was a respectful, silent reverence among us – almost like being in a church.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8451. Florida Man Finishes Four Corners Walk Across America

After over three years of trekking 20,000km back and forth across the continental United States, endurance athlete Kyndal Ray Edwards has completed his ambitious Four Corners Walk. His route took him from coast to coast, then corner to corner, starting in Florida and ending in Maine, where a crowd of supporters met him last week.

Kyndal Ray Edwards, 33, is originally from Orange Park, Florida. After struggling with drug addiction, mental illness, and the justice system during his early adulthood, he decided to become an endurance athlete and mental health advocate. On January 1, 2022, he began his walking project from the beach in Jacksonville.

Over the next 15 months, Edwards walked to Long Beach, Washington, traveling 5,298km from the Atlantic to the Pacific. His plans expanded, and he undertook the challenge of walking to all four corners of the contiguous United States. From Long Beach, he headed to the northwesternmost point, Cape Flattery, also in Washington. After trekking south to San Diego, the next corner he headed for was all the way back in Florida, the southeast extremity.

Along the way, Edwards posted frequently on social media about his journey and his recovery from mental illness.

On October 25, Edwards reached West Quoddy Head Lighthouse in Lubec, Maine. This is the northeast corner of the U.S., and the finish line for Edwards. He walked a total of 19,544 kilometers in 1,022 days, averaging nearly 20km per day.

Edwards and supporters at the finish line. Photo: Kyndal Ray Edwards

A cross-country walking journey, Edwards says, has been his dream since 2013. But with the end of his Walking Across America project, Edwards is already ruminating on future challenges. Online, he has proposed going on an adventure with a stranger in all 50 states, running more ultra marathons, and undertaking endurance challenges on every continent. However, he has yet to commit to one.

The end of Kyndal Ray Edwards’s journey came only days before Karl Bushby, another long-distance trekker, completed his 28-year Goliath Expedition. The pair also happen, Edwards pointed out, to share a birthday.

At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8448. Adventure Links of the Week

When we’re not outdoors, we get our adventure fix by exploring social media and the web. Here are some of the best adventure links we’ve discovered this week.

Yosemite Visitors Join Forces To Perform Extraordinary Rescue on Half Dome: When a Polish hiker slipped while descending the cables on Yosemite’s Half Dome, she slid down the steep granite face and was left clinging to the rock. Her fiancé then lost his footing while trying to reach her.

A group of strangers came together to rescue the pair. Using ropes, slings, carabiners, and climbing harnesses, the group formed a human chain to reach both stranded hikers and pull them to safety.

The Electric Arms Race Has Gone Too Far: Tim Shuff questions the growing use of electric motors in outdoor adventure. He argues that the speed and convenience they create undermine traditionally self-propelled activities like paddling and cycling. Today, you can even buy battery-powered SUPs.

While recognizing that electric assistance can make outdoor sports more accessible, he thinks that its wider use will change wilderness areas, reshape trails, and create pressure to build increasingly powerful equipment.

Jan Spicka descends from Huascarán Sur. Photo: Matej Zak

A Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain: Climbing Peru’s Huascarán Sur turned into a tragedy for a group of five friends last July. Czech alpinist Jan Spicka describes how he and partner Matej Zak reached the 6,768m summit before he plunged into a hidden crevasse on the descent.

Matej’s quick reactions saved his life, but their three friends, Saul Mendoza, Chicho Ugarte, and Arti Salas, disappeared while attempting to descend by a different route. With no official mountain rescue in Peru, friends and volunteers pitched in. After 24 hours in the freezing conditions, they knew it was unlikely they would see their friends again. GPS tracks and later searches suggest they had fallen through a collapsing snow bridge.

He Felt Stuck in New York. So He Started Walking to California: Feeling trapped by unemployment and a lack of opportunities in New York, 29-year-old Chantz Rivera began walking across America. He set off with a tent, sleeping bag, and some supplies in a shopping cart. Over 149 days, he walked to California, pushing his cart 32 to 40km a day along highways and through small towns, documenting his progress for his growing number of online followers.

This Mountain Race Makes Runners Carry 60kg of Beer and Supplies Uphill: Slovakia’s annual Sherpa Rallye is one of the world’s unique mountain races. Competitors have to haul heavy loads of supplies up a 5.7km rocky trail to a remote hut in the High Tatras, nearly 2,000m above sea level.

Men carry 60kg, including a 30-liter keg of beer, while women carry 20kg, with the loads secured to wooden frames. The race was started in 1985 by mountain porter Viktor Beranek. The event brings together the region’s nosiči (traditional mountain porters) and also helps stock the remote hut ahead of winter.

A Hiker Slid Hundreds of Meters Down Mount Timpanogos: A female hiker was seriously injured after slipping off the trail near the summit of Mount Timpanogos in Utah. She slid several hundred feet down the steep mountainside before landing on a narrow cliff ledge. Newly released helicopter footage shows her being hoisted to safety. She was taken to hospital and is reportedly recovering.

We Need to Move Past the ‘Shut Up and Die Like a Man’ Mentality in the Mountains: Climber and paraglider Will Gadd reflects on the psychological impact of mountain accidents. Climbing culture often glorifies the dangers people face in the mountains while discouraging them from admitting they are struggling.

Gadd discusses the deaths of two close friends and challenges the underlying expectation that climbers should simply suck it up and carry on. After decades in the mountains, he has seen that the consequences of an accident extend far beyond the immediate incident, affecting survivors and their families and friends for years.

Two Hikers Dead in Bear Attack in British Columbia: Two hikers, identified as 48-year-old Jennifer Balcarras and her 20-year-old daughter Selah Reed, have died in a suspected grizzly attack near the Snooka West Trail in Bella Coola, British Columbia. Their bodies were found during a search after the pair were reported missing by their family.

This comes just a year after a separate grizzly attack in the same area severely injured four people, including three children. The trail remains closed as authorities continue their investigation.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8445. Antarctica 2026-2027: Expedition Roundup

Here are the Antarctic expeditions for 2026-27. There will be more, as a few still scramble to raise the money or choose to keep their plans to themselves until the last minute. Because of the long charter flights, an expedition to Antarctica, even a “tourist” Last Degree trip, costs close to $100,000 — more, if you want to do something unusual. These are the journeys announced so far. Skiers hit the ice later this month.

Hercules Inlet is the common starting point for South Pole skiers. Skiers need to travel around 1,130km, averaging around 20km per day.

Brit Nathan Lumb originally planned a fully unsupported round-trip to the South Pole but scaled back due to “expedition logistics and funding realities.” He will now ski solo and unsupported from Hercules Inlet to the South Pole. Lumb previously completed a crossing of Norway’s Finnmarksvidda with guiding company Ousland Colliard.

Indian Neeraj Choudhary also trained with Ousland Colliard, crossing the Greenland Ice Sheet in 2024. He hopes to become the second Indian to ski solo and unsupported to the South Pole, following Satish Gogineni, who became the first in 2024-2025.

Satish Gogineni at the South Pole in 2025. Photo: Satish Gogineni

American Lucas Czarnecki will also ski solo and unsupported and claims he will livestream every moment of his journey: “What sets South Signal [his expedition name] apart from other polar expeditions is my commitment to bringing you along for every moment of this extreme adventure. Each day, I will be live-streaming my experience directly from the ice, sharing the raw reality of life on the polar plateau.”

Another American, Meghan Buchanan, will also tackle the route. She plans to start early, leaving for Antarctica on November 12.

Finally, German Dirk Langer will also attempt the route unsupported.

A Berkner Island start means a few extra kilometers, with skiers required to cover around 1,400km to the South Pole.

Max Worsley of the UK and Martin Nesse of Norway also plan to start in early November, but from the edge of Berkner Island. Max is the son of Henry Worsley, who died following an Antarctic expedition in 2015-2016. Max intends to follow much of his father’s final route.

At least two expeditions will begin at the Messner start, 911km from the South Pole. James Garnett of the UK and Norwegian Marius Rehn will each ski solo and unsupported.

Satish Gogineni will be back in Antarctica this year, hoping to build upon his Hercules Inlet experience. Gogineni’s 2026 plan is more ambitious: a solo, 2,000km journey from the outer edge of the Filchner–Ronne Ice Shelf to the inner edge of the Ross Ice Shelf via the South Pole. Gogineni is billing this as a “world-first human achievement” and the “longest solo traverse ever completed on the Antarctic continent.”

If successful, his route would indeed be longer than the truncated crossings of Colin O’Brady and Lou Rudd in 2018. However, his distance would still be well short of Borge Ousland’s 1997 expedition. Ousland became the first person to cross Antarctica solo and unsupported, though he occasionally used a rudimentary handheld kite known as a ski sail. Ousland skied for 65 days and covered 2,845km. At the time, sails were not considered “support.”

Last year, O’Brady tried a significantly longer expedition than his 2018 effort, attempting to ski from the edge of the Ross Ice Shelf to the tip of Berkner Island on the edge of the Ronne Ice Shelf, a total distance of over 2,800km. He eventually ended his crossing attempt after 100 days, with roughly 40% of his total distance remaining.

A map by PECS (the Polar Expeditions Classification Scheme) showing previous solo Antarctic crossing routes. Photo: PECS

Gogineni’s main challenge might be the weight of his sled. With so much weight spread over two sleds, O’Brady was forced to double carry in soft snow on the Ross Ice Shelf last year. A double carry involved shuttling half of his gear forward, then returning to pick up the rest. This meant he had to cover 15km for every 5km of progress. Gogineni says his sled will weigh 181kg.

Mardi Philips, Ida Olsson, and Nia Owens are calling themselves the Antarctic Valkyries and aim to become the “first all-female team to reach the South Pole of Inaccessibility.” They will start at Thorshammer Station (a spot near the Russian Novolazarevskaya Station in Queen Maud Land) and plan to kite-ski 2,500km, first aiming for the Pole of Inaccessibility (the middle of the Antarctic continent), and then continuing to their finish point at the South Pole.

The Antarctic Valkyries proposed route. Photo: Antarctic Valkyries

A Spanish scientific expedition will cross Antarctica by a new route in a unique wind-powered vehicle.

Part of the wind-sled involves a series of tents. Photo: La Vanguardia

Their “wind-sled ” is 15m long and consists of four modules. It is propelled using 20 kites of various sizes. The largest kite measures 150m² and the smallest 5m². The four-man team plans a 3,400km, 85-day journey from near Novolazarevskaya Station to the Italian research station Concordia, known as Dome C, on the Antarctic Plateau.

The wind-sled team’s proposed route. The green return route is planned for a future expedition. Photo: La Vanguardia

Meanwhile, three Argentine kayakers are preparing to paddle roughly 900km along the western coast of the Antarctic Peninsula, from Argentina’s Esperanza Base to San Martín Base. Diego Linares, Juan Tomaselli, and Javier Siede will start their journey in January and plan to spend 30 to 40 days on the water.

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

#8442. A Risky Summit Push on Everest

It’s now or never for the Russian team on the West Ridge of Everest. Andrey Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have set off for the summit without oxygen, hoping the weather will hold. They are completely alone on the mountain, and the push is full of risk.

Everest is still loaded with fresh snow after two cyclones, and there are only three of them to break trail and find the best route above 7,500m. That was their highest point during their earlier venture in October.  In addition, the wind may rise dramatically on Thursday, Vitor Baia, a Portuguese high-altitude meteorologist, told ExplorersWeb.

“It looks like we have a weather window of about a week, followed by a significant intensification of the wind,” leader Andrei Vasiliev told Anna Piunova of Mountain Club.

The question is exactly when the wind will increase, and whether the climbers will have enough time to summit and descend to a safer altitude.

“They have three days of weak wind, until Wednesday [November 11], but then the wind will increase sharply,” said Baia.

He says the best summit day will be Tuesday, with clear skies and weak winds. “Then they could use Wednesday to descend,” he added. “[That day will have] some clouds but still acceptable wind speeds.”

The situation will worsen remarkably on Thursday, when the wind will shift from the west to the northwest and increase dramatically to 50 kph at 8,000m and 60kph at the summit.

In addition, avalanche hazards are still high due to the large amounts of snow after the latest storm two weeks ago. Finally, as winter approaches, it’s getting colder every day, the climbers reported.

After their first trip up ended in a tactical retreat as the cyclone hit, they retreated to Kathmandu to recover from some health issues. Now they are fit again and ready for a second try. They returned to Base Camp last Thursday. Not surprisingly, they found it covered in a thick new layer of snow. Breaking trail, they sank at some points up to their waists, Vasiliev reported.

“Our high-altitude boots were in a cache two hours ahead of us, so we used duct tape to seal our pant cuffs to our trekking boots, trying to keep the snow out,” Kondrashkin reported. They eventually reached the cache and continued up in an exhausting 14-hour-long effort to a second cache (their Camp 1), where they had left their tent and sleeping bags.

They were near an avalanche-prone area and several snow slides swept nearby, prompting them to wait a day before continuing. Meanwhile, Shipilov showed signs of food poisoning. Kodrashkin recalls that Saturday morning started with the following conversation:

“Vitalya, how are you feeling? Are we heading up?”

That answer needs some clarification. A professional like Vitaly Viktorovich [Shipilov] really only has two modes: “We’re going” and “I’m dying.”

In fact, Shipilov had a fever and acute pain in the lower back, so the team headed down to their base in Lobuche. As this was going on, the snow had time to sluff off from the previously avalanche-prone area.

After just one night of rest, Vasiliev confirmed they were going up again. If all went according to plan, they will spend tonight back in their tent above the Lho La. Unfortunately, the climbers have not shared their tracker showing their current location and progress. On their previous trip up, they reached some 7,500m on Everest’s West Shoulder, near the beginning of the West Ridge and the potential detour to the Hornbein Couloir.

Everest’s North Face from the base of the West Ridge. Photo: Sergey Kondrashkin

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8435. Winners of the Banff Mountain Film Festival Announced

The annual Banff Centre Mountain Festival gathers mountain luminaries from across the world at the Banff Centre for Arts and Creativity in Alberta. There, a celebration of mountain culture recognizes the best outdoor films and books of the past year. The climax of the festival took place this past weekend, and the winners of the 51st annual Banff Mountain Film Competition have now been announced.

The Grand Prize went to Listen, directed by Taliesin Black-Brown. In this short documentary, sound engineer Hank Lentfer records the sounds of nature around his Alaska home, while caring for his mother with dementia. As Lentfer documents the degradation of the natural world, we witness the slow loss of his mother. Drawing together these two threads, the film explores connection despite, and through, loss, using sound.

Rose Bush, an Oscar Award-winning director of photography serving on the competition’s jury, called Listen “a film that engages human-caused environmental devastation — the existential dilemma of our time — at its nucleus with overwhelming love and hope.”

The film also won the Grand Jury Prize at the Tribeca Film Festival.

In addition to a grand prize, the festival honors films in several categories. The winning film in the Adventure category was The Navigator, a feature-length work from director Jordan Manley. The film follows Nainoa Thompson, who was the first Native Hawaiian in six centuries to make the journey from Hawaii to Tahiti in a traditional voyaging canoe. Now, almost five decades later, Thompson and his crew make the journey again, using only traditional navigation techniques.

The best film in the Mountain Culture category was Land of Canaan, produced and directed by Maggie Lemere. Lemere’s film follows a group of Palestinian olive farmers, who work to protect their ancient olive groves amid the destruction by Israel. The film “illuminated the vital relationship of courage, vision, hope, and traditional ecological knowledge,” juror Rose Bush said, “in service of the essential value of sovereignty via mountain culture.”

In the Environment category, director James Dawson’s winning comedic documentary Derek vs Derek follows two Devon farmers, both named Derek. One Derek is an ardent dairy farmer while his environmentalist neighbor begins rewilding his land. A feud naturally ensues.

The Arc’teryx-sponsored Best Climbing Film was Zindabad, directed by Tom Fish. Fish follows Pakistani climber Mueez Ud Din into the Karakoram, alongside his uncle Javed Ali and French climber Mathieu Maynadier. Ud Din explores a remote, unknown glacier system, while the film examines what it means to be a Pakistani climber among peaks long claimed by international outsiders.

Directed by Alexis Berg, the Mountain Sports winner, Roots, follows trail runner Ruth Croft as she examines her future as an elite ultra-trail runner after an injury. Roots is “about an inner journey of healing and acceptance, rooted in the essential joy of running outdoors,” explained juror David Arnaud.

The Snow Sports winner centers on American skier Jessie Diggins. Diggins is the most decorated American cross-country skier ever, but Threshold, directed by Torsten Brinkema, reveals the deep personal struggles behind that success. Juror Beki Henderson noted that “the jury particularly commends Jessie Diggins for her extraordinary courage and honesty in sharing her story.”

There were more mountain films this year than you can even imagine. With 610 film submissions and 83 finalists, it’s no wonder that there are still more category winners.

Best short film winner Hello, Beautiful follows climber Alannah Yip as she struggles with identity, training for the Olympics while enduring medical hair loss. Director Darcy Hennessey created a film which “reminds us that personal transformation does not always come from pushing harder,” juror Kerry Stauffer explained.

For best feature film, the competition announced Ceremony, directed and produced by Banchi Hanuse. The film tells the story of the disappearance of the ooligan, a small fish, from British Columbia’s Bella Coola River. Jurors praised the film’s use of watercolor animation, personal stories, and community broadcasts to explore the importance of a seemingly unimportant little fish.

Director Marco Tribelhorn’s 75 seconds won the Creative Excellence Award. The film documents free-skier Jacob Wester’s 75-second-long battle for survival while trapped in an avalanche.

“A compelling visual treatment brings to life an experience that is impossible to imagine,” said Kerry Stauffer.

Last but not least, the Audience Choice Award went to Only Music // Snake River. Director Justin Fountain’s documentary follows a band that ditches the tour bus for a journey down the Snake River, seeking connection with strangers.

Tickets for the international film festival are now available. We have also covered the recently announced Banff Mountain Book Award winners.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.
