#8434. ‘Get Out of the F—ing Tree!’ The Police Raided Our Deer Hunt

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 10:54 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

WHAT STARTED as a normal morning in the deer woods turned out to be the scariest day of my life. It was March 2020, and my friend Billy Phillips and I were hunting one of the suburban properties we have permission on in northern Virginia.

Our state has an extended urban antlerless season that ends in late March, and this particular hunt occurred right after the state of Virginia announced the “Stay at Home” order during the early Covid-19 pandemic. That particular order allowed you to leave home for a couple of reasons, and hunting was one of them.

We were hunting in a neighborhood of multimillion-dollar homes on three- to five-acre lots, and the property we were on that day was a double lot of 10 acres total. In our world, that’s humongous.

We parked at the end of an old driveway and walked a short way to our hunting spots. We climbed into our trees to set up about 100 yards from each other, with Billy closer to the trucks than my stand. Then, just as daylight broke, I spotted the lights of a car near where we parked. I looked through my binos and saw that an older Ford Taurus had pulled up. I remember thinking, Oh, this is kind of odd.

“Yeah, I’m looking at them,” he texted back.

Then I saw Billy’s truck bed light turn on.

“He’s in my car,” Billy texted again. “I’m getting down.”

At this point the sun was fully up and we could clearly see our surroundings. As Billy descended his tree, I saw a police officer in full uniform coming down the little hill toward us with an M4 in his hands. I pulled out my phone to record footage, the kind of video that says, Look at the unusual things we deal with while hunting in the suburbs.

Then I realized the cop had his weapon shouldered and was pointing it at Billy. He was screaming at him.

“Get on the ground! Get on the fucking ground!”

What he was saying was concerning, but the tension in the officer’s voice was worse. Billy was packing up his sticks as the cop approached. If Billy had turned around while holding the sticks or if he still had his crossbow in his hands, I don’t even want to think about what might have happened.

“Where’s your buddy?” the cop started yelling, getting even more aggressive. “Where’s your buddy?”

“Get out of the fucking tree,” he yelled.

“OK,” I replied, “it’s going to take me a minute.”

“I didn’t ask for feedback. I told you to get down here.”

The cop screamed at both of us the whole time it took me to get down. When I walked up to the cop, he spoke into his radio.

We were obviously perplexed, and he explained that he had snipers in the woods with their rifles trained on us.

“What is going on here?” I asked. “What is happening?”

“Shut up,” he told me. “I’m asking questions here. Hands on your head and turn around.”

I kept persisting with questions, trying to figure out why we were being searched at gunpoint and who this police officer was. The man wouldn’t tell us anything and proceeded to pat us down.

“I’m not a regular cop,” was all he said. “I’m a special cop.”

Read Next: The Best Tree Saddles, Tested and Reviewed

When the officer asked us what we were doing at this property, we explained that we are deer hunters and that we had permission to be here. I pulled up the signed permission slip from the landowner and showed it to him. Then I asked again what he was doing here. The officer told us that they spotted us on a UAV — an unmanned aerial vehicle. He was suggesting he had a military drone 30,000 feet above us, which is completely ridiculous for our county or most any police department.

“This is Fairfax County,” I said. “What are you talking about?”

His demeanor shifted and he got less aggressive — especially when I asked him why he was going through our vehicles.

“You had a wire on the floor, and I thought it was a bomb,” he said to Billy. Billy explained that he works in landscaping, and that he has sprinkler-head components in his truck.

By now we were starting to get nervous, and I noticed the officer’s uniform didn’t look quite right. There was something off about him. We kept asking why he was searching our cars without cause, and why he was on private property.

He didn’t give us his card or an explanation, and no other officers showed up. After the adrenaline of having a gun pointed at us wore off, Billy and I ran through what had just happened.

I told Billy that I thought the guy must be impersonating law enforcement, because the whole interaction just didn’t sit right with me. Billy and I mentor several police officers who are learning to hunt, and we’re huge supporters of police in general. Mind you, this was when Covid lockdowns were at their peak, and there was mass civil unrest throughout the country. The last thing we wanted to do was blast the police.

Still, we needed answers. We sent the short video clip I had recorded to a few of our police officer buddies, and one called me right away. He explained that it didn’t look right, and that what happened is not standard police procedure. He asked where we were and made a few phone calls to see if he could find out any details for us.

When my buddy called me back, he was laughing so hard he could barely speak. The man who had just ordered us down from our trees at gunpoint was indeed a police officer known as the department’s Tackleberry. (In the Police Academy movies, Tackleberry is a tryhard who’s overzealous in his enforcement.) This particular police officer had been on desk duty and was sent out on patrol as a result of scheduling issues caused by Covid.

My buddy learned that the officer had gotten turned around, thought he was at a park, and that we were doing something illegal. Billy and I had a meeting with the guy’s captain, who apologized profusely. We decided to leave it at that.

Read Next: Deer Hunting Every Day in the Suburbs of Virginia

It was a terrifying and confusing ordeal while it was happening, but now that a few years have passed, it’s just another crazy story from our time hunting the D.C. suburbs. Here’s this guy who thought he was busting the biggest crime ring of all time and instead ended up nearly arresting a couple rednecks in camo.

#8433. A 3,300Km Walk Across Japan in Winter

Japanese publisher Katsunari Tanaka has set himself the challenge of hauling a handcart 3,345km from Japan’s northernmost tip to the southern edge of Okinawa in winter.

Tanaka, 49, set off from Cape Sōya in Hokkaido on October 7 and is heading south to Cape Hedo in Okinawa. He hopes to complete the traverse in 100 days over five months. As he will have to contend with some challenging winter conditions along the way, he has described it as “playing chase with winter.”

Rather than carrying everything on his back, he is pulling a handcart that weighs around 100kg. He also has a support team with a vehicle in case of emergencies. At some points, different members of his team have walked with him for a day or so. His daily target is around 35km, with rest days and organized stops built into the schedule.

The first few weeks have already brought strong headwinds, rain, and some steep climbs, making progress difficult at times. Temperatures have dropped close to freezing. At the end of October, he crossed a mountain pass between Yoichi and Iwanai. There, headwinds stirred up the gravel as he walked. Then the snow began. In the end, he took refuge with his support team until the weather improved.

Despite the conditions, Tanaka reached Hakodate Harbor on November 3, having covered 621km across Hokkaido. From Hakodate, he is crossing the Tsugaru Strait by ferry to Aomori, then will continue south along the Sanriku coast to Sendai.

From there, he follows the Pacific coast through Fukushima to Tokyo before joining the Tokaido route toward Osaka before heading west to Kyushu. A second ferry crossing brings him to Okinawa, where he will walk to his final destination, Cape Hedo.

Along a road in Hokkaido. Photo: Katsunari Tanaka/Instagram

This is not Tanaka’s first experience of traveling Japan with a handcart. Around a decade ago, he spent three years visiting all 47 prefectures, selling books directly to people he met along the way. Loading his publications onto a cart, he covered the country between 2013 and 2016, eventually selling 13,477 books. At the time, many people questioned what he was doing and whether books could really be sold on the street. But he says the experience shaped his approach to business and his belief in the power of personal connections.

Tanaka insists that he is not an adventurer. Instead, he is traveling the country as a “merchant.” This time, he is making the journey to support his mentor, Arthur Hollands, a Japanese evangelist known for undertaking lengthy walks while carrying a large wooden cross. Along the route, Tanaka plans to stop at 11 locations to give lectures. He welcomes people to join him for sections of the walk.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8430. First Ski Descent of Meru Central

Tiphaine Duperier and Boris Langenstein of France have notched another Himalayan first ski descent, this time down the southwest face of Meru Central (6,310m) in India’s Garhwal Himalaya. The skiers are still surprised by how lucky they were.

Duperier admitted they started the expedition with low expectations after several projects earlier in the fall all failed.

“Snow conditions had been unforgiving,” she wrote. “We figured there was only a 3-in-10 chance of skiing the southwest face of Meru.”

But when they got there, the mountain was, they added, “generous” with them.

The three-peaked Meru massif, located in the Gangotri Glacier region of the Garhwal Himalaya, lies between Shivling and Thalay Sagar. It has three peaks: 6,660m Meru South, 6,310m Meru Central, and 6,450m Meru North. The ski descent line is in purple. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

“We spent six days of reconnaissance and three [days] climbing the peak, [thanks to] an unprecedented stroke of luck in finding a way through the icefall and perfect snow in the upper section,” she explained.

The icefall on Meru Central. Photo: Boris Langenstein

The icefall at the base of the peak was indeed one of the most complex passages. The pair climbed a 15m pitch of WI4 and left a safety rope, which they also used to rappel down after the ski descent, Duperier told PlanetMountain.

Otherwise, the pair of regular expedition and skiing partners climbed alpine-style and pretty fast, reaching the summit in less than eight hours. According to their research, they completed the second-ever ascent of the southwest face of Meru, after an Indian team in 2023.

At the summit on Meru Central. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

The skiers estimate they covered 1,500m on their descent, with some sections reaching 50 °. Snow conditions were good overall, hard but with a good grip.

“We skied like little monkeys on the wires in Rishikesh,” Duperier posted on social media. “It was just incredible, truly beautiful!”

While the climbers rappelled down the icefall onto the glacier below, the ski descent itself had no rappels or interruptions.

Duperier barely had time to return home to France to pick up a Piolet d’Or for the first alpine-style ascent of the Schell Route on the Rupal Face of Nanga Parbat, followed by the first ski descent from the 8,125m summit.

Tiphaine Duperier at the Piolets d’Or ceremony. Photo: Piotr Drozdz

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8427. I Hunted Ducks and Geese During the Glory Days of Earthquake Lake

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 12:25 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

It was a good day for waterfowl hunters. Wisps of fog seeped out of the melting landscape, and the sky was so low and solid that the geese—if they came—would be close to the deck. And it was cold. Two days before, a savage cold wave had swept across Tennessee, plastering trees and highways with ice and freezing ponds thicker than anyone could remember. Ducks and geese had been sitting it out, but on this third day, the ice was melting, and they would fly.

I slid the overhead hatch back and stuck my head up and out of the blind. Earlier it had been too dark to see how we were situated. I found that we were in a soybean field of perhaps 300 acres, already plowed for the spring planting. There was sparse timber on all sides. To the south, the way hunters faced when standing in the blind, the timber was not so tall or thick, but there was the heavy undergrowth that grows at water’s edge. The lake is closer than I thought, I said to myself. It’s only 100 yards or so away.

The blind was one of the biggest I had ever seen, and it was also almost invisible. From a duck’s overhead view, it no doubt looked like a rush-covered finger of land surrounded on three sides by timber, water, and waterfowl decoys—hundreds of them.

Thus oriented, I closed the camouflaged hatch and placed my feet near the gas heater. Sheer luxury! Being there almost made up for all those miserable days I have spent crouched in freezing mud holes. Perhaps a time or two before, in Texas and Louisiana, I’d been in more luxurious shooting stands, but they were small houses built on stilts above the water. This was a blind dug into the ground, lined with concrete, and covered with earth that had been planted with suitable cover. The blind’s eyes on the outside world were 12 sliding hatches.

Inside, the padded theater seats were as comfy as easychairs. Theater seats! I thought. Who in hell would have thought of putting theater seats in a duck blind? But it was perfect! The spring-loaded bottoms snapped up out of the way when you rose to shoot. That gave a man more foot and leg room and eliminated bruised shins if he turned around for backyard shots. I’m forever scraping my shins on the sharp-edged board benches found in most blinds.

As the light increased, so did the yipping of the Canada geese rafted up on Lake Isom just beyond the brushy boundary. A few minutes before, the even din of honking had changed pitch when a raft of Canadas took wing. Moments later another flight heaved into the morning fog.

Our lookout Jerry Jones made no sign that geese were coming our way, but the atmosphere inside the blind was becoming so charged with excitement that you could almost touch it. Feet shuffled, experienced hunters quaked with anticipation, and hands rattled shotshells in jacket pockets to make sure fast reloads were ready. Overhead and close by, there was a whistle of teal. Jerry’s eyes followed their crossing flight, but he did not signal us to shoot, because he didn’t want to spoil our chances for the big honkers that could be only minutes away.

From the lake came another din of geese taking off, and Jerry pulled his head lower and tightened his grip on his Browning autoloading magnum. Then he crouched even lower, eyes level with his lookout hole, and said, “They’re ours boys. Get ready—now!”

Shotgun barrels poked out of the blind, and there was ragged fire at eight or nine geese that were angling by to our left. The closest bird went limp in midair, but the one I fired at twice merely did the double wobble of a wise old goose that knows he’s being shot at.

Long ago I learned that in each flight of waterfowl there are one or two obvious birds. They are closer or bigger or out in front, and they tend to draw fire. When I’m sharing a blind with other hunters, I therefore pass up the obvious targets and go for a more obscure member of the flight. That bird is all mine, and I know for sure if I hit or miss. This time the obvious bird did draw most of the fire and was the only one to fall. Happily there was no chorus of “I got him!” that sometimes mars such events. These men, most of whom I’d met only that morning, were too experienced and too polite for that. Instead, each one claimed to have missed by several feet and that the goose must have been felled by the youngest member of the party, 15-year-old Charlie Lawrence.

“I did?” he whooped in wide-eyed amazement. “And that means you’ll have to fetch it,” Charlie’s father almost managed to say before the young hunter was out of the blind and sprinting after the prize, his rubber boots going flub-flub and launching dollops of river-bottom mud from the Mississippi in all directions.

Whoever first said that you can tell a lot about a man’s character by hunting with him really knew what he was talking about. Settling back into my posh seat, I knew I had learned a lot about my new acquaintances already and had a feeling that we would all be good friends before the day ended.

The only one of us who hadn’t pulled a trigger on the geese was Ralph McDonald, the well-known wildlife artist. His sliding hatch had stuck when the action started, so he had to sit out the first round. But Jerry Jones fixes that in a hurry by leaving the hatch open and covering the opening with a layer of cane that could easily be pushed aside. I liked that arrangement better than moving the hatch, so he fixed me up the same way. It gave me a better look at incoming birds.

In addition to Jerry, Ralph, and myself, our shooting party included H. Lea Lawrence, his teen-aged sons David and Charlie, country-music star Jim Ed Brown, plus Bill Morrison and Tom Callery. Lea, who lives in Nashville, is an outdoor writer whose name is familiar to readers of OUTDOOR LIFE. Morrison and Callery are field and legislative assistants to U.S. Senator William Brock of Tennessee. When I met Morrison and Callery that morning and learned of their political connections, I made a mental note to bend their ears about pending firearms legislation.

But there was no time for that now—ducks were coming, and Jerry was making seductive sounds on his big-barreled duck call. I couldn’t see the incomers from my angle, but it was easy enough to pinpoint their position by watching Jerry’s eyes. As they came closer, he crouched lower in the blind, and I knew they were within range even before he whisper-shouted, “Now!”

About 10 spoonbills were winging low and fast from right to left in front of the blind. I swung my 12-gauge double, a Model 21 Winchester, through one of the trailers, but before I could press the trigger the bird’s head kicked back and he collapsed in midair. Then I was aware of three or four other ducks tumbling out of the formation, but my attention was concentrated on another spoonbill. I swung the barrels ahead by about two feet and pressed the trigger. The load of No. 4s folded the spoonbill so cleanly that there was no need to think about a follow-up shot, so I chased the barrels to the next closest bird and gave him the remaining round. It was a straightaway shot, and the range was rapidly increasing with each beat of his wings. For a moment it looked like a miss, but then the spoonbill did a turnover and went into a power dive before thumping hard on the semifrozen field. At 60 yards, perhaps more, it had been a lucky shot, but all eyes had been on me, and the welfare of my ego was at stake.

While David and Charlie fetched the downed waterfowl, I made the innocent mistake of asking Ralph if he had scored on the spoonbills. Though he is a leading wildlife artist whose paintings have twice been featured on the cover of the Ducks Unlimited magazine, he has a well-earned reputation as a hard-luck waterfowl hunter. On his goose hunts something always goes wrong, and he was still looking to shoot his first honker.

Jerry, who owns and operates Sunset Court and Dock on Reelfoot Lake, is one of those old-fashioned guides who believes that a day not spent hunting or fishing is a day wasted. Just to make sure that he and his customers get in plenty of both, his fees for a guided goose-and-duck hunt—including lodging, a heated blind, decoys, transport to the blind, plus some of the finest duck calling and yarn spinning you’re likely to hear anywhere—are no more than you’d expect to pay to spend the night in a rundown New York hotel.

While I was hunting the Reelfoot and Lake Isom area, the local waterfowl population was estimated at some 70,000 geese and about a quarter of a million ducks, mainly gadwalls and mallards.

Historic Reelfoot Lake is well known among waterfowlers and it is an easy day’s drive from many large Midwestern and Eastern cities. Lake Isom, however, remains distinctly undiscovered, even though it is only a few miles away (see map, page 89).

Hunting seasons run from December into January. The duck limit is based on the point system. Some ducks, such as pintails and spoonbills (shovelers), are rated at only 10 points each, so it is possible to bag 10 ducks plus two geese in a single day. That’s a lot of shooting.

Ralph was about halfway through a lengthy explanation of how he had failed to get off a single shot at the geese when Jerry shouted “Get down!” at the two young Lawrence brothers, who were still gathering ducks. Another flight of spoonbills had just cleared Isom’s boundary and were coming directly at us. Charlie had left his shotgun in the blind, but older and wiser David had his pump ready and neatly dropped a drake.

The first half-hour had produced a goose and a half a dozen ducks—rich promise of a good day of shooting—but by midmorning the action tapered off to only an occasional shot at a passing duck. For some reason known only to themselves, the geese had decided to leave by the western side of the lake. We could hear their comings and goings, but none came within half a mile. Over a breakfast of ham and eggs cooked in a subterranean kitchen attached to the blind and complete with gas stove, we discussed the sagging action. Jerry allowed that we could move to a blind closer to the flyway the honkers had selected that day. Jerry owns or has access to 15 blinds.

After a quick scouting sortie, he asked for volunteers to try another blind situated where the honkers seemed to be heading, but he also told us the road was bad and we’d have to walk a ways. Jim Ed Brown, Lea Lawrence, and I volunteered, but not without some misgivings on my part.

Ralph McDonald elected to stay where he was, because, as he put it, he’d “… had a vision that the geese would soon come that way.”

Even before we got to our new blind, there was no doubt that Jerry was right. While walking about a quarter of a mile through a sparse stand of scrub timber, we spotted three flights of geese skimming low over a field of winter wheat. They landed almost in the new blind.

“Holy catfish!” said Jim Ed in the soft Southern accents that have made him a favorite of Grand Ole Opry fans. “All I need is two.”

Just then six or eight more honkers slipped in low over the wheatfield, wings cupped to land. Before they touched down, the leader thought better of it, and they flared hard left, leveled off treetop high, and headed straight at us. We were almost through the scrub brush by then, and there wasn’t much cover, but we ducked into what concealment there was.

“Get ready,” Jerry whispered just an arm’s length to my left. Like me, he was half hidden in a honeysuckle bank. Just then Jim Ed and Lea opened up, and two honkers dropped at once. A third, hit hard, scaled overhead, and Jerry or me, or both of us, hit him at less than 30 yards and folded him cleanly.

The rest of the formation had swerved violently when Lea and Jim Ed started shooting, and they were out of range before I could come to bear with my second barrel. The geese were still reasonably close to Jim Ed, and with the last shot in his Model 12 Winchester pump, another Canada pitched down, wing feathers humming in the rush of the fall.

“Friends,” drawled Jim Ed, “this is what I was brought up to call good goose hunting. I’ve got my limit, and we ain’t even in the blind.”

The timing was perfect, because less than an hour later Jim Ed was scheduled to leave by charter plane to tape his popular “Nashville On The Road” TV show. So he and Jerry took the four honkers back to the truck while Lea and I went on to the blind. Jerry got back as quickly as he could.

We had no more than settled ourselves when a flight of perhaps 20 geese came soaring up the field. Jerry called, and they began to circle. When we stood to fire, they flared sharply and momentarily gave us that near-motionless target goose hunters love. I shot at a bird on the far right end of the formation so there would be little chance that Lea and I would double on the same target. The Canada spun and fell without a flutter.

Meanwhile Lea was doing the same with a honker he had cut out of the flight.

That evening we feasted on lake-country froglegs, catfish, hush puppies, and tart cole slaw. It had been a great day for everyone except Ralph McDonald.

“I was sure my hunch about those geese was a good one,” he lamented, pouring himself a stiff snort of Reelfoot tea, “but I never got a shot. My record is intact.”

Read Next: I Gave Up My Duck Blind to Hunt Where the Mallards Actually Are

Reelfoot Lake in far western Tennessee was formed by the New Madrid earthquakes of 1811-12. The catastrophe depressed some 50,000 acres that were later submerged by water from the Mississippi River. The surge was so great that the Mississippi is said to have flowed backward for a while. Due to siltation, plant succession, and drainage, Reelfoot Lake now covers about 18,000 acres.

A colorful legend accounts for the lake’s name. A Chickasaw chief suffered from a deformed or club foot and reeled when he walked. Chief Reelfoot could not persuade any of the Chickasaw maidens to become his wife, so he and his warriors kidnapped a Choctaw maiden named Starlight. Her father, who would not allow the marriage because of Reelfoot’s deformity, was so angered that he ordered his medicine men to bring down a terrible vengeance on Reelfoot and his people. That, the legend has it, was the cause of the earthquake.

Lake Isom, scene of the waterfowl hunt in this story, was isolated from the big lake when a road was built around the southern end of the lake. A water-control structure and a ditch aided in draining the land that lies between the lakes today. The lake was given a name when the site was acquired for a waterfowl refuge from the Isom family.

Reelfoot’s excellent waterfowl habitat made it one of the most famous hunting areas on the Mississippi Flyway. The open-water portions attracted a variety of diving ducks, particularly canvasbacks. Mallards were most abundant and most popular among gunners. Eventually, however, the canvasbacks almost disappeared, and restrictive limits were imposed on mallard shooting. In the late 1960’s, with a one-mallard limit, Reelfoot had an all-time low in hunter usage.

As the mallards and some other ducks increased in numbers after the low, and limits were increased, the popularity of Reelfoot Lake among gunners increased somewhat, but it is taking a while for hunters to realize that it’s the same hotspot. Reelfoot is as good today as it ever was, and Lake Isom is just as good. Neither Reelfoot nor Isom is managed by the state, but public hunting is permitted at Reelfoot. At Lake Isom the hunting is on private land.

#8426. Arctic Town Kills Dozens of Grizzlies Feeding on Dump

For years, the grizzlies near Inuvik in Canada’s western Arctic relied on a stable source of food — the town dump. Generations of bears grew up feeding there. In 2025, wildlife officials found that over 40 grizzlies regularly visited the dump.

In 2025, before the fence was built, trail cameras captured multiple grizzlies hanging around the dump, looking for food. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

It was too much. This year, the town built an electric fence around the site, hoping the bears would move on. They didn’t. Some started coming into town looking for food. Others just hung around the fence, searching for a way in.

“They would just do these laps around the dump, and they would just be emaciated, sickly,” said Toby Halle, a Renewable Resources officer based in Inuvik, told the CBC. “They don’t know what to do with themselves. And quite frankly, we don’t know what to do with them.”

Inuvik, population about 3,500, was the first community in that part of the Arctic to try to wean the bears off their dependence on dump food with such a fence, which cost about $700,000. The problem was, the bears had not learned to depend on any other source of food.

“We’ve probably had three to four generations of bears that have been using that landfill,” said local mayor Peter Clarkson. In the end, the town took a drastic solution — but one that was probably unavoidable in the circumstances: They shot 28 bears.

Tragic though this is, officials hope that within the next couple of years, the remaining bears in the area will move on and learn to eat more natural fare.

Many other Arctic towns still have open dumps where bears come to feed. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8423. Leonard Clark: Explorer, Spy, Liar

Born in 1907, Leonard Francis Clark was an explorer, war hero, and writer who made daring expeditions all over the world before dying mysteriously in Venezuela in 1957.

Today, he isn’t well known, but has this lack of scrutiny been a blessing in disguise for this forgotten explorer’s legacy? His books, filled with adventure and excitement, read like fiction. The more one digs into the forgotten reports and old newspaper articles, the stranger his story, and the more complicated the man.

A Tibetan expedition under Leonard Clark in 1949. Photo: The Marching Wind

The main sources on Leonard Clark are all, well, Leonard Clark. This isn’t necessarily damning, but it is something he shares with most good con artists or cult leaders. The following is a short biography based only on official records, such as draft cards and census data. The first rule of reading historical accounts is that every historical figure is a liar.

Leonard Francis Clark was born in British Columbia, Canada, in January 1907 to two Americans, Maud and Francis Clark. In 1909, they moved back to Maud’s birth state of California, and Francis promptly died.

There are conflicting statements online about which university he attended, but census data shows him living only a few kilometers from the University of California (now the University of California, Berkeley) in 1930, so I’d put my money on that one. He began traveling right out of college, spending three years in Borneo on a hunting trip with his brother, Laurence. By the late 1930s, he was on a National Geographic-funded expedition to the South China Sea.

His article, cryptically titled Among the Big Knot Lois of Hainan, was published in the 1938 September edition. In it, he recounts how he and a small expeditionary force landed in the midst of a deadly cholera epidemic. Relying on hired porters to carry their supplies, Clark and Nicol Smith, another future travel writer, set off into the interior. Soon, malaria and infection laid low most of the team, including, per Clark’s article, Smith.

Again, according to Clark, Smith urged him to take the remaining five porters, the minimum of equipment, and keep going.

Much of the article is dedicated to ethnographic observations, with the typical exoticism and patronizing tone of the era. At one point, Clark noted that the Li people of Hainan reminded him of indigenous Amazonian people and “the men were tall, many six feet.”

A recent study showed that the average height for men in Hainan today is 168.7cm, around 5’6″, and the same study found that average heights had increased over the past century. This is a small nitpick, but he’ll repeat this unlikely claim again in the Amazon. It is worth noting that the “six-foot-tall Indian warrior” was a stock character in contemporary pulp adventure fiction.

There is an interesting contrast between Clark’s account and some of the newspaper reports, especially one from a Virginia paper, which relied on Smith for the story. This article implies Smith did much more than lay around his tent with malaria. Clark’s article claims he was the first white man to enter 47 different villages, while the newspaper says that Smith was the first Westerner to penetrate inland.

There is a third source: the 37-minute silent film compiled from the expedition’s footage and photographs. In it, we briefly see a white man lying under a mosquito net as if ill. But whether this is Smith, Clark — who admits himself to experiencing malaria symptoms on the expedition — or the third westerner on the expedition, one Jack Munroe, the film doesn’t say.

His previous experience in the region led him to return a few years later, this time on behalf of the U.S. Office of Strategic Services (OSS), a precursor to the CIA. His work during the war centered around the China-Burma-India corridor, particularly the Yellow River valley. He was involved in organizing guerrilla activities, building intelligence networks, and navigating the Japanese surrender of Taiwan.

In a later press statement, Clark explained how he had worked with General Dai Li, and that “the Reds had it in for him,” due to the infamously repressive actions Dai Li took against Chinese Communists. Dai Li, whom historian Barbara Tuchman called “China’s combination of Himmler and J. Edgar Hoover,” was killed in a 1946 plane crash. Wide speculation places the blame for this crash on either the Chinese Communist Party or the OSS. This would make things awkward for Clark a few years later.

He continued his government work in the area after the Second World War, sliding right into the developing Cold War. In 1949, Clark led a large, well-funded expedition into Tibet.

It would have gotten him in the history books if Leonard Clark identified a peak higher than Everest. Unfortunately for him, he didn’t. But he certainly proposed that he had, when he claimed that he’d surveyed Amne Machin and found it to be 9,040m high.

Amne Machin is not taller than Everest. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

Clark published his account of the expedition years later, titled The Marching Wind. In it, he wrote with his typical style: daring escapes, stereotypically aggressive natives, and breezy adventure writing. What it did not contain was the data to support his claims.

As soon as he released his first report and measurements, geographers, geologists, and mountaineers pored over them. Bradford Washburn, a cartographer, photographer, and explorer, published a thorough examination of the surveying techniques and questionable results from Clark’s expedition.

A contemporary review in the Alpine Journal, commenting on his photographic evidence, wrote that “anything more unconvincing could hardly be imagined — yet, ostensibly…the mountain was the object of the expedition.” More on that “ostensibly” later. Suffice it to say, Clark’s estimate that Amne Machin could be almost 1,000m taller than Everest was not adopted by geographers at large.

The review concluded that “it can hardly be wondered if mountaineers, whom Mr. Clark rather despises, cannot accept his results.”

Clark blithely remarked that he had “some rather bulky complaints by mountain and other specialists,” but admitted subpar instruments made his results “indecisive.”

A surveying party including Clark, far right, taking observations of Amne Machin. Photo: The Marching Wind

If he doesn’t seem too broken up, it may be because Amne Machin’s height wasn’t the main purpose of his trip. Clark was supported logistically and went on behalf of Ma Bufang, the fiercely anti-communist warlord ruling Qinghai, on the border of Tibet. Anticipating that he could not long hold out against the People’s Liberation Army, Clark proposed that his expedition would map a path of retreat. This would help Ma Bufang’s army cross into Tibet and continue their anti-Communist campaign from the mountains.

Clark envisioned Ma Bufang’s Qinghai, through Tibet, becoming “the bulwark against Red invasion of India.” Clark went on the expedition and did his mapping, but the PLA took Qinghai only months later, and Ma Bufang fled to Hong Kong.

In his introduction to his book, Clark claims that the United States was unaware of his “very slight aid” to Ma Bufang and local anti-Communist guerrilla activities, and he involved himself with the Chinese Nationalist army “as a private American citizen.” If I were to say that I was skeptical of this claim, I would not be the first to do so. But that falls under speculation, so I will say only that I sent a Freedom of Information Act request to the CIA regarding Clark’s work in Tibet. However, the records were not forthcoming.

After returning from the mountains, Clark went to a wedding anniversary celebration in Canton, surrounded by his fellow Allied intelligence members and their set. Things got a little out of hand, and Clark soon found himself in a Cantonese jail with a bullet wound and two manslaughter charges.

Chinese Nationalist soldiers on Clark’s Tibetan expedition. Photo: The Marching Wind

The other two men involved were Harold Harris, an Englishman who died immediately, and Willard Freeman from Massachusetts, who died later from his injuries. Clark also sustained a gunshot wound, but had recovered sufficiently by late November to be moved from hospital to prison.

The three had been drinking together in Freeman’s home when, at about 4 am, servants reported hearing gunshots. Mrs. Maya Freeman entered the room at 10 am to find an odd and grisly scene. Harris, fully clothed, lay dead on Freeman’s bed with three gunshot wounds. Freeman, completely nude, was also lying on the bed, alive with two shots to the abdomen. Clark, in his underwear, was on a second bed, with a single gunshot to the chest. Furniture was overturned, indicating a fight, and all three were covered in scratches. The only weapon found was Clark’s .32 caliber pistol.

Later that month, the Canton District Court denied Clark’s bail request. The court, still trying to determine what exactly happened, focused on the widowed Maya Freeman. Interviewing her for hours, they attempted to make her confess to an affair with Clark. Both of them repeatedly denied it. Clark further denied that he had fired any of the six shots, though he didn’t name the shooter.

Clark’s brother contacted former OSS head General William Donovan, who rallied the State Department. Clark reportedly was concerned that if he wasn’t released before the city fell to the Communists, he would be tortured for information regarding Dai Li and OSS activities. The State Department investigated and, thanks to its intervention, Clark was released.

Clark’s legal troubles were splashed across newspapers worldwide. Photo: The Journal (Caldwell, Ohio) November 24, 1949

What exactly happened in Freeman’s house that night? Was the dispute between the Allied Intelligence men personal, or professional? If the world were ever to find out, it wouldn’t be from Clark. Once freed, Clark returned to California and moved on to his next adventure. It was an ambitious one: Leonard Clark was going to discover the lost city of El Dorado.

He had an old parchment map to the lost city, he claimed, which showed it lay in the upper reaches of Peru. Never mind that it had been pretty well established for over a century that El Dorado was a myth, following the efforts of explorers Alexander von Humboldt and Robert Schomburgk.

Clark’s resulting account, The Rivers Ran East, contains all the genre tropes you would expect from a fictional work in the same setting. Native Amazonians are ferocious cannibals who capture Clark and keep him in a poison snake pit, crocodiles can and must be lassoed (there are no crocodiles in Peru, only caimans, a type of alligator), and there is a beautiful lady explorer, Inez Pokorny, who is secretly part of a London-based international El Dorado-seeking organization. If this organization existed, I have found no trace of it outside of this book.

If I were to list every blatant impossibility in his account, we would be here for days, so I’ll just continue on one particular thread: Leonard Clark seems to have a very loose grasp of which animals are in Peru and how big they are. At one point, he is blinded by a venom-spitting snake. There are three venom-spitting snake species, and none of them are in South America.

His 500-pound (227kg) boars must be peccaries, pig-like animals native to the Americas. The Amazon native white-lipped peccary tops out around 40kg, while the unconfirmed giant peccary still has only been estimated at 50kg. Likewise, his 12m-long pythons must be anacondas, which have a maximum recorded length of eight meters.

Clark claims to have found El Dorado. What he found was a group of Chicham people panning for gold, who traded some of it to Clark and Pokorny. According to his book, Clark traveled under the pretense of investigating botanical medicines, while actually searching for El Dorado.

In fact, as the El Dorado adventures on the page grew more and more ridiculous, I began to wonder if it were not the other way around. Here I have fully entered the realm of speculation. American corporations were, and still are, extremely interested in extracting resources from South America, including pharmaceuticals. In fact, Clark also reported success in this area, claiming to have identified over 60 native medicinals with commercial possibility — as well as Amazonian oil.

In praise of Clark’s work, Baron Wolfenegg, Director of Colonization for the Orient in Peru, accidentally said the quiet part out loud, stating he’d “learned much from this book that will help my work of colonizing this vast jungle.”

On Peru’s Perené River. Photo: The Rivers Ran East

It is practically gauche for explorers to die in their beds, and Clark, who seemed to mold his life to the shape of adventure stories, was no exception. Clark was in Venezuela in 1957, looking, he claimed, for diamond mines. He entered the Amazon basin, never to emerge again.

One history of the OSS offhandedly mentions that Clark died with “two native arrows in his back.” I suspect the explorer’s penchant for sensationalism survived his death. Other contemporary reports describe Clark as having drowned when his canoe overturned.

As I pointed out earlier, most of the information on Clark’s expeditions came from Clark himself. Since he never returned, startlingly little information is available about his death at age 49.

The area around the Orinoco River in Venezuela is, in fact, rich in diamond deposits. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

The line between fact and fiction is notoriously vague in much of the adventure travel genre. Is Herman Melville telling the full truth about his time spent on the island of Nuku Hiva? Did anyone actually complete the “Long Walk” out of a Soviet detention camp, which Slavomir Rawicz falsely claimed as his own tale? How much artistic license and embellishment is acceptable before exploration accounts become outright hoaxes?

I didn’t write this article with the goal of proving Leonard Clark a charlatan. It can hardly be denied that he really was going to these places, at the very least. But there are enough discrepancies and inaccuracies in his accounts to conclude that they should be taken with a grain of salt, and his treasure-hunting pseudo-archaeological tendencies make his anthropological findings, well, questionable.

As a contemporary New Yorker book critic put it, Clark had “the same gift, or weakness, for grandiose verbiage that got Marco Polo in trouble with his fellow Venetians.”

The real story, and one which may never be fully disentangled, lies in why, exactly, Clark was doing his exploring. Somewhere between the romantic adventurer and the cynically colonialist spy using exploration as a guise for the extractive, violent enforcement of Western capitalist hegemony, lies the truth.

“Every explorer has two faces,” Clark wrote in the opening to The Rivers Ran East; “the secret one and the one he shows to the world.”

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8418. The Dane Steadman Interview: Hard Going and High Stakes in the Karakoram

There is much more to an expedition than summit or failure. In this exclusive interview, American climber Dane Steadman explains why his recent new route on Karakoram’s Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) “was much more difficult than the one on Yashkuk Sar, both on and off the mountain.” Strong words, considering that he and his partners were awarded a Piolet d’Or for Yashkuk Sar.

The one factor on the team’s side was the previous experience of Steadman and Cody Winckler. “It was our third trip, so we knew a lot more about climbing there,” Steadman said. Indeed, after Yashkuk Sar, Winckler and Steadman returned to Pakistan in 2025 and climbed two new routes with Blake Berghoff.

Grant Stewart of Canada, the third member of the Sosbun Brakk team, is a skilled climber but was less familiar with the Karakoram. His most remarkable climbs had taken place in Alaska and at home in the Canadian Rockies.

Comparing the two epic climbs, Steadman highlighted the differences.

“The trip to Yashkuk Sar went incredibly smoothly. Getting there and acclimatizing was great. We had an amazing weather window for the climb. Nothing on it was exceptionally hard, and we did it on the first try, and the descent went without a hitch,” he said.

He explained that the hardest parts of that 2024 first ascent were their lack of experience and the uncertainty that came with it. An unexpected mishap also occurred during the climb itself: a snow mushroom collapsed above them, forcing them to change their ascent route halfway up.

“On the other hand,” Steadman went on, “almost everything about the Sosbun Brakk trip was hard: lost bags, sickness acclimatizing, big storms beforehand and while on the mountain, hard climbing and route-finding, and a very difficult descent through terrible weather.”

The unclimbed north face of Sosbun Brakk. Photo: Dane Steadman

“[Overall,] on the Yashkuk trip, I think we felt for most of the time that we would be able to do it with only some moments of doubt here and there,” Steadman admitted. “On Sosbun, we thought much of the time that we wouldn’t be able to do it for all the reasons above. We were quite surprised [to succeed].”

One unusual aspect of the Sosbun Brakk expedition was its dates. They went to Pakistan and climbed in early fall, two months later than the Karakoram’s typical July-August climbing season. Yet, Steadman says this was planned to account for the changing climate in the high ranges.

Approaching the face. Photo: Dane Steadman

“First, while the Karakoram doesn’t receive as much of the Indian monsoon as the main Himalaya, it is still a monsoonal climate, and I think (and have heard from many locals now) that the weather tends to be much better in the fall than in the summer,” he explained.

“Second, we were concerned about rockfall and generally unsafe conditions on the face, being relatively low and lacking in buttresses. We thought the colder temperatures would make things safer and increase the odds of finding good ice.

“Third, Grant was able to get time off in October more easily than earlier in September. In a perfect world, we probably would have gone one to two weeks earlier, but we worked with what we had.”

Asked about other expeditions planning to visit the Pakistani mountains in the future, Steadman suggests that October could be a good time for certain climbs, but that September is also great. “This year, the weather was much less stable in September-October than I’ve experienced on my previous trips. Things got much snowier than normal for that time of year, [according to locals], but I think the weather was still better than through most of the summer.”

The team planned a single alpine-style push in the purest style. However, when they started on the route, they encountered an impassable hanging serac. They had to retreat and decide whether to try a totally different line or go home.

The team decided to try a route to the right of the originally planned one. But how could they be sure they wouldn’t reach another dead end?

Steadman admitted it was difficult. “The line we ended up climbing was entirely hidden from view at our base camp and where we acclimatized,” he explained. “[It was] an east-facing gash on a north face, which we only saw from the northwest until we tried it. From all the views we had before the first attempt, the central line we first attempted appeared to be the only route that wouldn’t involve climbing huge walls of monolithic gneiss, as it followed ice streaks and sheets for a good amount of it, and only looked to have relatively short cruxes.

At the base, they discovered that a tiny but menacing serac hung over the center of the wall, threatening the lower half. (The upper half appeared protected by an overhanging rock wall above it.)

“So we shifted to a different line to the left, which looked much harder but possible, and planned to rejoin our intended line at mid-height, under the protection of the overhangs.

That attempt didn’t so much dead-end as things compounded, forcing a retreat. “We were tired even on the approach from non-stop efforts to get there and acclimatize. We had some equipment issues, were moving slowly, and most of all, the climbing was damn hard,” he said.

In the end, they chose to rappel down after a two-hour-plus lead climb on a pitch that hadn’t looked hard from below. Dusk was approaching; there was no bivy spot in sight, and they were only 300m up the 1,800m wall.

“However, from our position on that attempt, we were able to see the right-trending, east-facing gash on the right side of the face, which was totally hidden by monolithic rock walls from all our previous vantage points,” he said. “So, after bailing, we felt like the central line would require fresh bodies, minds, and a huge weather window, but the right-hand line would be much more feasible.”

The climbers rested for three days in base camp and then set off again. “We certainly felt that we could get shut down at any number of points, especially at the steep mushroom-choked chimney on the headwall below the junction with the northwest spur,” Steadman admitted. “But it seemed like there was a good chance we could do it, and, most importantly, we felt like we could bail from anywhere [if necessary].”

The summit ridge of Sosbun Brakk after many tough decisions. Photo: Dane Steadman

However, bailing would not be easy. “The lower part would involve rappelling off V-threads, he explained, and if they had to turn back below the headwall, they would need to rappel out right to an icefield.

Route topo: line of ascent in red, line of descent in yellow. Photo: Steadman/Stewart

The trio summited on their fifth day on the wall, at 4 pm. Days were now short, and they still had a long, uncertain descent ahead. The descent began on the line of ascent, but eventually deviated to new terrain.

“We descended the upper half of our route, almost entirely rappelling down the northwest spur, with a little bit of downclimbing/traversing),” Steadman said.

They then reached a point west of their ascent line, where the face was slightly smaller and more covered in permanent ice. “Then we rappelled straight down, diagonaling back left partway down to avoid some serac hazard.”

Descending in worsening weather. Photo: Dane Steadman

Dane Steadman and Cody Winckler of the U.S., together with Grant Stewart of Canada, completed the first ascent of the north face of Sosbun Brakk (6,414m) in Pakistan’s Karakoram last month. The peak had been climbed only once before, by a Japanese team in 1981, from a different, milder side. The new 1,800m route includes difficulties of M7, WI5.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8414. Natural Wonders: Crowley Lake Columns

It is low tide on Crowley Lake in Mono County, California. The water is still, the trout abundant, and one or two kayakers paddle about, enjoying the lack of tourists. But as you walk along the shore, something out of place appears: spiral pillars and arches that seem left over from some ancient civilization.

Crowley Lake is a man-made reservoir, administered by the Los Angeles Department of Water and Power. It was named after an important figure in the community, affectionately known as the Desert Padre. This beloved Catholic priest worked tirelessly to help a community that experienced water shortages throughout the 1920s and 1930s. Water from the nearby Owen Valley was diverted to Los Angeles, leaving very little for local residents.

Father Crowley made it his mission to bolster tourism in the area. The publicity led authorities to help locals by drawing up plans for a dam and reservoir. When Father Crowley died in a car accident, authorities completed the Long Valley Dam and named the reservoir Lake Crowley. If it weren’t for his intervention, the lake wouldn’t have existed.

As the years passed, visitors continued coming to Lake Crowley to fish, kayak, camp, and hike. Then something weird started to appear on the cliffsides. The banks appeared to be receding and hollowing out, revealing hundreds upon hundreds of spiral columns. Many onlookers believed they were man-made. Some compared the formations to Moorish architecture from North Africa and Spain.

Though the reservoir is man-made, the area’s history dates back millennia. Crowley Lake lies in the wider Long Valley Caldera, one of Earth’s largest calderas.

The caldera sits atop one of three ancient supervolcanoes in the United States. Around 760,000 years ago, this supervolcano erupted and spewed 650 cubic kilometers of rhyolite rock and ash into the air for a week. It covered the land in a blanket of ash and debris over one kilometer thick. And it left behind a 32km long, 18km wide, and 900m deep depression in the earth.

The depression formed when the subterranean magma chamber emptied onto the surface and the remnant shell of a roof collapsed. The blast and pyroclastic flow were so powerful that ash fell as far as Kansas. Along the rim are the lava dome complexes of Mammoth, Glass, and Bald Mountains. The caldera also hosts a cluster of hydrothermally active lakes called the Mammoth Lakes.

The main feature left by the eruption is the Bishop Tuff, a few square kilometers in area. A tuff is a rock of compacted volcanic ash. At the base of this tuff stand the Crowley Lake Columns. They range in thickness, average about six meters high, and are generally reddish, beige, grey, or white with a dark water line at their base. Some lean while others stand straight.

Attached by arches, the columns are so evenly spaced that to some visitors, it resembles an ancient temple. Horizontal cracks about 30cm apart add further detail to the columns. They look like they hold up the cliffs but they simply remained after the softer cliff rock started to erode.

The high levels of silica, sodium, and potassium in the columns make the rock conduct heat well and cool suddenly. This led to the cracks. Similar formations exist in Guatemala, Mexico, and New Mexico.

The few people who frequent Crowley Lake have long known about the columns. Only recently, social media has started to highlight them. This is due to its seclusion on the lake’s shoreline. The only ways to access them are on foot or by boat, kayak, or 4×4. The dirt road on which you turn from Highway 395 is so steep and uneven that it can bog down vehicle. Visitors tend to drive halfway, then walk.

Despite popular speculation that these columns are an example of columnar jointing, they are not. The geological process is different. With columnar jointing, the spaces are not so far apart. A study by geologists at UC Berkeley used a combination of computer models, X-ray diffraction, and electron microscope imagery to determine how they formed. It turns out that the culprit is simply…water.

“We hypothesize that the columns are a result of instabilities at the liquid water and steam interface as cold water seeped into the still-cooling Bishop Tuff,” says Noah Randolph-Flagg, the study’s lead scientist.

The columns formed through hydrothermal convection. Photo: Shutterstock

Essentially, rainwater seeped down into the hot ash and started to boil, producing a mineral glue called mordonite. The boiling water formed a column of steam which carried the mordonite upward through the rock. Thus, the mineral hardened the rock, leading it to remain standing while the surrounding rock eroded. This process is called hydrothermal convection or circulation.

According to Randolph-Flagg, it only took 100 years to form these columns. The study also states that “the liquid water–steam interface probably is the source of the instability that generates columnar flow.”

As for the erosion of the cliffside, the reservoir’s water levels fluctuate throughout the year with the seasons. After the completion of the reservoir, the columns started to reveal themselves. The constant rise and fall as well as a repeating freeze-thaw cycle caused the tuff to recede. It revealed the columns and also hollowed out the cliffside to form shallow caves. Take care when going into these little caves or pushing on the columns, as they might not be as stable. As the cliff continues to erode, we should see more of these columns.

The arches resemble Moorish architecture. Photo: Shutterstock

In terms of the Long Valley Caldera, scientists have closely monitored its activity, which has been increasing since the 1980s. Since it is a one of only 20 supervolcanoes in the world, they have paid close attention to its potential for eruption, despite its status as inactive.

There have been small earthquakes and signs of movement in the earth as if it were “breathing.” This is seen in Yellowstone, another supervolcano. While there are no immediate signs of an impending eruption, the caldera’s history and current activity suggest that it is possible.

Kristine De Abreu is a writer at ExplorersWeb.

Kristine has been writing about Science, Mysteries and History for 4+ years. Prior to that, Kristine studied at the University of Leicester in the UK.

Based in Port-of-Spain, Kristine is also a literature teacher, avid reader, hiker, occasional photographer, an animal lover and shameless ramen addict.

#8412. A Bear to Be Thankful For: I Hunted a Record Black Bear on Thanksgiving Day

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 1:46 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

The 1966 deer season on the Keweenaw Peninsula, northern Michigan’s Copper Country, opened on November 12 and was due to close on the 27th, the Sunday after Thanksgiving.

Up to Thanksgiving day, our whole family had no luck. My dad, my brother Bruce, and I had hunted hard and had seen deer almost every day, but nothing that we wanted to shoot. Even my mom, who goes along with us on our deer hunts but makes no pretense of hunting hard, had seen deer but had no shooting.

The only family success scored was by my dad’s uncle, Bill Anderson, from Detroit. He drove the 550 miles north to hunt with us, and went home with a good buck.

I had special reasons for feeling disappointed about drawing a blank. I was born and grew up in the Copper Country. I’m now 24, and I’ve hunted deer since I was 14. That year my great-uncle Bill sold me his old rifle, a .32 Special Winchester Model 94. That old gun had killed a lot of deer and a few bears before I got it, and it went right on being lucky for me.

From that fall of 1956 until 1965, I killed a deer with it every year. Then circumstances compelled me to miss the 1965 season. I went into the Navy Reserve three years ago, and was called to active duty in the spring of 1965. When November of 1966 rolled around, I arranged for a leave to coincide with the deer season in the Upper Peninsula of Michigan, and so I was back again, hunting the woods and swamps where I had been lucky ever since I was a kid, but not doing so well for a change.

To make matters worse, I had to leave for the naval base at Norfolk, Virginia on  Saturday morning, November 26. I knew, when we left home right after breakfast on Thanksgiving day, that I had just that day and the next in which to kill a deer, or I’d wind up skunked for the first time in my life.

We’re a Finnish family, all hunters and fishermen. My dad, Ernest Haataja, runs a refrigeration business in the village of Ahmeek, about 20 miles up the Keweenaw Peninsula from Houghton, the biggest city in the Copper Country. The peninsula is wild and rugged, and there are not too many roads. It’s a pretty fair game area, with deer, bears, coyotes, and bobcats.

The Keweenaw juts northeast into Lake Superior like a rough and rocky finger, 50 miles long and 15 to 20 wide. In the small bays along the eastern shore, sheltered from the high winds and pounding seas of the great lake, there is excellent fishing, mostly for lake trout. The whole Haataja family loves to hunt and fish.

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

My brother Bruce is two years older than I am. He’s married, has four youngsters, works at a local mine, and does some beaver trapping as a sideline. We have three sisters: Vicki, 18; Carolyn, 10; and Mary Beth, 6. Pop, mom, and sister Mary Beth with the bear in Ahmeek, Mich.

One of our favorite summer vacations for years has been to charter a small commercial fishing boat for a week or so and make the 50-mile trip on Lake Superior to Isle Royale National Park, the big, roadless island that lies 20 miles south of the Canadian shore. The fishing for lake trout there is as good as any we know about, and the sea lampreys have made lighter inroads than they have along the south shore of Superior. We have even seen lakers rising for flies in July and August around Gull Rocks, a few miles off the main island.

There is great pike fishing in some of the 30-odd inland lakes on Isle Royale, too, but we don’t care too much for pike and don’t often go after them. We live aboard our chartered boat, cooking our meals, sleeping on cots, and moving around as we choose. Isle Royale is 50 miles long, and there are plenty of sheltered harbors to anchor in. We hike, explore, and fish, and everybody has a fine time.

Bruce and I have had some other very good fishing trips together, too, including one to Lake Nipigon a few years ago that was a real adventure.

The bear didn’t move a muscle. He just kept his eyes riveted on me as though trying to figure out what I was. Or maybe he knew, and tried to stare me down. Except for his head and round ears, he looked like a low-slung black bull, and he was so big he stunned me.

The family also has a cottage on Little Traverse Bay, about 15 miles from home, that we use for fishing headquarters in the summer and hunting in the fall. And from the time Rabbit Bay, nearby, freezes over until it breaks up in the spring, dad spends a fair share of his time fishing through the ice for lake trout. Fishermen are still catching trout out there with live lampreys attached to them, but some heavy stocking has been done, and the fishing continues to be fair.

Until I left home to go into the service, I never missed a chance to go along on those winter trips, and Bruce still goes as often as he can. Dad has built a set of light runners for a small boat, and if a hard wind is blowing offshore and there is danger that the ice may break loose and drift out into the open water of Keweenaw Bay, they take that outfit along. It’s so light that one or two men, going onto the ice on foot the way we do, can drag it easily. In an emergency, the runners can be taken off quickly, and the boat is ready for use in the water.

I’m telling all this to help you understand how I felt about getting shut out on that 1966 deer hunt. I didn’t have any very good alibi, either. The weather was cold and clear, and hunting conditions were not bad although we had only a little snow-hardly enough for tracking_ The Keweenaw Peninsula sometimes gets as much as 20 feet of snow before winter is over. We had 234 inches by the end of March this year. But I had killed deer on bare ground plenty of times before, and thought I should be able to do it again.

Deer, however, are not so plentiful there as they were a few years ago. Many local people blame it on the opening of doe seasons, under special permit, by the Michigan conservation department. Whether or not that is the reason, we don’t see nearly so many deer as we used to, and we don’t see horns so often. But I knew that if I had to go back to Norfolk without filling my license, all that would be pretty cold comfort.

Thanksgiving morning dawned clear and cold with hardly any wind. The leaves would be dry and noisy underfoot, but it promised to be a fairly good day for hunting. We had been putting in most of our time in an area around Mud Lake, not too far from our cottage, but that morning we decided to try another location. We planned to drive out beyond Fort Wilkins State Park almost to the end of the peninsula. That’s as far north as you can go without getting your feet wet in Lake Superior.

Bruce drove our four-wheel-drive vehicle, and I rode up front with him. Dad and mom were in the back seat. Mom had packed lunch for the four of us. We’d stay in the woods until night. We usually start our deer hunting before daylight and hunt until dark.

Beyond Fort Wilkins we took a muddy logging road and then turned back toward Hoar Lake. A second logging road took us into remote, wild country miles from anywhere and near enough to the Superior beach so that we could faintly hear the noise of surf. We knew the place was a good area for deer, and even on Thanksgiving Day, there wasn’t much chance other hunters would find their way so far into back country.

On foot, we skirted a thick cedar swamp that lay between us and Superior, and we split up. Mom stayed behind. She found a spot where she could keep an eye on a runway along the edge of the swamp and announced that she thought she’d have as much chance of killing a deer there as anyplace.

Knowing her habits, we suspected that she’d watch the runway for an hour or so, work around the end of the swamp, hike down to the beach, and look for agates. That was about what she did, too, but the swamp proved too much for her and for once she turned back and joined us for lunch without sighting Lake Superior.

Bruce and dad headed down into the swamp. I turned to the right, into big, fairly open hardwood timber. There were high ridges running all through it with quite a lot of oak on the slopes. That meant acorns, and they are likely to mean deer.

The day had stayed bright and crisp. The ground was bare, and the leaves were really noisy underfoot, so I moved slowly, walking a few steps and then stopping to look in all directions. stepped carefully from one tree to another, trying to be as quiet as a cat and hoping I’d see a deer before me.

I found a little deer sign, but not so much as I had expected, and I was beginning to wonder whether I had picked the right place, after all. Then I cam over a rise, and a strange bare spot just ahead caught my eyes.

All around me in the timber, dead leaves lay in a thick carpet, but in front of me was a bare patch some 75 feet square. ln all the time I had spent in the woods I had never seen anything like that, and I stood there looking it over carefully, trying to puzzle it out. It seemed as though a huge vacuum cleaner had been at work.

Then, on the downhill side of the bare place, I saw a big mound of heaped-up leaves, and squatting in them was a black bear bigger than any I had ever expected to see. He saw me in the same instant, and we stood and stared at each other.

Read Next: Revolver Versus Semi-Auto: Which Is Best for Bear Defense?

My eyes must have bugged all the way out of my head. The bear was less than 75 feet from me in open timber, not hidden by anything except his own leaf pile, and that fell far short of concealing him. Why I hadn’t seen him in the first instant after I came over the rise, I still don’t know, but I guess I just wasn’t looking for a bear. It was that bald spot, raked clear of leaves, that caught my attention and held it those first few seconds.

The bear didn’t move a muscle. He just kept his eyes riveted on me as though trying to figure out what I was. Or maybe he knew, and tried to stare me down. Except for his head and round ears, he looked like a low-slung black bull, and he was so big he stunned me.

For a moment I didn’t know whether to shoot or try to sneak away, but I made up my mind on that score in a hurry. I had seen bears in the woods once or twice during previous hunting seasons, but nothing like this one. I had never expected to have such luck, and I wasn’t going to pass it up.

I don’t know how long we looked at each other. It was probably only 10 or 15 seconds, but it seemed like a full minute. I’ll say one thing: I didn’t get buck fever.

I didn’t move a muscle either, until I raised my gun. The old rifle was not scoped, but at that short range, I didn’t need a scope. I brought my rifle up slowly and took careful aim. The bear standing partly sideways to me, I clouted him just below his left ear. The 170-grain softpoint knocked him as flat as a flapjack. He didn’t kick much, but he was an awful lot of bear, and he looked even bigger lying on the ground than when he was on his feet. So I walked close to him and put another shot into his shoulder to make sure.

Then I started looking things over. The bear had raked all the leaves downhill into one big pile, and only eight feet farther down the slope, I found a hole dug under a big cedar windfall. It was about four feet across and three feet deep and seemed freshly dug. If it had been used before, he had enlarged it and cleaned it out. Sand and gravel had been thrown in every direction.

Without much question, the bear had intended to use the pile of leaves for his winter bed. If I hadn’t come along, he probably would have dragged enough of them into the hole to line it before crawling in, raking the rest over himself, and curling up until spring. He would have had dry, snug quarters. The slope of the hill provided good drainage, and the windfall would have kept the worst of the snow off him. The leaves he had gathered would have been enough to cover three or four bears.

He was a lot too big for me to dress without help, so I headed back to the car. Dad and Bruce were there when I arrived, and mom came along a little later. They had heard my two shots, and their first question was whether I had killed a deer. I said no, and tried to string them along, but I guess my excitement must have shown.

“Well,” Bruce said right away, “maybe you didn’t get your buck, but from the way you look you sure got something. What was it?”

“It was a bear,” I blurted. “It’s the biggest bear I’ve ever seen. It’s so darned big it looks like a grizzly!”

“Deader than a salt herring,” I assured him.

We hurried through chow, and then started back to the bear. When we got there, it was all the three of us could do to roll him over for dressing.

As we worked, we debated whether we could drag him out to the nearest logging road. It didn’t seem very likely, for we had almost three miles to go on bare ground. It was plain that this bear was a huge thing for three men to handle, but we agreed to try.

That turned out to be a big joke. We took hold of his head and forefeet, but we found that by tugging and grunting for all we were worth we could move him only a few inches at a time. We couldn’t have dragged him out of the woods in a month of Sundays.

I hated to leave him, but it was getting late in the afternoon, and there was no choice. We were sure no animal would molest him, and the area was remote enough so that there was very little chance other hunters would find him. Even if they did, he was so big that nobody would make off with him. We hiked out to the car, laying plans to retrieve my bear in the morning.

We did some phoning that night and early the next day and learned that a logging outfit, the Kilpela Brothers from the town of Mohawk, was operating not far from where I had killed the bear. We contacted them, and to our great relief they said they’d hitch a dray behind one of their tractors and go in with us and bring him out. It was after dark before the job was done.

I had taken a fine trophy. My bear was not only a record-setter for size, but also he had a thick, shiny, black pelt in perfect condition. There wasn’t a rubbed spot or blemish on it anywhere. It made as beautiful a rug as any hunter ever took. There wasn’t even one claw missing from the feet. That’s probably because there is no bear trapping in Michigan anymore. If there had been traps in the woods, he would almost certainly have blundered into one during his long life.

We took him to Rinne’s Fuel Company in Ahmeek and weighed him on certified scales there in the presence of witnesses. We were all a little surprised at what the scales revealed. I had killed a black bear that weighed 570 pounds field dressed, the heaviest ever recorded in Michigan. He was 15 pounds over the previous state record holder. That one was shot in the 1950’s near Newberry farther east on the Upper Peninsula by Dean Loveless, a bowhunter from the downstate city of Hamtramck.

Michigan game men estimated that my bear had weighed not less than 650 pounds alive. His dressed weight was 82 pounds below that of the giant black killed in Wisconsin in 1963 by Otto Hedbany of suburban Milwaukee. That one dressed 652, and its live weight must have been around 725. Hedbany’s bear was described in “Biggest Black Ever Killed,” OUTDOOR LIFE, May 1964.

Although I shot mine in the head, I did not damage the skull. My shot went in at the butt of an ear, and the bullet from the .32 Special opened up enough to kill him in his tracks. But it lodged somewhere in the brain cavity and did not come out. As a result, the skull was intact for measuring. I intend to enter it in the current Boone and Crockett Club competition, and the state game men tell me there is very little doubt that it will go into the record book, maybe not too far from the top. The Hedbany bear, by the way, was not eligible for entry, because the skull was shattered when it was killed.

A photographer for the Houghton Mining Gazette came to Ahmeek that night, Friday, and took a picture of me with my bear. That was the only one I got of myself with the bear. It was too dark that night for us to use our camera, and I boarded a plane to go back to Norfolk early Saturday, so there was no time to think about more pictures. There were plenty taken after I left, but I wasn’t in them. That’s not too unusual for me. I’m the one in our family who takes the pictures most of the time, so I don’t appear in many.

I’ve promised myself one thing: If I should ever take another bear as good as that one, I’ll get some color pictures of myself with it, just to remind myself of my luck.

After we had gotten my bear into town and had weighed him, I had a chance to think the whole thing over. I couldn’t help feeling almost sorry I had shot him, but if I were doing it over again, I’d do the same thing. He was too much trophy to pass up. and I’m sure I’ll never in my life see another bear like him. Yet I did realize how long he had lived there in that wild country. He wasn’t doing any harm, and it seemed almost too bad that I came along that Thanksgiving morning when he was making his winter bed. He was my first bear, and much as I like hunting, if he should also turn out to be my last, I don’t believe I’d be disappointed.

On the other hand, shooting him was the most exciting thing that ever happened to me. I went back to the naval base without getting a deer, but I didn’t mind. That bear was all the Thanksgiving present any hunter could ask for.

This story, “Bear to Be Thankful For,” appeared in the Sept. 1967 issue of Outdoor Life. The current Michigan state-record black bear, according to Boone and Crockett Club, is bear whose skull measured 22 11/16 inches and was killed in Newaygo County in 2009 by Donald R. Corrigan. Haataja’s bear is not listed in the B&C records.
