#8410. Everest Winds Force Climbers to Retreat

Despite weather forecasts of high winds on Everest, the Russian team gamely left for the West Shoulder today. But in the end, they couldn’t fight the gale-force winds, which eventually forced them back to their tent at the base of the mountain.

“It’s calm right now, but the forecast is worrying,” leader Andrei Vasiliev wrote yesterday from a snow cave, where Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and Vasiliev had sheltered for the night at 6,400m. Late today, they confirmed that the weather forecast of powerful winds was unfortunately right.

The three climbers had fixed three pitches above them, according to Mountain Club, and were hoping for firmer terrain as they approached the West Shoulder of Everest, en route to the West Ridge.

However, as we previously reported after consulting meteorologist Vitor Baia of Portugal, conditions were good Tuesday and Wednesday, but winds rose dramatically today.

On a previous foray up Everest, one of the climbers heads for the West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

“The winds are very strong,” Vasiliev reported. “It is impossible to work higher on the mountain.”

Vasiliev said that his team is retreating to a tent at 5,600m. This is below the Lho La Pass, possibly on the Khumbu Glacier beside the vertical section leading to that col.

The question is whether the team will wait there for better conditions, retreat to their base in Lobuche, or call off the expedition.

Some charts still show a chance of variable winds tomorrow, before the jet stream hits the mountain with full force (see below).

Wind levels at around summit altitude on Everest this week, by meteoexploration.com

Baia says the winds will remain high every day until next Tuesday. From Wednesday on, the winds should be weaker.

The Russians set off on their third trip up Everest last Monday, hoping to summit via the West Ridge without oxygen or external support, and with no one else on the mountain.

On Tuesday evening, they checked in with Mountain Club from their tent at 6,000m, where they spent the night. The hard trailbreaking through fresh snow had slowed their pace, but they expected better conditions on the following day, as they started up steeper terrain toward Everest’s shoulder. They knew the snow up there was harder and compacted. They could even see their trails from the previous trip up, so no new soft snow had accumulated.

The Russian team on Everest’s West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

Yesterday, Vasiliev noted that they had climbed four pitches on old fixed ropes, plus another eight pitches of their own. The team is fixing ropes at the hardest sections, but not entirely along the route. As expected, snow conditions had improved with the more vertical terrain, but as Vasiliev pointed out, “it’s not exactly easy walking yet.”

The Russian leader insists the route to the summit is doable. However, wind delays and a lack of time may ultimately end their attempt.

#8407. Piolet d'Or Team Shares Insider Tips for Climbing in Peru's Huayhuash Range

Marc Toralles, Ruben Sanmartin, and Bru Busom of Spain were awarded a Piolet d’Or for a highly committing new route on Yerupaja Grande (6,634m), the highest peak of Peru’s Huayhuash range. ExplorersWeb asked the young climbers about the ascent and the unique character of the Andes. They offered some tips for climbers looking to add Peru to their list of high-altitude destinations.

In the last few years, the Andes have reportedly been affected by climate change. “The main effect is that the huge snow structures on the summits are exposed to warmer temperatures and become more fragile,” Sanmartin said. “Basically, they fall down more easily.”

“That means that climbers need to pick their route carefully [when judging] what lingers above them,” Buson explained.

Bru Buson, left, and Marc Toralles. Photo: Ruben Sanmartin

This was especially noticeable last year on Siula Grande. “There was a snowfield midway up the face as we trekked by, and on our way back, it was not there anymore,” said Marc Toralles. “Somehow, the melting process in the Andes, with its 5,000m and even 6,000m [altitudes], is similar to what we are seeing every year in the Alps, where we all work as mountain guides.”

For Toralles, the best thing in the Andes is how accessible the mountains are. “It’s so easy to go climbing there,” he said.

There are no lifts or paved roads, but you can go, climb a peak, and return in three or four days. Something like that in the Himalaya would take weeks. All you need is to get to Huaraz by plane, bus, or car from Lima, where you may stay some days to acclimatize (there are many options), and then go to the mountains.

If your planned peak is not in the right conditions, you will surely find some other one to climb. Also, the options are infinite for ice climbing, alpine climbing, rock, trekking, anything.

The team’s base camp. Photo: Spanish Yerupaja Grande team

Red tape is minimal. There are no permits for peaks. Instead, climbers and trekkers pay a small fee to cross the comunidades (the areas) where they want to climb, and climbers can hire arrieros (riders with mules) to help carry equipment and supplies to base camps or higher. On this occasion, the climbers kept their logistics basic, with little beyond a slightly larger base camp tent.

With some of the more accessible places, it is advisable to have someone watching over your base camp while you are away climbing.

Once you leave the beaten track, you’ll be alone. “There are no crowds in Huayhuash,” Buson said. Why aren’t they more popular? The climbers explained that Huayhuash peaks are typically more difficult than popular options in the Cordillera Blanca, also in Peru. But they note this lack of popularity is changing fast. “We are seeing much more people in recent years, both climbing and trekking,” they said.

Thanks to the many possibilities, the three Spanish climbers didn’t need to do much planning. “We know Huayhuash pretty well, we are familiar with the terrain and the climbing style, and we are aware there are always options,” said Toralles, who has climbed there four times.

That said, the line the Spaniards climbed on Yerupaja last year was not improvised. They had that route in their mind for some time. “We heard of someone who had tried but called the attempt off, so the route was there to be climbed,” Toralles said. “In addition, it didn’t require a long time, and the timing worked for me, since I was traveling to Peru anyway to do some guiding.”

Buson and Sanmartin joined immediately. To acclimatize, they had worked double shifts the previous weeks around Chamonix, where both are professional guides. “Working on the Mont Blanc massif intensively was great training and acclimatization,” they said. “We were ready by the time we set foot on the mountain.”

The best time to climb in Huayhuash is July, August, and the first half of September.

Once on the mountain, they carefully checked the conditions of the face and the line they would follow. “In the Andes, you have to assume that the summit ridge snow formations, the famous ‘mushrooms,’ the big cornices, and the seracs on the sides of the face are mostly in a delicate state. You need to assume that you will progress on deep, unstable snow, and the entire structure may have deteriorated and be prone to crumble,” Buson noted.

To deal with them, the climbers used some custom-made tools: They attached alitas (little wings) to their ice axes for a better grip on foamy, loose snow. They also carried small snowshoes for some sections.

Ice axe with ‘alitas’ (wings). Photo: Nick Sheldon/Mountain Project

Conditions in the Andean mountains are always variable. “You’ll find different terrain depending on the altitude and the orientation,” Sanmartin added. “This is why technical routes on these peaks require a high level of commitment, which, as happened on our route, may be even higher than the intrinsic difficulty of the pitches,” Toralles explained.

With this in mind, the Spanish team named the route they opened Essence of Commitment.

Marc Toralles ready for the ridge, with large snow pickets and an ice axe with ‘wings’ attached. Photo: Buson/Sanmartin

In this sort of terrain, it is paramount to stay focused and make the right call between subjective and objective factors. “There are countless reasons to turn around on climbs like this, but one must keep going, except for obvious reasons, such as an injury, a completely impassable section, or a really violent storm,” Sanmartin said.

“That is why it is important to consider all options and potential issues before starting the climb. You need to be sure you like the line and want to climb it.”

Asked whether they reached a point of no return during their climb, Buson admitted they did. “On reaching the ridge, we became aware that trying to downclimb the same route would be quite complex. However, we also saw the route upward was doable.”

The Spaniards did not count on a professional meteorologist. “We had our friends texting us the online, free forecasts over InReach,” they said. “One of the positive aspects of Huayhuash is that good weather periods tend to last for quite a while. Weather windows of five to six days are common.” During their climb, for example, they enjoyed acceptable weather, with some rain and snow but no high winds.

They warn that conditions in the Andes are changing quickly. “You need to observe the mountains and adapt,” Toralles said. “Some people aim for a route based on the information in a guidebook printed 50 years ago, and find a totally different scenario.” He pointed out that Siula Grande looked different in 2025 than it did when he climbed it just three years earlier.

Sanmartin noted that conditions have also changed on normal routes. They are now drier, more complex, and often more hazardous. “In some cases, climbers are abandoning the normal routes up the glaciers and opt for ridges which may be more difficult but in better conditions,” he said.

The climbers think the Andes will not get as crowded as the Himalaya, simply because the logistics are not as advanced — no refuges, no helicopters — and because they don’t have the sizzle of being the tallest peaks on Earth. “But they have incredible potential,” they said.

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8406. Townsend Whelen’s Favorite Big Game Hunting Cartridges

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 23, 2026 3:23 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

Townsend Whelen, who was one of Outdoor Life’s first shooting editors and one of the founders of the legendary gun maker and outfitter Griffin & Howe, wrote extensively about big game hunting from the early 1900s until his death in 1961. His articles on rifles, ballistics, cartridges, optics, and marksmanship have endured through the decades, thanks to his concise, informative prose that was based on years of practical experience as a hunter, competitive shooter, and career army officer.

He opined at length on the best cartridges for big game hunting, though he approached the topic with a characteristic open-mindedness. He was willing to consider and try new rounds as they became available, and loved to test out new equipment in the field and report on their results.

I’ve unearthed Whelen’s favorite hunting cartridges by reading through his archived stories and books. One way to think of this list is as a snapshot of some key milestones in ballistic history, as well as an over-the-shoulder peek at the hunting rounds Whelen viewed as state of the art over his decades-long career. Many of his observations embody timeless wisdom and are still relevant today.

You’d think that among the cartridges he touted the .35 Whelen, which bears his name, would be included. After all, taking the .30/06 case—which was technologically advanced for the time—and necking it up for hard-hitting .358-caliber bullets was a winning combo.

For not much increased recoil the .35 Whelen launched bullets from 180- to 250-grains and quickly gained a reputation as a solid big-game killer. (Most .35 Whelen loads feature one of four bullet weights: 180, 200, 225 and 250 grains).

Whelen mentioned the round in passing—notably to give credit to James Howe (of Griffin & Howe) for designing it—and briefly touched upon others’ accounts of it in the field. But if he ever hunted with it, or even shot it much, he didn’t document the experience. As far as I’ve been able to learn, he never messed with the round in any meaningful way—other than doing some hand loading for it—and its exposure to the general reading public was through other writers of the time like Elmer Keith, who hunted with it extensively and was a fan of the 250-grain spitzers.

READ NEXT: Two Rifles for the Ages, Chambered in .35 Whelen

This blind spot to his eponymous cartridge extended to the .400 and .375 Whelens, which Howe developed at the same time, though they never caught on the way the .35 did. If I had to guess, I would say that Whelen’s modesty, refined upbringing (he came from Philadelphia aristocracy), and soldierly sense of decorum precluded him from overtly praising the cartridge, rather than the existence of some undisclosed antipathy toward it. To ballyhoo something bearing his name would be unseemly.

The list of cartridges Whelen recommended for big game evolved over the years. His career spanned an era of explosive growth in cartridge and bullet development as well as the adoption of such marvels as telescopic sights.

For this reason, his earlier writings (from 1906 to the mid 1920s) prominently featured cartridges like the .30/40 Krag (aka the .30 Army), the .30/30 W.C.F. (aka .30/30 Win.), 7mm Mauser, .250 Savage, and their peers. Later on, he became a fan of rounds such as the .270 Win., .257 Roberts, and .348 Winchester. Throughout his long career, however, he never wavered in his praise for the .30/06 and the .375 H&H (with some qualifiers) when the topic of big-game hunting turned to cartridge selection.

Whelen’s career as an authority on hunting and shooting marched in lockstep with the creation of the .30/06 and its rise to prominence. He was intimately familiar with the round and could rattle off technical details in his sleep concerning its history, refinements, the pros and cons of the numerous available loads, and its qualities on the battlefield and for hunting.

He worked with the cartridge both in his capacity as a sportsman and as an army officer—particularly after World War I when he transferred from Infantry to Ordnance, eventually becoming the commanding officer at Frankfort Arsenal and the director of research and development at the Springfield Armory.

He hunted extensively with the .30/06, killing all manner of big game during his many adventures. One of his preferred projectiles was Western Cartridge Company’s 180-grain open point boat tail bullet, with which he killed a grizzly bear, three moose, two caribou, two bighorn sheep, four mountain goats, and six deer.

He documented his experience with this bullet in particular because of occasional reports of 180-grain bullets failing on game. He wasn’t buying it.

“There has never been even a suspicion of a failure on any of the game I have shot with it,” he wrote. “Many other sportsmen have had a similar experience.”

He found the bullet delivered good accuracy as well. “In my own rifle it was averaging about 2-inch groups at 100 and 4 inches at 200, and I got one moose killed clean at 325 yards and two goats killed likewise clean at 325 and 350 yards,” he said.

On bigger game, many hunters gravitated toward 220- and 225-grain bullets back then, and the relative killing power of those bullets when compared to the wimpy 180s was a hot topic of discussion.

Whelen gives the heavier bullets their due, in particular the 220-grain soft points: “This bullet has made a very enviable reputation for fine killing power on larger game, and particularly on moose and Alaskan brown bear.”

But when it came to terminal ballistics, a topic he was better versed in than most, he didn’t go along with the accepted wisdom that bigger meant better.

“It is generally supposed that the 220- and 225-grain bullets are more reliable killers on our larger game than those of 180 grains. I am not so sure of this,” he said. “I would put them about on a par with the best of the 180-grain bullets up to 200 yards. Beyond that distance the trajectory of the 220 grain bullets becomes too curved to surely hit a deer or animals of that size.”

In his opinion the effectiveness of those heavy bullets topped out around 225 yards, while the 180s and 172s could hit accurately and kill game to 350 yards.

If he sounded like he held strong opinions on the .30/06 that’s because he did. He never hesitated to discuss—and defend—its effectiveness on game, but he also didn’t hold back critiques either.

For instance, he took ammunition manufacturers to task in the 1930s for allowing the general quality of .30/06 ammo to slip. He had three “fine scope sighted rifles of Springfield, Winchester, and Remington makes” in his collection and gathered data that showed accuracy had fallen off from an average of 1 ¾ to 2 inches at 100 yards to 3-inch groups, while at 200 yards ammo that used to print 3 ½- to 4-inch groups now couldn’t shoot better than 7 inches.

He felt ammo makers were neglecting the round in favor of newer offerings that were in the limelight and used the power of his platform at Outdoor Life and other publications to chastise those companies.

“With such ammunition a fine .30/06 rifle is no longer a 350-yard rifle, but rather a 225-yard arm for sure hits,” he said.

The reason for his bluntness was that he hated the idea of the .30/06—which he said is “never a mistake”—not living up to its potential as our best all-around big game cartridge.

Not many deer hunters today would list the .250 Savage among their top picks for whitetails—assuming they’re even aware of the cartridge’s existence. A lot of them would dismiss it as underpowered, though that would have come as a shock to Whelen. I guess whitetails have become more bulletproof since his day.

But when ballistic visionary Charles Newton created the .250-3000 in 1915 for the Savage 99 lever action, he gave American sportsmen their first high-power round capable of pushing a bullet at 3000 fps.

To be clear, Newton favored 100-grain bullets in the round with a muzzle velocity of 2800 fps, but Savage wanted to break the 3000-fps barrier and loaded it with 87-grainers which would do just that.

A lot of hunters jumped on the .250 Savage bandwagon and quickly demonstrated its worth in the woods.

“It has proved to be a very good deer cartridge,” Whelen wrote. Adding, “from hundreds of reports from the game fields it is quite clear that it gives a much larger percentage of instantaneous kills on deer with the first shot than does the .30/30 cartridge.”

Whelen shot the .250 with 87- and 100-grain bullets, developing handloads for both. He favored the lighter bullet for small game, and the 100-grainers for varmints and deer.

When he first recommended the .250 Savage for deer, the .257 Roberts hadn’t been developed yet. By his own admission in later years, he felt anything the Savage could do the Roberts could do better.

“I think that a well-informed rifleman would hardly choose this cartridge because the .257 Roberts cartridge slightly excels it in every respect,” he said.

I’m taking a bit of liberty with this one, as when Whelen wrote about mule deer earlier in his career he advocated the .30/30 W.C.F. as a minimum acceptable cartridge and, as he often did, defaulted to the .30/40 Krag with a 220-grain bullet as optimal. That said, he did throw in a qualifier that any cartridges for other Western game would serve well on muleys.

After the .270 Win. came on the scene, Whelen became a huge fan, calling it “one of the finest cartridges we have for American big game shooting—a very remarkable load.”

He extolled the killing power and flat shooting characteristics of Winchester’s 130-grain spitzer bullet and described how a moose he shot with it “was killed more instantaneously than I have ever seen a moose killed before or since.”

His Winchester Model 54 was the most accurate big game rifle under 10 pounds he owned—capable of printing 10-shot groups at 100 yards measuring 1 7/8 inches, and 4-inch groups at 200. Relative to harder-kicking cartridges like the .30/06, the soft-shooting .270 lent itself to “fine practical accuracy.”

As much as we link Jack O’Connor and the .270 Win., there’s little doubt as to Whelen’s perspective: “I think it is our best American big game cartridge in the hands of a rifleman who can avail himself of its flat trajectory and fine accuracy.”

When Whelen joined the Army in the late 1890s prior to the Spanish American War, the .30/40 Krag (aka the .30 Army and .30 US) was the standard issue round. It didn’t serve in that role long. It was adopted in 1892, replacing the .45/70 and then was ousted by the .30/03 (which later became the .30/06) in 1903.

But like most cartridges employed by the military it became a hit with the sporting public, and as one of our first domestic smokeless powder rounds hunters took a shine to it. It shot a metal jacketed round nose 220-grain bullet at 2000 fps from a 30-inch barrel, which was hot stuff back then. In a carbine configuration with a 22-inch barrel, the velocity dropped to 1960 fps, which was still impressive for the time.

In 1899, a hunter killed the world-record Rocky Mountain elk with a .30/40 Krag, which cemented its reputation as good medicine for wapiti.

Whelen liked the .30/40 Krag for elk, too. When he expressed this opinion in his book, American Big Game Shooting, he felt it was the minimum one should attempt to hunt elk with and made a point of emphasizing the use of the 220-grain bullet rather than some of the lighter projectiles available.

That said, in 1923 he gushed about the capabilities of the .30/40 Krag with a 180-grain open point bullet, declaring it “the most useful and all around successful cartridge ever made for the American sportsman.”

This round is all but forgotten today, having been consigned to the ballistic graveyard in 1936 when Winchester ditched it, along with the Model 1895 rifle that it was chambered in.

But from 1903, when it was developed, through the Roaring 20s the .35 Winchester had a respectable run as a big-game round that fell in between the .30/30 and the .405 Winchester, both of which are still produced today.

Like the .30/30 and .405, the .35 W.C.F. is a rimmed bottle-neck cartridge that shoots soft-pointed lead bullets from lever actions. Launching a 249-grain lead cast bullet at 1,500 fps would be plenty potent for moose, which is why Whelen mentioned this cartridge in American Big Game Shooting.

Of course, being a magnanimous sort, Whelen also lauded the .30/06, the .405, and later on cartridges like the .270 Win., as good options for moose hunters.

Nowhere does Whelen reveal his level-headed style of analysis more than when discussing hunting large bears. He loathed exaggeration as much as he loathed excessive recoil, so when discussing the best all-around bear cartridges he walks a fine line in terms of his recommendations.

On the one hand, he discusses how tales of the ferocity of grizzly bears are vastly overstated—but without minimizing their toughness. “Today there is little danger attending the hunting of the grizzly for a fairly good shot, equipped with a modern rifle of fair killing qualities,” he said. “However, a grizzly can carry away an awful amount of lead, and from the standpoint of obtaining the bear as well as for protection, a powerful rifle is desired.”

What qualified as “powerful,” according to Whelen, were the .30/40 Krag, .30/06, .405 Winchester, .35 W.C.F., and the .375 H&H.

He rightly noted that the .375 H&H was a cut above those other rounds. At one point he described a scenario where when hunting large coastal brown bears in thick cover around salmon streams carrying a stopping cartridge like the .375 H&H was the way to go.

But he also understood the limitations of the round in terms of practical accuracy for most hunters.

“The recoil is not severe to the thoroughly trained rifleman, but is rather heavy for the novice and the ordinary sportsman,” he said. He goes on, “I find that in many cases sportsmen have selected a rifle for this cartridge because they have had failures with the .30/06 cartridge. In almost every case of this kind, if we analyze the seeming failures with the latter cartridge we find that they are due either to a lack of skill in marksmanship, or else the wrong type of .30/06 cartridge was chosen for the game in question. The .375 Magnum cartridge will not in any sense make up for any lack of skill in marksmanship.”

He continues to hammer home this point, saying that a typical sportsman will be ill served trying to overgun for any animal, and is better off shooting a milder round with an appropriate bullet.

“It is certain that if one is a poor or only ordinary shot, he will shoot much worse with a rifle taking the .375 Magnum cartridge than with one less heavily loaded, due to recoil.”

That perspective is as valid today as it was more than 70 years ago.

This entry requires a bit of a caveat. First off, when Whelen first wrote about hunting black bears in American Big Game Shooting, he didn’t offer a recommendation as to which cartridge to use. It is the only animal in that book that he declined to list one for.

His reason for doing so was because he didn’t care to hunt them himself and wanted to see them protected by adequate game laws. At the time, bear hunting was largely unregulated and they were regarded as vermin. “The black bear is the only species of American game that I have not shot, and I never want to,” he said.

“The black bear is the most amusing, ludicrous, human, and understandable of our wild animals, and in captivity it makes a most interesting pet…. It is entirely harmless, except on rare occasions, and it should be protected by proper game laws and permitted to survive,” he wrote.

That said, the book, which he did in conjunction with the Western Cartridge Company, had a picture of the above-mentioned .35 Winchester Cartridge under the black bear entry, which was a good all-around big-game woods cartridge.

The rise in popularity of the .35 Remington no doubt hastened the .35 Winchester’s demise. It was initially offered in Remington’s Model 8 autoloading rifles and was later chambered in Remington Model 14 and 141 pump actions and the Model 30 bolt gun. Marlin started chambering it in their lever-actions in 1953.

All this is to say that Whelen recognized the capabilities of the .35 Remington as an all-around option for deer, bear, and other animals.

“It is a good cartridge of moderate power and range for use on all American big game. It kills very well at medium ranges, being in this respect much superior to cartridges of the .30/30 class,” he said.

He felt the cartridge was a good match for the Remington Model 141 in particular. “Deer will be killed much more quickly and reliably than with the other cartridges adapted to this rifle, and (in the rifle it) is by no means out of the running for moose, elk, or large bear.”

Among all the cartridges Whelen spent time with, the quarter-bore “Bob” was his all-time favorite. He was in touch with Ned Roberts during the development of the .257 Roberts in the mid-1920s and encouraged him to call it the “.25 Roberts” in light of the effort he put into creating the round, which is based on a necked-down 7×57 Mauser.

“I think that no man has ever spent so much time in perfecting a cartridge as Mr. Roberts spent on this one,” Whelen wrote.

Whelen who was an avid varmint shooter and one of the driving forces behind the development of benchrest shooting, loved small, accurate cartridges so it is no wonder he took to the Roberts when it was introduced.

He favored 100-grain bullets for their flat shooting and hard-hitting capabilities on woodchucks out to 300 yards and on crows to 200 yards.

“I do not know of a finer long range varmint cartridge than this, particularly with hand loaded cartridges,” he said.

He didn’t feel it was limited to popping chucks, however. “It has been proved innumerable times that the full charged cartridges loaded with 100- and 117-grain bullets give excellent results on deer, and we therefore here have a most excellent all-around cartridge which a rifleman can use all year round for small game, varmints, and target, and then take into the big woods after deer in the fall,” he said, adding that other than for moose and elk it was a good option for other game, including antelope.

Because of its light recoil, relatively soft report, excellent accuracy, and—for the time—wide bullet selection he said it would get the nod as the one cartridge he’d pick if could only shoot one.

“I consider it a very fine cartridge, and if I had of necessity to confine myself to one rifle only I would choose it in this caliber,” he said.

Whelen didn’t mince words when it came to hunting sheep and goats. He knew through first-hand experience how challenging those animals are, both in terms of the terrain that needed to be traversed to get to them and the difficult shots a hunter was often confronted with.

“The hunting and stalking of them is so difficult that the head of a big ram killed by fair stalking is the most prized trophy that can fall to the rifle of the American hunter,” he said. “Often the stalk ends with the hunter quite a long distance from his game with no chance of getting closer unobserved…for this reason a rifle adapted to a cartridge of very high velocity and extreme accuracy at long ranges is most desirable.”

The two cartridges he touted as most capable for this work were the “.30 caliber Model 1906” shooting a 180-grain “Open Point” and the 7mm Mauser shooting a 139-grain open point.

In his personal 7mm Mauser he was able to swap between 175-grain expanding bullets for large game and the 139s when it came time to go after deer, sheep, goats, antelope and varmints.

When zeroed with the 139s at 100 yards, the 175s shot to point of aim at 200 yards, giving him the confidence to swap ammo as needed on the fly.

But he warned his readers not to count on that trick working in their personal guns.

“Sometimes such a combination can be worked out for certain rifles, and for some it cannot be accomplished. It is not so much a matter of cartridge, caliber, or make of rifle, as how the individual barrel vibrates with each load.”

One thing that’s clear to me is that Whelen would have been a fan of the 6.5 Creedmoor. He loved mild, accurate, and effective cartridges, particularly when loaded with the proper bullets, and didn’t hesitate to employ them on big game.

It shouldn’t come as a shock then to see him sing the praises of the 6.5x54mm Mannlicher Schoenauer as a good choice for caribou.

“Caribou are often found in open or partly open country, and the careful stalking necessary to bring them to bag, with attention to wind, sight, and hearing, make their pursuit a splendid sport,” he said. “As they are often in localities where it is impossible to approach very closely, a rifle which is effective at long range is very desirable, usually one firing a military or semi-military type cartridge.”

He says that caribou deserve a cartridge along the lines of what one would use for elk and lists the .30/06, 7mm Mauser, and 6.5mm Mannlicher as examples—all of which were standard issue military rounds at the time.

Whelen’s eclectic and open-minded approach to cartridges was a defining characteristic of his. So narrowing down a list of his favorite rounds is a bit tricky.

On the one hand, in 1923 he called the .30/40 Krag the “most useful” and “successful cartridge ever made for the American sportsman” that “will kill with a single shot any game in America.” He supported that statement with anecdotes of his own success with the .30/40 Krag in the field, including a one-shot instant kill on an 18-foot crocodile. Fast forward 17 years to 1940 and he doesn’t even mention the round in his thick tome, The Hunting Rifle.

That was in part a function of the rapid pace of small arms and ammunition development at the time. Whelen’s job was to be on the cutting edge of all things ballistic, and he refused to stick with something out of a sense of nostalgia.

That said, having read much of what Whelen wrote I’ve aimed to do him justice with the rounds I picked. All of the cartridges mentioned are ones he bestowed his imprimatur upon, though I will be curious to see what other fans of “Townie”—as he was called by his friends—think his favorites would have been.

John B. Snow is Shooting Editor of Outdoor Life, where he oversees the publication’s firearms and shooting coverage. This includes gear reviews, features on technical innovations, stories on shooting techniques and general hunting coverage with the occasional fishing story thrown into the mix.

#8402. Lucky Skier Survives Night in Crevasse With Two Broken Legs

Thirty-nine-year-old Emeric Ossatte was ski touring alone earlier this week in the Savoie area of the French Alps, near the Italian border. He removed his skis to posthole up a steep couloir in his boots and crampons when an avalanche swept him downhill, depositing him into a crevasse and breaking both his legs.

The Grenoble-area man, who wasn’t carrying a beacon or radio, doffed his backpack and crawled 100m through the crevasse, looking for a way out. But the sides of the crack were too steep, and there was no cell service where he was. He remained beside an air pocket in the crevasse overnight. Meanwhile, when he didn’t check in that evening, his family raised the alarm.

According to a Facebook report by France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue unit, they saw the slide path but no immediate sign of Ossatte. The search continued until the middle of the night, when avalanche risk forced the rescuers to suspend their operations.

The following morning, a large contingent of 28 ground rescuers, dogs, three helicopters, and a physician to tend to Ossatte, if they found him alive, converged on the site, according to France 3. They found his gear and dug for five hours until they heard him calling out.

“He was really lucky,” said Major Yoann Haberey of the mountain-rescue team. “We knew he was under there. But we didn’t think we’d find him alive.”

By the time they extracted him, he had been there almost a day and a half. The hypothermic but lucky survivor was flown to the hospital for treatment and surgery on one of his legs.

#8398. Weekend Warm-Up: Behind the Climb

As the title suggests, Behind the Climb brings viewers behind the scenes with Colin Haley and Tyler Karow as they take on the Southeast Ridge of Cerro Torre. Colin Haley has tried the route, which he calls a modern classic, three times already without success. He hopes that by teaming up with Tyler Karow, the fourth time will be the charm.

As the short film opens, Haley introduces us to the route in voiceover, while on-screen, he and Karow make the approach, crossing ice fields and fording streams. The route is divided into three main sections: the Haston Crack, Ice Towers, and Headwall. Haley was turned back twice by bad weather on the Ice Towers; neither of them has finished the Headwall.

The Headwall section of Cerro Torre. Photo: Screenshot

Hoping to catch the Ice Towers still frozen and the Headwall still warmed by the sun, they wake up so early that the sun hasn’t actually finished setting yet. At that point, it seems more accurate to say that they took an evening nap and then climbed through the night.

Interspersed with clips of climbing, Haley and Karow review the footage, sharing the little logistical details and impressions of the climb. The Ice Towers, which Karow recalls attempting many years ago, have changed since he last encountered them. Once, as the name implies, thick with ice, much of it has now melted. “In another ten years,” Karow predicts, they will “look drastically different.”

Ice or no, they complete the second section. This time, the weather cooperates. Haley takes over for the final section, the dastardly Headwall which has repeatedly eluded him. The climbing itself is going well, but melting snow above is making the rock run with water. This, he admits, is a significant disadvantage to their carefully timed plan.

Despite the wet holds, Haley makes good progress. With only a few pitches to go, the self-described pessimist Haley admits that he finally thinks they’ll make it to the top. When he does finally reach the summit, grinning widely at the camera, voiceover reveals he has already started worrying about the descent.

Keeping with the promise to go behind the scenes, the video doesn’t stop at the summit, where they stand victoriously and admire the views. We follow the pair as they rappel down.

At the bottom, when asked how they feel about the adventure, both men reply simply that their hands hurt. Not all of us can be poets.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8397. Kayaking 3,700km Around Britain

Dominic Gribbin, 61, is more than five months into his attempt to circumnavigate mainland Great Britain by sea kayak. But relentless weather delays and mounting costs have made the journey even more challenging than anticipated.

Gribbin launched his expedition from Crinan Harbour in Argyll, Scotland, on June 2. Paddling clockwise, he has covered 2,584km. From Crinan, he continued up the west coast of Scotland and around Cape Wrath and Duncansby Head before heading south along the eastern and southern coasts. His latest reported landing point is Cliff End, near Hastings, Sussex.

The journey has not gone according to plan. Persistent Atlantic weather systems have cost him weeks of paddling time, while the financial demands of keeping the expedition moving have forced him to return to freelance television camerawork so he and his wife, Nicola, can keep it running.

While he paddles, she follows him in their motor home, offering shore support. He now hopes to complete the circle at Crinan in the spring of 2027. ExplorersWeb caught up with him to find out more about the journey. This interview has been edited for brevity and clarity.

How do you approach the daily distances on a journey like this?

Given that circumnavigating the British mainland is at least 3,700km, I have aimed to do at least 40km per day. My longest day so far has been 64km. However, weather, tides and daylight length, particularly in autumn and winter, often curtail one’s best-laid plans. Some days have been necessarily shorter, forcing me to make an earlier-than-envisaged retreat to shore.

But this is very much a journey, not a race, so we will continue into the spring of 2027. It’s an incredible opportunity to explore every nook and cranny of the GB coastline.

How much has the weather affected your progress?

Roughly 85% of delays have been due to poor weather, 5% to Ministry of Defense firing range timetable restrictions, and 10% to the need for very specific tidal windows to navigate particular sections safely and efficiently. I have never had a delay due to fatigue or the need for a break from the sheer physical nature of this endurance challenge. I have used days when I have inevitably been “weathered-off” to recoup my energy reserves.

For most of the trip, it has been extremely rare to have more than four days of consistently good paddling weather. Setting off from Crinan on June 2 meant we did not enjoy the heat wave experienced in southern Britain. Instead, we lost at least six weeks to poor weather. Constant Atlantic westerly and northwesterly fronts brought wind, rain, and high seas that seemed to permanently batter northwest and northern Scotland throughout June, July, and even into early August. It was one of the great untalked-about weather phenomena of summer 2026. It wasn’t all heat waves…at least not in Scotland!

Are you camping or sleeping in the motorhome?

Northwest Scotland and its wild, deeply indented coastline often made road access to the sea impossible. Early on, we accepted that there would be long stretches when Nicola would not see me for over 24 hours. She was often doing double the mileage I was doing and negotiating some very hairy driving conditions on roads way off the beaten track. That resulted in many planned and unplanned wild camps along the coast.

By the time we reached the north coast of Scotland, road access had improved dramatically, and Nicola and I were generally able to coordinate to meet at the end of each day. This has proved an invaluable motivator, especially when the going gets tough. Our little motorhome has been a blessed retreat each night since then: a warm, reassuring space to relax, gather one’s thoughts, and plan ahead.

The route so far. The blue line indicates the paddling route, the red line shows the route taken by the support vehicle. Image: Dominic Gribbin

What kayak are you paddling, and how much equipment do you carry?

I am paddling around Britain in a Rockpool Taran 16 sea kayak. It is a fast, beautifully handmade sea kayak, perfect for putting down the miles and handling heavy seas. The trade-off is stability, but it has a prodigious load capacity in its various watertight bulkheads.

I carry a kayak trolley permanently on the back deck. I have already worn out two and am constantly experimenting with new designs to better negotiate the challenges of gravel and sand beaches. As a minimum, I carry basic wild-camping equipment in case I need to spend an unexpected night ashore.

Food consists of pre-cooked rice bags, canned fish and vegetables, alongside plenty of snacks. I favor savory rather than sweet food. Coffee is my essential luxury, and I normally carry a can or two of beer!

Safety equipment is extensive. I carry a Garmin 86i Marine GPS with InReach satellite tracking, an ICOM marine handheld VHF radio with AIS and GPS, a spare VHF radio, flares and smoke signals, and a McMurdo personal locator beacon. I also carry a fully integrated Garmin Fenix 7 Pro watch, first-aid kit, spare paddles, compass, mobile phone, battery packs, head lamp, repair tape, and a Swiss Army penknife.

The kayak itself weighs 24.9kg, but the overall weight, including my dry suit, buoyancy aid, clothing, and equipment, easily reaches 35kg or more. It is a significant challenge to negotiate all this weight over an often-unfriendly shoreline. However, the kayak benefits from the additional weight when afloat.

What have been the most memorable moments along the way?

We have had numerous encounters with sea otters, hunting pods of bottlenose dolphins, and close-up encounters with bottlenose mothers and calves. There have also been golden eagles and white-tailed sea eagles, the largest raptor in the UK. Some of the most sublime moments have been the inshore sea passages through the sheltered waters of the Inner Hebrides.

Successfully rounding Cape Wrath was a truly intimidating but awe-inspiring milestone. Then there was rounding the infamous Duncansby Head and crossing the Pentland Firth near John O’Groats, finally seeing the compass turn south. The huge breeding seabird colonies off Caithness and much of Scotland’s northeast coast have been incredible. Literally tens of thousands of birds, a deafening cacophony of noise!

Right along the GB coastline, we have also met some of the warmest and most generous people.

Have there been any particularly frightening moments?

The huge seas off Strathy Point, Caithness, are probably the closest call yet. If something had gone wrong there while two kilometers offshore in those seas, I wouldn’t be writing this.

I have experienced plenty of rough and scary seas so far, but that occasion was another order of magnitude. They were enormous seas driven by Arctic swell and spring tides. They were the biggest seas I have ever paddled in. I am not in a rush to return there, despite its beauty.

You have mentioned that you are now combining freelance work with the expedition. How are you managing the financial and logistical side?

We were able to make this happen because I am self-employed. We bankrolled the expedition using our savings. The motorhome for shore support was the biggest investment. We initially calculated finances based on finishing by mid-autumn, but those weather delays cost us dearly. We ran out of money by mid-September, so I have had to return to my freelance work as a TV cameraman and juggle that with keeping this Edge of Britain expedition going until we are finished.

There is no stopping this winter. Instead, we will take advantage of every weather window.

I began paddling at age 11 when I permanently borrowed a friend’s old kayak and started exploring the ankle-deep waters of the River Ver and River Colne around St. Albans and Park Street, Hertfordshire. At 13, I started British Canoe Union training. This began my lifelong involvement with whitewater paddling on rivers around the world.

Back in the 1970s and 1980s, when I began, skills progression was funneled entirely through very actively encouraged participation in canoe slalom. Everybody who learned to paddle at club level was involved in slalom to some degree. I chose men’s C1 (Canadian singles) and competed at a national level, ranking 19th in Great Britain by the age of 18.

University changed my orientation somewhat, and the allure of traveling to exotic whitewater rivers around the world took over. Expedition whitewater paddling then became my life, and still is, alongside expedition sea kayaking. Combining this with filmmaking ultimately led directly to my career as a television cameraman.

Why did you decide to take on a circumnavigation of Britain?

The decision to attempt to paddle around the GB mainland is the culmination of a life spent kayaking. It is hard not to fall in love with the sheer beauty and allure of the world of the river and coastline, particularly here at home in the UK. This country has what is widely celebrated as some of the best sea kayaking in the world. To dream of exploring the whole GB coastline in as much detail as time and weather allow is, to me, a logical conclusion.

The actual decision came extremely late in the day, namely January 2026. This began a frantic period of organization ahead of our June 2 launch. Admittedly, April would have been better, but prior commitments meant we had to begin in June.

I chose a clockwise route around Britain, thinking (naively) that the prevailing winds would vaguely be behind us for at least half the journey. I also wanted to tackle the potentially most technically demanding section of the northwest and north coast of Scotland early, in the height of what I envisaged as long summer days. How wrong I was!

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8391. No-Oxygen Team Will Try Again on Everest

Last week, high winds forced the Russian team to retreat from their third trip up the West Ridge of Everest. Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and leader Andrei Vasiliev were climbing without oxygen, with no one else on the mountain. Winter is quickly approaching, but they have no intention of going home yet.

“We are at Lobuche…resting, analyzing the weather forecast. Still want to try again,” Vasiliev texted to ExplorersWeb over the weekend. The climbers had not intended to reach the summit on that latest attempt up the West Shoulder of Everest via the Lho La Pass. Vasiliev explained that they were not yet acclimatized enough to reach the top without bottled oxygen. They did expect to reach higher than before, but high winds made that impossible.

In a post on social media, member Sergey Kondrashkin recalls the latest trip up. On November 11, the team set off from their tent at the base of the West Ridge, fixing ropes in bitterly cold temperature. Contrary to forecasts, winds were light. The climbers pitched their tent at 6,500m and still had time to fix some ropes above them.

On the following morning, however, the gusts were too strong. After waiting in vain for the gale to abate, and with a forecast of even stronger winds the following day, Kondrashkin returned to the ridge, trying not to be blown away. He advanced between gusts and retrieved his two ice axes and the rope he had left behind the day before. They would need that gear for the descent.

Once at the bivouac, the climbers retrieved their tent, cached some food and fuel, and downclimbed to their Camp 1 at some 5,600m. It lies on the steep section between the Khumbu Glacier and the Lho La. Here, they pitched their tent again.

On Friday, the Russian team rappelled down to the glacier, on a “relatively safe” line to the right, where an avalanche fell as they descended.

Today, the team sits at their lodge in Lobuche and looks intently at weather forecasts. “We have not yet decided when to return to the mountain,” Vasiliev told Explorersweb. “It’s all about the weather, and the forecasts announce high winds on November 20-21.”

#8385. Officials Blame Climate Change for Glacier Collapse That Killed 17

Russian authorities are now attributing the September 6 disaster on 5,047m Mount Mizhirgi in the northern Caucasus to climate-related destabilization of a hanging glacier.

A half-million cubic meter avalanche struck a military group undergoing mountain training, killing 17 and injuring 10. Another 10 were unharmed and made it down the mountain on their own.

The impact generated a powerful blast of displaced air that reportedly threw some victims 200m to 300m down the slope before burying them. As we reported at the time, helicopter pilot Alexander Davydov caught the avalanche on video.

Eldar Khashkhozhev of the Russian Ministry of Emergency Situations explained that warmer temperatures melted the protective blanket of snow, exposing parts of the glacier. This weakened the ice support that had glued enormous masses of snow and ice in place for decades.

A helicopter inspection of the region’s snow conditions the month before found no cause for concern, and some officials insist the catastrophe was unpredictable. However, climber Dmitry Polyakov, who was in the region at the time, disputed the idea that the glacier posed no danger. He said climbers had long recognized its instability and generally avoided approaching it in large groups.

The Mizhirgi Glacier against Mizhirgi East and West peaks, site of September’s catastrophic glacial collapse. Photo: Shutterstock

The key points of Khashkhozhev’s explanation were:

Rising temperatures have raised the altitude of permanent snow cover.

Previously protected sections of the glacier became exposed, weakening the supporting ice beneath the hanging masses.

Eventually, gravity overcame that support and triggered a collapse that struck the training group.

This was the largest climbing disaster in the country since 2002, when another glacier collapse in North Ossetia, located between the Black and Caspian Seas, killed 125 people, including local residents.

With major landslides in summer and the possibility of glacial collapse at other times of the year throughout the world, mountaineers will have to adjust their behavior, as previously safe areas become potentially deadly.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8383. Never-Say-Die Climbers Will Try Everest Again This Weekend

Despite a forecast of high winds on November 21 and 22, Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov have decided to begin a new attempt on the West Ridge of Everest on Sunday.

The three stubborn Russians will again proceed via the West Ridge without bottled oxygen, support, or any other living soul on the mountain.

The forecasts warn of very high winds all weekend. Nevertheless, they will start up on Sunday, November 22, although that is the day with the strongest winds forecast.

“That day, we will remain low on the mountain and climb to our Camp 1 at 5,600m,” Sergey Kondrashkin wrote.  “From there, we will push upward when the weather improves and the winds die down, aiming to descend around November 28.”

This means the team does not intend to summit quite yet. The goal is to acclimatize by reaching 7,500m and then return to their headquarters in Lobuche.

“The question is, will we manage to complete the summit push within the time frame of our permit, which expires on December 15, and will the weather cooperate?” said Kondrashkin.

The fall season on Everest usually lasts until the end of November, since December 1 marks the start of Nepal’s winter climbing season. However, the permit dates may have been extended for this team, which is climbing off the normal route.

Once winter settles in, it will be the turn of Jost Kobusch of Germany, who will attempt to solo Everest in winter along the same route the Russians are now attempting.

On their third and latest trip up the mountain so far, the team only reached 6,700m before hurricane-force winds up to 130kph forced them down, according to former team member Natalyia Belyankina. Belyankina had to leave the expedition for health reasons but has stayed in touch with the others.

#8381. Jost Kobusch to Train For Everest on Difficult, Dangerous Lhotse Shar

Jost Kobusch of Germany has moved to the Khumbu Valley to finish his acclimatization before attempting to solo winter Everest, beginning on December 22. His preparations include reaching 8,000m on a different peak.

Kobusch is now in Chukhung village, from which he climbed 6,165m Island Peak (Imja Tse) yesterday. He spent the night at 6,000m, topped out again today, and went back down, as shown in his live tracker. He told ExplorersWeb that he will now rest for a few days in Lukla and then hike up the valley again to inspect conditions on his next acclimatization goal: the difficult Lhotse Shar (8,383m).

Kobusch’s climb on Island Peak as shown in his tracker today.

“My plan is not to summit but to tag 8,000m up its flank, before heading for Everest,” he said.

Lhotse Shar is one of the main points of the Lhotse ridge, and it is climbed from south side of the mountain, with Chukhung as the starting point. The main options are to climb the southeastern flank of the Shar itself or try a direct line up the formidable south face of Lhotse.

Neither of them is easy, and the upper sections are statistically dangerous. According to Wikipedia, Lhotse Shar has the highest fatality rate of all principal or secondary 8,000m peaks. For every two climbers who summit, one person dies attempting to.

Lhotse and its various points, as seen from the south. Graphic by Gorskie Mapy

The Austrian expedition that first reached the summit of Lhotse Shar (from the Southeast Ridge) in 1970 set four altitude camps, the uppermost at 7,600m. They used supplemental oxygen on their summit push. “Almost the whole of the route from below Camp 1 to Camp 3 was fixed with rope, as climbing was very difficult,” they reported in the AAJ.

Kobusch will not have to deal with the nearly vertical terrain and cornices near the summit, but will have to face difficult mixed terrain and uncertain conditions if he wants to venture up to 8,000m. He will not use bottled oxygen, and as far as we know, he will climb on his own.

Kobusch plans to reach the base of Everest on December 22 and solo the mountain in winter without oxygen, via the West Ridge — the same line currently being attempted by a Russian team.

This is Kobusch’s fourth attempt, and he is willing to try to reach the summit this time. However, when asked about it, he admits he does not know if such a feat is actually possible. “Every expedition, every training session, every difficult decision has been part of preparing myself for what I know will be the biggest challenge of my life,” he admits.

Kobusch’s is not the only confirmed climb this winter. Denis Urubko of Russia is reportedly preparing to climb Broad Peak in winter, possibly starting at the end of this year and continuing through January and February. On social media, Urubko mentioned he will be climbing with Vitaliy Skel. However, details of his route have not been confirmed.

Simone Moro of Italy has not decided whether to try Manaslu again this winter. He told ExplorersWeb he is still considering his plans while observing the “crazy weather this season.” Moro attempted Manaslu in fall to acclimatize and become familiar with the terrain. However, like every other team, he had to call off the attempt due to dangerous conditions following extremely heavy snowfall. Moro wants to climb the peak in alpine style.

#8379. How to Sharpen an Axe Like a Pro. Follow This Step-By-Step Guide

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 9:01 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Many people have heard the story about two woodcutters chopping trees in the forest. One chops and chops and chops without stopping, while the other takes breaks frequently, but still manages to chop more wood. That’s because while the second woodcutter was “on break” he was sharpening his axe. Now, lots of corporate types use this story to try to teach something about productivity, or whatever, but the only real takeaway is that a sharp axe translates to less hard work.

A sharp axe will have less glancing blows, cut deeper, and cut faster. I’m not interested in banging a dull hunk of metal into a tree for 15 minutes when I could get it done with a sharp axe in less than five. So, sharp axe good, dull axe bad. But how do you get your axe nice and sharp and ready to do some real work? Believe it or not, it’s not that hard, and it doesn’t require expensive tools. Here’s a step-by-step guide on how to sharpen an axe.

For this method, you really only need two tools. A flat file, and a “puck” style sharpening stone. The file should be a Mill Bastard file. These are commonly available at any hardware store, and if you get your technique correct, you don’t actually need a puck afterward. An 8” or bigger file makes this easier, but you can get the job done with a smaller 4” or 6” file. I sharpened my axe for this tutorial with a flat file for leveling chainsaw guide teeth, just to show that it can be done with a small file. (Plus my bigger file is getting dull and needs replacing).

After you’re done with the file, you can move on to really honing and sharpening the edge with a puck-style sharpening stone. I use stones from Straight Grain Supply because they are dual grit, and the rubber band makes for a good grip. The stones come in a variety of grits, and can be used alone without a file if you like. I use both because the file removes damage faster, and the pucks give a finer edge with the higher grits. For these stones, I keep a bowl of water and an old toothbrush handy. Letting them sit in water before sharpening helps to keep them from getting clogged up with metal particles. A quick scrub with the toothbrush and some water helps keep them clean and cutting properly and it extends their effective life.

If you have a vise, that can be helpful if there is damage that needs to be sharpened out, but it’s not necessary. If you don’t have a vise, having a longer file is much more important. More on that later. The last thing you will need is a dull axe. I used my “beater” axe for this story. It’s been used as a wedge and it’s been beaten and deformed. It has smashed more than a couple rocks while taking out roots. It had some serious damage, so I thought it would be useful to show how effective the file is. As a final note, I recommend wearing a pair of work gloves for this because your hands and fingertips will be very close to the edge.

First, you will take your file and set your edge angle and thin out the cheek of the axe. The cheek is the portion of the blade just behind the edge. You can thin this out if you want the axe to chop deeper into the wood. If you have to bring the edge back for significant damage, you should file the cheeks down a bit to keep the cutting performance. Make sure you are sharpening the whole bevel, not just a tiny portion near the apex of the edge. With a longer file, you can ball up an old rag or t-shirt and put it on the axe head and rest the tip of the file on it as you make your cuts with the back of the file. Make the rag thick or thinner so you can match the angle of the bevel. The rag acts as a pivot point for the file and allows you to keep pressure on the axe head so it won’t move around.

With a shorter file, this method won’t work, and a vise really comes in handy. For wood handled axes, rubber jaw protectors, rags, or leather can be used to protect the wood in the vise.

Pull your file across the edge from one end of the blade to the other. These files don’t just cut with the file moving from tip to handle, they cut laterally as well. You will feel the file bite into the material. Lift the file and go back and make another lateral pull.

Make passes until all damage is removed, and you feel a burr on the entire edge. Flip it over and do the same thing to the other side. After you have apexed the edge on both sides, you should have a pretty sharp axe, and you can stop here if you want. With just a file, you can achieve a great working edge. But, I like my stuff really sharp, so I continue on to the stones.

Once you have removed the heavy damage and re-profiled the edge with your file, you can refine the edge with progressively finer grit stones. If you don’t have a file, you can use a coarse stone to profile the edge, but it will generally take more time, especially if there are some deeper chips or rolls in the edge.

After your stones have soaked in the water a bit, “scrub” the entire beveled edge with the puck using small circular motions to refine it. Hold the puck with your fingertips and keep them away from the edge.  You will see the scratch pattern from the stone taking away the tooling marks from the file.

Give your stone a dip in the water every so often to keep it clean. When you have removed the marks from the file on the whole edge, repeat for the other side. Then, get the next grit stone, and repeat this process on each side. Scrub until the scratch pattern from the previous grit stone is gone. I usually use the medium grit after the file work and progress to a fine grit. Straight Grain Supply makes two-sided stones in 80/220 grit, 220/400 grit, and 220/600 grit. I use the 220 grit and then progress to my 400 grit side. I have used the 80 grit side for damage before, and it will remove material fairly quickly, but I still prefer a file for initial shaping. After you have done both sides with medium and fine grit, your axe should be ready to go, and will be taking on a nice shiny, somewhat polished appearance. If you have a leather strop, you could make a few passes on that just to remove any small burr that might remain and further polish and refine the edge, but the bur will be gone on the first chop or two anyway. I strop mine because I have one, and I like the edge to be shiny after I’m done. If you want the best edge possible, take your time with these steps. It will be worth it when you start chopping.

Once you profile and remove damage on an axe, maintaining that sharp edge shouldn’t take much effort at all. After each use, take a minute or so and hone each side with your finest grit puck stone before you put the axe away, and you will generally be able to go a long time before you have to sharpen it again. Now, if you happen to hit a rock or nail or something else that damages the edge beyond small nicks, then you will have to go through the whole process again. A little maintenance after each use will keep your axe cutting well between sharpening, and prolong the life of the axe head. If you maintain it with a file every time, you will remove more material than needed and wear out the bit of the axe sooner. An ounce of prevention is better than a pound of cure.

Read Next: Best Camping Axes, Tested and Reviewed

A little time spent learning how to sharpen an axe will pay dividends when it comes time for chopping. For me, few things are as frustrating as a tool not performing the way it is supposed to. The thing is, you might not think your axe is even dull, but if you take the time to sharpen it, you will be amazed at the difference in cutting performance. Invest a little in sharpening and save a bunch of hard work down the road. Keep your axes sharp.

Drew Conover is a contributor at Outdoor Life. He writes mostly gear reviews and how-to articles on survival and navigation. He lives in Upstate New York with his wife, two daughters, and a goofy dog.

#8377. The Paradox of Seeking First Ascents in Western Nepal

Western Nepal has plenty of unclimbed 6,000m peaks, and Fredrik Strang of Sweden and Sindre Kolbjornsgard of Norway went there planning to climb one. In the end, they settled for a slightly lower but equally memorable experience. It showed again that adventure goes beyond first ascents.

What I remember most clearly about that narrow summit is not the grade, the exposure, or the possibility of being first. It is the silence. No helicopters. No voices from a fixed-rope queue. No machinery, no aircraft, no distant sound of a settlement. Only the two of us, a serrated horizon, and an unfamiliar feeling that the mountain had asked a question rather than supplied an answer.

Sindre Kolbjornsgard, left, and Fredrik Strang. Photo: Fredrik Strang

After 18 expeditions to 8,000’ers and witnessing the evolution (or devolution) of climbing on these peaks’ normal routes, Strang and his partner knew they wanted something different. Quoting Ed Viesturs, Strang said: “The point is to climb, not just to summit.” With that in mind, the climbers set four criteria for their objectives:

Several route options face the climbers’ base camp tent. Photo: Fredrik Strang

Strang noted that there are many unclimbed peaks left in Nepal, but they were looking for a compelling climb, not just a dull summit for the record. They finally found one that satisfied their criteria, but then, as with so many other teams in Nepal this fall, harsh weather put their original objective out of reach. Not to mention that traveling Western Nepal is anything but simple.

“Our intended destination was in the Nyalu Lek area north of Simikot,” Strang revealed. “We planned to cross Nyalu La, a pass around 5,000m, with a support party that included porters and pack animals.

“Getting from Surkhet to Simikot took two rough days by road, with landslides having damaged or obliterated sections of the route. At Simikot, we learned that Nyalu La was probably impassable. Even reaching the place where we planned to start trekking had been uncertain.”

When they did reach the area, they confirmed that their planned peak was not an option. Hiking further down the valley, they pitched their tent at Phendol Kermi Camp. Snow had covered the trails beyond, but they simply had no time or money to move to some other region in Nepal. Instead, they decided to explore whatever was close to their camp.

During their stay, they encountered Mick Fowler and Victor Saunders of the UK. Bad weather had likewise spoiled the British pair’s plan to attempt an unclimbed 6,000m peak farther east, known only as Peak 6010, according to Mount Everest Foundation.. This would have been their second attempt on that peak. Ironically, dry conditions spoiled their 2025 attempt. This time, too much snow forced them back. Read more on the British climbers’ adventure here:

On October 6, Strang and Kolbjornsgard made their first acclimatization climb on a 5,177m peak at 30°20′49.82″N, 82°03′54.82″E. They found no records of any previous ascent.

“From base camp, the mountain looked straightforward, and lower down, it largely was,” Strang recalls. “Steep grass, mud, and broken rock led toward the upper slopes. But at about 4,500m, the route disappeared beneath knee-deep snow, then waist-deep snow, and every step became a small excavation.”

In the following days, they scouted and flew their drone down side valleys, looking for possibilities. Their second peak was 5,227m in altitude, located at 30°07′50.86″N, 81°41′56.58″E. Again, there were no records of previous ascents. Parts of it were far from fun.

“Below 4,500m, progress was tolerable, but above that, we were back to forcing our way through knee-deep, sometimes chest-deep snow,” Strang wrote. “A tremendous amount of work for very little enjoyment.”

Luckily, the climb improved once they reached the summit ridge, on relatively easy mixed rock and ice, which they assessed at PD+.

“Fowler and Saunders climbed the mountain several days later and found an easier way to descend and reascend the step on its western side,” Strang noted. “Their solution was a reminder that the first line a team takes is not necessarily the best one.”

Still, the peak that most drew the climbers’ attention was a remarkable 5,337m tooth of rock above Yangchya Lake at approximately 30°07′09.36″N, 81°41′21.15″E.

“We began calling it Daara Peak — a name inspired by the Nepali word for a projecting tooth or fang and by the mountain’s striking shape,” Strang said. He notes that the name is provisional while they await a response from the authorities about their suggestion.

Strang describes the peak as “a saber-toothed tiger’s canine protruding from a mass of rock. The terminal pinnacle was about eight meters high, overhanging and sharply defined against the sky.”

The climbers about to reach what they called Daara Peak. Drone photo: Brizendra Bhattarai

The problem was approaching the peak in deep, heavy snow from Yangchya Lake. They failed on their first attempt to tackle its northeast headwall. After waiting out another snowy spell, the climbers returned for a second attempt on October 17-18.

The climbers dealt with 250m of rather poor rock, a complex approach to the headwall across a bergschrund with soft snow, and the uncertainty of moving on unfamiliar terrain. They had no idea of the difficulties ahead or whether they could protect the passages ahead on their way to the summit. The route included vertical granite walls, with cracks adequate for cams, some mixed terrain with the crux at M6, a steep couloir, and then the summit ridge: a granite edge with some 30cm of snow on top.

Climbing terrain on Daara Peak. Photo: Fredrik Strang

Rather than technical difficulty, the section was delicate due to exposure and a lack of comfortable belay stations. Yet, the climbers reached the summit on October 18, including the top of the final pinnacle, thanks to a crack in the rock.

Strang admits that the climbing details suggest a rather straightforward ascent: a 5,337m summit via its northeast side in five pitches with difficulties up to M6. However, Strang judges the climb on a different scale. “The technical crux was behind us, but it was the part I remember most vividly,” he explained. “It was the silence.”

Strang and Kolbjornsgard’s route up Daara Peak. Photo: Fredrik Strang

On many of the 8,000m peaks I have climbed, the human presence has become part of the landscape: camps, radio calls, fixed ropes, queues, and the distant sound of aircraft. Up here, none of that reached us. Mountains extended in every direction. There was no audience, no ceremony, no one to confirm that we had accomplished anything at all.

Strang also reflects on the paradox: They enjoyed the wild terrain but, if every climber chases solitude by visiting these last remote valleys, the character of these places will inevitably change. For Strang, the question is not to ban access. However, he says:

Perhaps some summits should remain untouched — not because we lack the skill or courage to reach them, but because future generations deserve to inherit mountains that have not all been reduced to objectives.

The climb from the ridge. Photo: Fredrik Strang

#8373. This Massive Swordfish Smashed the State Record, But It Was Disqualified Over a Technicality

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 10:20 AM EST

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

Capt. Jake Kinzie had a crew of three buddies aboard his 28-foot boat while fishing off Ocean City, Maryland the morning of November 11. The anglers ran 60 miles out to fish the deep “Poorman’s Canyon” for prized swordfish — and they would end up catching the largest sword in state history.

Swordfish action has been good this year along the Atlantic Coast. And the “Way Out” crew had caught a couple big swords earlier off Ocean City.

In 1,000 feet of water, they put out a swordfish squid bait on a 10/0 Mustad hook, taken deep with a strobe light attached to the leader to get the attention of a heavy fish.

“Jake is an old friend I’ve known for over two decades,” Capt. Keith Jacobs tells Outdoor Life. “We were with our other buddies Joe Spayd and Blaine Beyer, and about 2:30 p.m. we hooked a deep, very large fish.”

Spayd was on the rod, fighting the tough swordfish for about three hours. It never jumped, and the anglers never saw it until late in the fight.

“We knew it was huge because it was so powerful,” says Jacobs, a professional boat captain and owner of “OC-anic,” a fishing gear and apparel company. “But we figured it was only 300 pounds or so until we saw it near the boat as the sun was setting.”

Jacobs said it was all they could do to keep the swordfish from getting close to their boat and perhaps breaking the 80-pound test braided line on the Daiwa reel that Spayd used. He fought the fish from a gunnel rod holder on a swivel.

“Jake had to spin the boat a lot to keep the line from rubbing against the boat hull,” Jacobs says. “Three hours into the fight we got the fish close to our boat, but it was still full of fight.”

While Spayd stayed on the rod battling the sword, Beyer hit the fish with a harpoon dart. The metal dart head, with a heavy line fitted to it, detached from the pole.

“Then I hit the fish in the head with a big gaff – which wasn’t such a good idea,” says Jacobs. “It went crazy and ripped the gaff handle out of my hands and we lost it overboard.”

Jacobs hit the fish with another gaff to help control and kill it.

“We struggled to get a tail rope around the fish,” Jacobs says. “All that time lines were crossing, we’re wet, tired, the fish is thrashing beside the boat – and it’s getting dark in three-foot seas.”

Finally, the anglers secured the massive swordfish with a tail rope. After the fish died, they had to haul it into their 28-foot boat.

“We didn’t have a block-and-tackle or come-along, so we had to grab it and haul it over the stern and around the boat’s two outboards,” Jacobs says. “We had to do it twice, because a fin got wedged in one of the outboards.

“We were exhausted when we finally got it in the boat. It took up about half of the deck space. That’s when we headed back in to Ocean City.”

The anglers arrived at Ocean City’s Snug Harbor waterfront neighborhood about 9 p.m. There the 12-foot long fish was hauled out of their boat with a crane and weighed on a digital scale at 558 pounds.

It topped the largest swordfish caught by a sport angler by 165 pounds. That fish was boated in 2022 by angler Jeff Jacobs and weighed 393 pounds.

Read Next: Maine Tuna Fisherman Accidentally Catches Record-Setting Swordfish

The anglers covered their 558-pound swordfish with fish bags and ice, and the next day contacted authorities to enter it as a state record catch.

“But we learned that because we used an electric Daiwa reel it doesn’t qualify as a Maryland record,” Jacobs says. “Other states have record fish that are certified if electric reels are used by sporting tackle anglers.

“The tackle we used – even with electric reels – is far different than what commercial fishermen use.

“I wish Maryland had another records division that allows electric reels for heavyweight marine gamefish like swordfish, tuna, and giant marlin.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.
