#8372. A Climbing Expedition to 'Central Asia's Patagonia'

A mix of nine young and veteran Croatian climbers spent the month of August in one of the world’s great big-wall destinations — Kyrgyzstan’s Karavshin Range in the Pamir-Alay Mountains. Reaching up to 5,509m, the once-unknown granite cliffs now draw dozens of international climbers each summer. The two core valleys, Ak-Su and Kara-Su, have become known as Central Asia’s Patagonia.

The two leaders, Sinisa Skalec and Lukas Grbac Lackovic, spoke to ExplorersWeb about the place and their expedition. First, a summary of their four weeks.

Though suffering from stomach issues for most of the month (more on this later), the team repeated several granite classics, did a new route on 4,430m Pik Breeze, and attempted another new line.

Three climbers — Bruno Crljenic, Eva Pejic, and Lackovic — identified a potential new line below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They knew of only one previous route on the mountain, done by Russians Marina Popova and Denis Prokofiev.

Beginning at 5am, the three crossed a broad scree slope, then began climbing a couloir below Pik Breeze’s west ridge. They negotiated loose rock, briefly followed an existing line, then moved left into crack systems.

Canned Horse, 6c A0, top. Below, previous attempts and possible other lines on the face.

They climbed until 9pm, when they bivouacked on a tiny ledge with space for only two people sitting and one lying down. Resuming at 8:30am, they reached a col after three pitches and continued directly up slabs and cracks. They finished on easier IV/V terrain and followed a 200m ridge to the summit.

They called their new line Canned Horse, 930m, 23 pitches, 6c A0. It took them a further eight hours to get back to base camp. During the climb, they found pitons on two pitches. They believe these belonged to another route on the west ridge, but the history of that line, and whether it was finished, is uncertain.

Pejic, Grbac Lackovic, and Crljenic on top of Pik Breeze.

Later in the month, one of those climbers, Eva Pejic, became quite ill and couldn’t leave base camp on her own. Five teammates helped evacuate her, carrying her for 11km, with assistance from other climbers in the area. She later recuperated in the Kyrgyz city of Osh.

On the first crux of ‘Perestroika Crack.’

Three small teams of Croatian climbers also repeated Perestroika Crack, one of the best-known long granite routes in Central Asia. It rises about 900m up the north face of 4,240m Pik Slesova and is graded 7a. Three members reportedly free-climbed the route. One pair, Sinisa Skalec and Ivan Vitez, completed the line in a single marathon day, beginning at 5:45am, summiting at 5:40pm, and returning to base camp at 11pm.

Skalec and Vitez also repeated the French Ridge of 4,510m Kyrkchilta, 2,000m, 6b to 6b+. They needed one bivouac. Toward the end of August, this same pair attempted a first ascent on the Central Pyramid of Ortotyubek, overlooking base camp. Their attempted route mounted slabs and cracks to the left of the established Take It Easy line, which other Croatians had repeated during that month. The pair gave up their attempt on August 26 because of illness.

Base camp with Ortotyubek and Slesova in the background.

Here is our interview with Lukas Grbac Lackovic and Sinisa Skalec about Kyrgyzstan and the Ak-Su Valley.

Lukas Grbac Lackovic repeats the Central Route on a peak called Small Russian Tower.

Was Eva Pejic’s health problem related to altitude or something else? What was the illness you experienced on Ortotyubek?

Eva’s health problem was unrelated to altitude exposure or the expedition environment. However, most of us experienced gastrointestinal infections, with symptoms including diarrhea, bloating, fever, and general malaise.

This significantly hindered our climbing, as everyone in the team got sick at least once, and some even twice. Recovery was slow and never quite complete –- we felt weak for most of the trip.

One thing that helped us recover was having plain rice with some of our meals, which we could get from the cook if we asked nicely. If we were to go again, we would definitely bring our own food and cooking equipment. This can be inconvenient, but it could potentially save you at least a week of being sick and unable to climb.

Another view of base camp and peaks Ortotyubek and Slesova.

Can you describe the Karavshin Valley? How large is it? Are there many side valleys?

The two main valleys are Ak-Su and Kara-Su. Adjacent to them is the Jaupaya Valley, which provides a descent route from Pik Breeze and is home to several peaks around 5,000m high. The rock quality there appears poor, and we are not aware of any established rock routes in the valley.

Another interesting area, west of Kara Su, is the Orto-Chashma Gorge. An Italian team made a first ascent there during our visit.

Here in the West, we first heard about the area following the kidnapping of four American climbers in Kara-Su in 2000. Since then, it seems to have calmed down and become a popular climbing destination. Is the area considered safe nowadays, or are there still things climbers should be aware of?

Currently, the security situation in the valley is good. There is always the possibility of tensions, of course, as the valleys lie close to the border with Tajikistan. However, in recent years, the number of climbers visiting the area has increased exponentially, reaching around 100 climbers this year.

How many other climbing teams were in Ak-Su and neighboring Kara-Su while you were there? Where were they generally from?

During the three weeks we spent in the valley, up to 50 climbers were in BC at the same time. Climbers came from all over the world — Chile, U.S., Spain, Slovakia, Poland, Belgium, Slovenia, France, Italy, Germany, Japan, Russia…

We had originally planned a different expedition, but had to change our destination just a few months before departure. Some friends from Croatia and Slovenia recommended the area because of its straightforward logistics and relatively good security, while still offering impressive big walls and proper crack climbing. It didn’t take much to convince us!

Are there any military checkpoints? Do you need special permission to enter the valleys?

Yes, you have to pass through a military checkpoint when entering the area. However, the outfitter handles the application for the necessary permits and ensures everything goes smoothly.

Some call the Karavshin Valley the Patagonia of Central Asia because of its aesthetic granite walls. Do you think that’s a fair description?

Patagonia is definitely more spectacular, but you pay the price with poor weather. If your goal is to climb almost every day, then the Karavshin Valley is a better choice.

Climbing in Patagonia requires much more logistical planning and preparation, and the approaches themselves are a whole different story. In the Karavshin Valley, on the other hand, there is no snow or ice during the season we visited (August), at least on the walls we climbed. Some of the walls are just a 15-minute walk from BC, and you can reach them all in approach shoes.

Perestroika Crack and some of the classic routes in the Karavshin region are arguably just as good as the best rock routes in Patagonia.

How does the granite compare to Yosemite and Patagonia?

The granite is more similar in texture to that found in Patagonia; it is not as smooth as in Yosemite. The overall rock quality is generally good, although, as everywhere, some sections are of poorer quality. One downside of the mild climate in the Karavshin Valley is the dirt and grass that accumulate in the cracks, which can sometimes detract from the aesthetic appeal of the routes. The more popular and frequently climbed routes have already been cleaned and are pleasant to climb.

You climbed 4-1/2 pitches of the route on Ortotyubek. How much did you have left to complete it?

There were still two-and-a-half pitches left to complete the route up to the junction with Take It Easy. We envisioned the second half of the fifth pitch following the line up to a point just below the overhang that runs diagonally to the left.

From there, the sixth pitch follows a thin crack, from which you need to traverse left and move into another system of thin cracks that runs across the roof and joins the Take It Easy belay (seventh pitch). The last pitch of Take It Easy offers a logical exit, and we don’t think there is any need to come up with an alternative.

At the point where we stopped (first half of the fifth pitch), we left two pitons that we used as a temporary rappel anchor. Repeating parties are welcome to remove them, as they were only left for temporary use. We think a few bolts may be needed in the second half of the fifth pitch, but that obviously depends on the abilities of those planning to complete the route.

All belays are equipped (the first and fourth belays have two 8mm bolts each, while the second and third have two pitons each). In addition, we left two or three pitons in the first pitch and two in the second pitch.

As in the rest of the valley, some sections of the route are filled with dirt and grass. We made an effort to clean the cracks in the first four pitches, but there is still plenty of work to be done, especially on the sixth pitch, which we didn’t climb, where a thin crack is completely filled with dirt.

How much unclimbed rock remains? Are the obvious lines already established, or are there still many promising routes?

The most obvious and accessible lines may have already been climbed, but there is still plenty of potential. Anyone willing to put in the effort can establish a new route. The area is vast, and there is still plenty of room for new routes.

Your website mentions that there are guidebooks to certain routes in that area. Where are these available?

Most of the guidebooks we used are available online. We received some Russian guidebooks from climbing friends, although we didn’t use them very much. We’re not sure how easily they can be found online. Climbers from all over the world bring various materials to the camp, so most route topos and information can be obtained there.

What was the most unexpected experience on your expedition — something that might surprise climbers familiar with other big-wall areas?

We were pleasantly surprised by the kindness and honesty of the Kyrgyz people. When traveling to remote parts of the world, many of us tend to expect that someone will try to rip us off and that we will have to haggle over everything, but that was not the case here.

Although some things took longer than expected, everything we had agreed on in advance was respected, and all the logistics for transportation and accommodation worked very well.

As we mentioned earlier, our only complaint concerns the food and hygiene conditions at the camp, which were most likely responsible for the digestive problems experienced by virtually all the climbers staying there.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8369. Avalanche Fatality Warns About Early Ski Conditions In The Alps

Little snow on the mountains doesn’t mean safe conditions. An American off-piste skier died in the Austrian Alps last weekend after he triggered a small slab avalanche. It was far too small to bury him, but it carried him into an open crevasse below.

With the ski season barely underway, this is Europe’s first avalanche victim this season. Last year, 151 people died in avalanches in Europe between October 2025 and June 2026, according to the European Avalanche Warning Service (EAWS), a shockingly high number.

Most victims perished in the Alps: 40 in Italy, 35 in France, 33 in Austria, and 18 in Switzerland. It was the 13th most deadly year in the Alps since record-keeping began. It was also the highest tally ever since the “Winter of Terror” of 1951. Avalanche prevention standards have improved a lot since then, but the number of leisure skiers, climbers, and hikers in the mountains has multiplied as well. (Most of those 1951 victims lived in villages along avalanche paths.)

Last weekend’s accident took place at the Stubai Glacier ski resort. The glacier sections of the Alps typically open for skiing weeks earlier than other mountain resorts, thanks to their higher altitudes and glacial ice, which can provide good October skiing with just a thin layer of fresh snow on top. Stubai’s ski resort opened on October 9 this year.

A map of Stubai Glacier ski resort with the area where the accident happened circled in red. Photo: Stubaier Gletscher website

The skier was on his own, descending in an out-of-bounds area below East Daunkogel Peak at around 11:30am. His tracks on the snow show he eventually abandoned the snow-covered glacier and ventured onto a steeper, rocky area. At some point, he broke a small slab of crusty snow which dragged him some 50 meters into an open bergschrund, where he fatally fell 20 meters down, according to the Lawinen Report.

The accident site, with the skier’s tracks visible,  and the movement of the snow slab — 50m long and 25m wide — marked in pink. Photo: Lawinen Report

Other skiers in the area saw the accident and raised the alarm, but the skier had died by the time they extracted him from the crevasse. The police said the slab was only 10cm to 30cm thick, and the slide was just 25 meters wide. The crevasse was 50 meters below the fracture point.

Avalanche risks are obvious on heavily loaded slopes, but the accident shows that even a thin layer of unstable snow can cause a fatal fall. In this case, the snow had fallen recently on the verglass ice over glacier-polished smooth rocks, and had no time to settle. This is a typical early-season condition in mountain ranges around the world.

The accident occurred at nearly 3,000m of altitude on a shady northeast slope, where the snow hardly transforms and settles during the short days of late fall. To make things worse, the report notes that wind had picked up that morning, creating a weak crust on top of loose, cold, fresh snow, prone to breaking.

The avalanche site on the following day, with most of the terrain covered by freshly drifted snow. Photo: LWD Tirol

The second factor was exposure: the skier might have easily survived the fall if the terrain had been a clean snow slope, but he was on rocky terrain, where a simple fall can result in serious injuries. Most of all, the crevasse below created what the report described as a “terrain trap.”

Fans of backcountry and ski touring are eager to hit the slopes with the first snowflakes, and the scarcely covered terrain may provide a false sense of safety from avalanches. But objective dangers go beyond the big powder avalanches and also include falls on icy slopes. In addition, the glaciers are dry after the summer, and the crevasses are wide open.

After a mild fall, snow is falling heavily in the Alps this week, significantly improving conditions for the region’s ski resorts. Most of them plan to open this weekend. The snow will also offer backcountry opportunities, but conditions will remain highly unstable.

Snowfall up to two meters is expected today in the Alps. Graphic by meteoexploration.com

The avalanche report today by the Tiroler Avalanche Service (LWD Tirol) shows low risk in the Austrian mountains, a remarkable risk (3 out of 5) in the Swiss Alps, and moderate risk (2 out of 5) in France.

The avalanche forecast for today, November 19, in the Alps shows remarkable (level 3-orange) risk, especially in Switzerland. Graphic: LWD Tirol

#8367. Family Offers $75,000 Reward to Save Climber Missing on Mount Fuji

Today, November 19, the Yamanashi Prefectural Police announced that they had ended the search for Chinese climber Chen Jie, 25, according to the Japanese national newspaper Mainichi Shimbun. Chen disappeared November 8 on the main Yoshida Trail after continuing alone up 3,776m Mount Fuji during its closed season.

Ironically, Chen belonged to China’s Blue Sky Rescue Team, a volunteer search-and-rescue organization. His mother told local media that he had participated in rescues in China and had some mountaineering experience, including trekking and climbing in Nepal and Indonesia.

#8365. LaCrosse Alphaburly Aero 2000 Boot Review: The Warmest Boot LaCrosse Has Ever Made

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 12:22 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Insulated rubber boots are warm and waterproof. But they’re also heavy, hard to walk in, and uncomfortable to wear for a long period of time — or at least they used to be.

Last week I got the chance to field test LaCrosse’s new Alphaburly Aero 2000 insulated rubber boot on a whitetail hunt in Saskatchewan. The program for this kind of hunt is as follows: A cheery Canadian guide drops you off at a ground blind somewhere way back in the bush; you sit there overlooking a small shooting window from dark to dark; you never leave the blind because the biggest buck of your life could show up at any moment.

Temperatures on this hunt ranged from the mid 20s to low 40s, which is chilly but not as cold as we were hoping for (temps in the teens with a good foot of snow really get the bucks moving during daylight up there, or at least so I’ve heard).

This was the perfect testing ground for a rubber boot that’s designed for cold-weather deer hunting, among other duties. Here’s how the new LaCrosse boots held up.

The heart of the Aero line is the ETPU midsole, which borrows its design from the high-performance running world. The midsole returns energy as your foot strikes the ground (or more simply put, the boots are springy). The effect is that the boots are incredibly comfortable to walk in. If you’ve ever walked, say a mile or so in heavy boots, you know that they can wear out your legs and feet quickly. But with the new LaCrosse design, the weight of the boots is offset by the springiness of the midsole.

But your feet won’t stay comfortable long if they’re cold. It’s common for rubber boot wearers to get sweaty feet early in the day, and then freeze later on. To combat this, LaCrosse is using a completely new type of insulation called Solarcore. This is a kind of aerogel, which is woven into the interior of the boot. It’s promoted as a very light, very warm kind of insulation. In fact these boots are up to a pound lighter than other insulated rubber boots in their class. That lighter weight makes them easier to walk in as well. Importantly, Solarcore also repels water, unlike Thinsulate. So if your feet do get sweaty or wet inside the boot, that moisture doesn’t soak into the insulation.

There is a Pro version of the boot, which is all rubber, but I prefer the neoprene. I think it’s more comfortable on those long, all day sits.

There’s also an 800g insulation version of the boot if you don’t anticipate hunting in very cold weather. If you’re going to mostly be hunting in the mid-20s and 30s, I’d say you’d be fine with the 800g boot. If you’re hunting in temps below that, or your feet get cold easily, go with the max insulation.

During any long sit in the cold, your feet and hands are most likely to get cold first. But over the four days that I hunted, essentially sitting still for 10 hours at a time, my feet never got cold. I wore a light wool sock with a heavy wool sock over it. No foot warmers. No heater. I was impressed with how well the Solarcore insulation worked.

After the Saskatchewan hunt I took the boots to my family deer camp in Wisconsin and did a morning of still hunting with them. As advertised, they were extremely comfortable to walk in. I stepped in a stream that was shin deep and stood in it for about 2 minutes (just as a test) and experienced no leaks.

Only time will tell how these boots will hold up over the long term. I think one benefit of the full rubber Pro version is durability. Any sort of neoprene can wear out with hard use. So if you’re planning on hiking in these boots in any sort of rugged terrain, you’d want to go with the Pro version.

These are expensive boots, but they are jam packed with new technology. They are a true upgrade from basic insulated rubber boots that we’ve all worn in the past. If you hunt in cold weather, if you want to walk comfortably in rubber boots, or if your feet get cold easily, the Alphaburly Aero 2000 is a good option to consider.

I’m a Midwesterner who likes winter and I think I could wear these boots in zero degree temps without fear of getting cold. If you really wanted a bulletproof setup, pair these boots with a pair of heated socks and never get cold feet again.

Alex Robinson is Outdoor Life’s editor-in-chief. He oversees an ace team of writers, photographers, and editors who are scattered across the continent and cover everything from backcountry sheep hunting to trail running.

#8362. Climbers Forge New Route on Mount Shiva Despite Limited Acclimatization

Benjamin Bosshard and Philippo Sala will never forget their first experience above 6,000m and the new route they opened with Stefan Siegrist on India’s Bahai Jar, commonly known as Mount Shiva (6,142m).

Expeditions in the Indian Himalaya are usually quiet while they take place, because satellite phones and InReach devices are prohibited. After returning home, however, Stefan Siegrist reported the details, and the team’s cameraman, Christoph Detschmann, posted a video summary of the expedition:

Siegrist had already visited the Chenab River Valley area in 2018 and climbed Shiva with Jonas Schild, but he still had designs on its unclimbed northwest ridge. At the end of September, he, Sala, and Bosshard reached base camp in the Kishtwar massif.

The three Swiss climbers were prepared to endure a long period of bad weather while acclimatizing. To their surprise, a good weather window opened right away. It was too good to let go, despite not being properly acclimatized and Sala and Bosshard’s lack of altitude experience. The window was expected to last just three days. After many “discussions and doubts,” they left base camp on October 3.

Sala had a headache during the first night, but the following morning, they were all totally focused on the climb, and the tension disappeared. “We assumed that we were unlikely to reach the summit on this attempt, and that we would therefore be wasting our only chance before a major storm [brought] the season to an end,” Siegrist explained. “So from then on, the idea was simply to climb higher and acclimatize, without any pressure.”

A moment of the climb, filmed from a drone by Christoph Detschmann

Along the way, they discovered they had left their stove at base camp. No problem in 2026: The expedition photographer used his drone to carry the stove up to them. The use of drones was a hot topic during the recent Piolets d’Or: To what extent are they compatible with alpine-style criteria? While most accepted that drones could scout routes or sections out of view of base camp, many considered using them to carry equipment up a face to be a red line.

On Shiva, Siegrist says the delivery of the missing stove “proved essential to the success of the climb.”

The climbers spent their second night at 5,250m at the base of the northwest ridge. On the following morning, they had to decide whether to continue or retreat. They all felt well, but starting the highly technical ridge was a point of no return. At the same time, Siegrist recalls, they realized they really had a chance to summit. And so on they went.

That night, they stopped at 5,700m in what Siegrist describes as a crucial moment. His partners had not been at that altitude before, and the latest forecast announced a storm the following day. They decided to wait and see what the morning looked like. No one slept at all that night.

The last meters before the summit. Drone image by Christoph Detschmann

The sky was still clear when the team set off again at 4am, so they chose to keep going. “After a while, we finally reached the final slope, just 300m below the summit,” Siegrist wrote. By now, they knew they were going to make it. They reached the top at 7:44am, six days after arriving in Base Camp, and just in time to hurry down as the storm approached.

The climbers descended via the 1985 Japanese route (see topo below), and reached their base camp tents as it started to snow.

#8360. Camino Day 21: Hórreos and the Father of the Modern Camino

We leave so early from Casa Cruceiro de Ferreiros that we have to unlock the door and let ourselves out. I can tell Suzanne is itching to go, but we only have one headlamp, which effectively yokes us until dawn, or first coffee. Then she will rabbit away. The last few days we’ve not walked together, which is not to say we’re walking alone—there are 1,000s of pilgrims to walk with.

The Way isn’t that different from days past, and the *turigrinos* are getting harder to distinguish from the rest of us. Yes, they have their cute little daypacks, but the clothes aren’t shiny anymore, and I see a weariness, a gift of several days on trail.

We walk by stone fences on rolling, rural lanes, and I try to imagine what it must have been like when resources were so slim that split stone was the material available for fences. There is a gentle roll to The Way, and we pass the 100.000k marker to Santiago—unremarkably just there, with no fanfare, like 1,000s of others. The end is 62 miles away!

Time and millions of pilgrims have worn down the Camino

I am so fascinated by the hórreos of Galicia

We walk between six-foot high dry lay stone fences, and later on a sunken path, worn down by this pilgrimage. I admire hórreos of every make: stone, brick, wooden, and a mix. They seem randomly dropped more than thoughtfully placed, at odd angles with what is around them. I know that many have been here since the 13th century, but neither they nor the landscape yield to one another. Hórreos evolved to dry millet until the arrival of the New World crop: corn. Galicia’s climatic peculiarities prevent grain from completely drying in the field. Done mechanically today, in years past the ears were shucked and stored whole to allow air to pass through, to cool and dry the grain to prevent spoilage and fermentation.

In an information sheet I found at tonight’s hostel, it reads:

In 1973, a Decree was approved by the Spanish state to protect all the old granaries and huts existing in Galicia and Asturias, which attempted to address the consequences of the abandonment of the rural way of life and the loss of use of the granaries.

I guess I won’t be transporting any hórreos home to my backyard.

We’re in pulpo territory, and find pulperías and copper cauldrons smoking away outside roadside bars. The Galician preparation for octopus is smoked paprika, sea salt, and a splash of olive oil, all served on a seven-inch wooden plate and eaten with toothpicks. Suzanne largely doesn’t partake because the octopus is known to be an intelligent sea creature; I’d feel bad too if they weren’t so tasty. Kinda like bacon—if they are so smart, then why so tasty? It is a tragic, delicious bargain we’ve made with nature’s smartest species. On a lighter note, we’ve stumbled into a soft homemade cheese, queso con miel, that is drizzled with local Galician honey, and the seasonal padrón peppers.

Padrón peppers – fried with olive oil and sprinkled with course salt.

I walk with David and his mother Maxine, from Australia. She is a 12-month cancer survivor, and as David tells the story, when she was at her lowest… she interjects “so exhausted I couldn’t even hold a cell phone” David pitched walking the Camino. That was 18 months ago, and here they are. I think back to other Aussies I’ve met, and each seemed to be recovering from a major health crisis.

Then, there was Fritz, born in Nebraska, but sent away to boarding school at a young age and never returned, living abroad and traveling the world in the business of finance. He tells me a different side of the story of how the modern Camino came to be. His friend was named Manuel Fraga Iribarne, who in the mid-1990s was the regional governor of Galicia, then one of the poorest, most isolated agricultural regions of Spain. At that time, the Camino was a quiet trail mostly walked by devout Catholics, historians, or avid hikers. (When they invent time travel, this is where I’m returning, to walk the Camino of those days). Working with Father Elías (the priest of O Cebreiro), who was the spiritual father and painted the yellow arrows to guide the way, Fraga had the political and marketing know-how to rebuild the infrastructure we enjoy today. One hears of Father Elías in Catholic stories, but not Manuel Fraga Iribarne. Thank you, Fritz.

This is his eighth Camino, and he is reluctantly walking it because it is a graduation requirement for the two-week course he took in Santiago. Fritz has been so interesting to listen to and walk with, but I recognize in him that, like myself, much of our success has been given to us through the privilege of the stations our parents gave us. As men, much of how we see ourselves is through our work, and Fritz, David, and the other unhappy conservative I walked with are all now retired. Their importance and reason for being is gone, and I think they are looking for something that replaces what they have lost. SH*T, I think to myself, this is the third man I’ve walked with that turns out to be angry and older—what is she, The Camino, trying to teach me?

He has seen the world and chooses to be angry. I see his unwavering support of the current administration being driven more out of retribution for the direction the country was going than any hope or vision for its future. If our country was a garden and overrun by weeds, his solution would be to drown it in Roundup and pave it over. No more weeds. My Dad would say that weeds are just misplaced plants and can have a lot to say diagnostically about the health of the soil. The goal isn’t to keep a weed-free garden, but to produce food, knowing that for the health of the soil, weeds must coexist.

So I walk with Fritz until I can’t. Yes, he is a fast walker, unencumbered, and fresh on the Camino, but I tire of his raw anger, in such contrast to the Anglican faith he professes. My exit comes when he sees his pickup van in case he is ready to stop walking. He goes over to check in and I, who have no pickup van, sit and rest. Fritz continues on, walking over the hill and out of my Camino never to be seen by me again.

#8358. Two Inexperienced Teens Rescued After 20 Hours on Grossglockner

Two Germans, aged 15 and 18, spent almost 20 hours on Austria’s highest mountain before being evacuated by helicopter on November 15.

Initially, police reported that the pair had reached the 3,798m summit and become lost while descending. The younger climber disputes this, saying they turned back near an alpine hut at 3,454m because his companion felt ill.

Their ordeal began around 11pm on Saturday, November 14. The pair had no previous experience with such a serious mountain but had prepared by reading websites, watching videos, training physically, and asking others for tips. They did carry crampons, an ice axe, a helmet, a rope, a belay device, and extra clothing.

It took them a long time before they decided to turn around. The descent went well at first, but one spot seemed too dangerous to them. To get around it, they followed someone else’s tracks in the snow, but these suddenly disappeared. While looking for a way, they got stuck in deep snow. The older boy called for help at around 6:30pm on November 15, according to Austria’s public broadcaster, ORF.at.

At first, rescuers tried to guide the pair down by phone. More than two hours later, the boys were still stuck, so a helicopter was dispatched. The rescuers found the pair, exhausted, disoriented, and hypothermic, at around 2,800m.

A rescue helicopter brings the teens to safety. Photo: Mountain Rescue KALS

Yesterday, rescue leader Peter Tembler criticized their lack of alpine experience and preparation. He told the German Press Agency that the pair lacked sufficient experience for the terrain and winter conditions. He also criticized their late-night departure.

According to Tembler, a section that normally takes four to five hours took them almost 12. Other climbers reportedly advised them against continuing.

Tembler believes an increasing number of inexperienced people are attempting serious alpine routes, sometimes encouraged by social media accounts that downplay the difficulty. The disagreement has become a public debate about risk-taking, responsibility, and rescue operations.

As Austria’s highest peak, Grossglockner is a magnet for climbers of all stripes and has more than its share of controversy. In January 2025, a man abandoned his less experienced girlfriend high on the mountain. She froze to death, and an Austrian court later convicted him of manslaughter for leaving her behind. This summer, an altercation occurred on Grossglockner in which a guide insulted a passing climber and nearly pushed him off a cliff. At the same time, veteran alpinists are still setting bold new routes here.

According to Der Standard, Grossglockner gets 8,000 to 9,000 ascents each year, with an average of two dozen rescues annually. Between 2005 and 2025, 29 climbers have died on the mountain.

#8357. Mountain Lion Is the Ultimate Underrated Game Meat. Here’s How to Prepare It

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 1:48 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Luke Ellsbury and his 16-year-old daughter had chased the lion for miles through a foot of snow in some of northwest Wyoming’s harshest country. Their bluetick-English coonhounds finally treed the cat on the far side of a steep ridge, about 7 miles from their truck. By the time they tagged the lion, they’d already been running for well over half the day, and they still had to skin and field dress it.

“There was no good horse trail or anything to travel in and out,” Ellsbury says. “Just getting to a lion is a lot of work.”

At that point, some lion hunters may have chosen to take the skull and cape and leave the meat, a practice that’s legal in some states like Wyoming and one that’s currently being leveraged to ban “trophy” mountain lion hunting in Colorado. Anti-hunters there are trying to persuade the voting public that hunters don’t eat mountain lion meat.

But Ellsbury, like plenty of cougar hunters, wanted the meat from his mountain lion. So, he and his daughter and two friends carefully removed the animal’s backstraps and quarters and packed them out, making the long haul back on that cold January day and reaching their truck after dark. Lion meat, Ellsbury says, is worth the work. Two years later, he’s still savoring the remaining packages of that lion’s meat. He made burnt ends out of it recently. “I’ve eaten my fair share of lion, and I really like it,” he says. “It’s pretty versatile and mild tasting.”

Ellsbury, a large carnivore biologist for the Wyoming Game and Fish Department, longtime houndsman, and former guide, doesn’t blame people for being gun shy about eating mountain lion. It is a cat, after all. But he and others insist it’s worth trying. And not unlike other wild game, from moose to bighorn sheep and chukars to sandhill cranes, mountain lion meat is best consumed while keeping a few best practices in mind. Here’s how to prepare and cook mountain lion meat.

Yes. Although mountain lion meat certainly isn’t as popular as wild game like whitetail deer or wild turkey, it is edible, tasty, and safe to eat when prepared properly. The meat from mountain lions and other predators, like black bears, has long carried a stigma because these critters eat other animals, which brings unfounded concerns about taste and safety. In reality, mountain lion meat tastes great, it’s lean, and relatively easy to cook, says Luke Worthington, a Wyoming hunter, guide, and founder of the Wyoming Houndsmen Association.

“It tastes a lot like pork,” Worthington tells Outdoor Life. “I do the old shake and bake with it, where I cube it up and put it in shake and bake and fry it.”

And that stigma around mountain lions has changed dramatically over the last 15 to 20 years. Worthington says his clients are becoming more interested in hauling out lion meat to take home. He credits a growing movement focused on eating other, more nontraditional types of wild game.

“I tell people, ‘This is good meat and edible meat, and it’s worth it. If you kill an animal, it’s worth taking the meat,’” he says. “The least you can do is try it.”

Most people who’ve eaten lion say the meat tastes much like a lean pork, with a mild or almost sweet flavor that is well suited to picking up other flavors in marinades, rubs, or sauces.

Ellsbury also says that while the fat from a deer or elk can be waxy and unpleasant tasting, lion fat — like bear fat — can be quite good. He likens lion fat to pork fat, although the two animals have vastly different quantities. (Lions are much leaner.)

A common concern that many people have with lion and bear meat is trichinosis, a food-borne disease caused by a parasitic worm that lives in meat-eating animals. The worm doesn’t seem to bother mountain lions, but it does affect humans and can be fatal if left untreated. Historically, most people contracted trichinosis from eating undercooked or raw pork, but advances in commercial food and farming regulations have largely lowered the risk, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. There is still a risk with meat from mountain lions, wild boar, and bears, but it’s minimal: the CDC reports that the U.S. only sees about 15 confirmed cases per year.

While the risk of contracting trichinosis to humans is low, it still exists. That means hunters need to be sure they kill those parasitic larvae before eating.

Read Next: 6 Wild-Game Rituals That Are About Much More Than the Meat

To safely prepare mountain lion meat, don’t cook it rare or medium-rare. Instead, cook it thoroughly to an internal temperature of 165 degrees Fahrenheit. The CDC also notes that “Curing (salting), drying, smoking, or microwaving meat alone does not consistently kill infective worms. Homemade jerky and sausage were the cause of many cases of [trichinosis] reported to CDC in the past.”

Because most lion hunters want the cape, Worthington recommends carefully skinning the animal before beginning to remove meat. From there, butchering a mountain lion is similar to quartering and butchering an ungulate. Just be sure to field dress your lion as quickly as possible. Because lions eat other animals, their guts can cause the meat to spoil quicker than a deer’s, Worthington says.

For hunters who are unable to pack out an animal whole, consider the gutless method and remove the front and hind quarters, backstraps, and edible portions of the neck.

Worthington cautions that, depending on the size of the cat, the front shoulders may not offer much meat, and the backstraps are often about as round as a hefty bratwurst. But he says they’re still worth saving to cook later.

Dan Thompson, Wyoming Game and Fish Department’s large carnivore section supervisor, cautions hunters to avoid a lion’s anal glands when skinning and field dressing the animal. The glands, used by cats and dogs to mark their territory, are pungent and can ruin the meat if punctured.

To prevent accidental infection from trichinosis, wear disposable gloves when handling the lion and meat, and wash your hands thoroughly when you’re finished.

Consider dividing meat into roasts, steaks, and ground burger. Ellsbury and Thompson both recommend slow cooking roasts in a crock pot with carnitas seasonings to make tender tacos, or with barbecue seasonings to create pulled lion sandwiches.

You can also marinate and grill steaks. But while you need to make sure the internal temperature reaches 165, be careful not to overcook lion meat as it can easily dry out and become tough.

If you’re looking to impress someone who might be skeptical of mountain lion meat, Thompson recommends this recipe for pork stuffed mushrooms by Steph Gaudreau. As with other recipes, you can easily substitute ground lion meat for ground pork.

Read Next: Calling Mountain Lions at Close Range Is a Predator Hunt Unlike Anything Else

#8354. Weekend Warm-Up: Hurricane Chase

This episode of the storm-chasing TV series Storm Rising is titled “High Plains Insanity.” Produced by National Geographic, it follows Reed Timmer and Mike Theiss as they attempt to navigate and document an increasingly unpredictable storm season.

The storm-chasing segments are filmed like an action movie but, as Timmer makes clear, thrills are not the primary objective. As the climate changes and disrupts weather patterns, tornadoes are no longer limited to traditional seasons and ranges.

“That’s why we need to be on the ground directly in the path of these tornadoes,” Timmer explains, “to record data that no one else is getting.”

Driving into a potential tornado in Montana. Photo: Screenshot

With hurricane and tornado seasons now overlapping, the duo is forced to split up to cover the widest possible area and collect the most data. For the tornadoes, this means deploying a probe into the funnel.

Up north in the High Plains of the U.S. West and Midwest, Timmer and his dog Gizmo narrowly miss storm after storm. Gizmo is better company but is a very poor navigator, while Timmer isn’t much better. He constantly makes wrong turns as he tries to navigate, drive, and prepare equipment. He does take the time to explain the basic weather science behind what he is failing to catch, such as the conditions that created the baseball-sized hail pummeling his windshield.

Timmer and Gizmo drive through the aftermath of a powerful tornado in the Black Hills of North Dakota. Photo: Screenshot

In Florida, Theiss sleeps in his car to maintain a constant vigil along the coast. Our current scientific abilities, he explains, allow us to track the movement of hurricane systems with decent accuracy. But predicting their strength is much more difficult. Systems can suddenly turn deadly or fizzle out with equally short notice. In this case, the terrible-looking storm comes to nothing. He heads back up north to rejoin Timmer and Gizmo.

Gizmo buffeted by fierce winds. Photo: Screenshot

The reunited trio prepares for a final effort, complete with a windshield-replacement sequence. They immediately drive into the gathering storm, passing through a hail core which, of course, destroys the new windshield. The Lord giveth and taketh away, et cetera. Soon they’re racing on a near-empty tank, as the powerful storm threatens to touch down at any moment.

It’s their last chance this season to deploy the probe. When, after a long, tense chase, they actually manage to do it, both erupt into cheers. Gizmo appears unmoved, despite the fact that this ground probe, deployed in a storm of this type, in this area, has never been deployed before and is sure to yield valuable scientific data.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

#8351. 💪 Into the High Sierra: ❄️ Snow, Ice & Forrester Pass 🏔️

I packed up from my stealth spot before daybreak and used the park bathroom to change and brush my teeth.

Sitting at a picnic table in the shade, I pulled up my blog. That’s when I realized I had forgotten to hit publish on the post I had scheduled for the previous week. I rushed to get it published, feeling frustrated with myself and the growing pressure to keep up with my writing. Once my blog was published online, I spent the remainder of the morning working on my next entry.

I picked at my leftover Chinese food for breakfast as I sorted through my resupply and packed my backpack for the trail.

Walking through town, I stopped at Carl’s Jr. for a Double Quarter Pounder with Cheese and a strawberry milkshake. I packed the burger away to eat later on the trail.

Before hitching out, I stopped at Elevation Gear, where I finished my ice cream and spent a few minutes visiting with Marble, Electrolyte, and Pickles while telling them my plans.

Walking along the road toward the Horseshoe Meadow turnoff, I dropped my pack to hitch. I stood with my thumb out, hoping for a ride. A few vehicles stopped to offer rides, but they were heading either to Whitney Portal or the Alabama Hills. A couple of other drivers pulled over only to tell me they didn’t have room.

The gestures were kind, but each time a vehicle stopped, my hopes went up. Having them dashed by another apology made the wait even more discouraging.

After 30 minutes with no luck, I grabbed my backpack and walked farther out of town.

A farmer working in a field across the street told me he had been watching me try to hitch a ride down the road. He wished me luck.

The driver, Taquito, told me he was a Triple Crown thru-hiker from Omaha. He was on vacation, hiking and providing trail magic to hikers. We talked about hiking, his Triple Crown experiences, and the trail magic he was sharing with hikers. He took me all the way to the Horseshoe Meadow trailhead.

At the trailhead, Taquito offered me even more trail magic in the form of Gatorade, an apple, and Gummy Clusters. Taking advantage of the nearby trash cans, I drank the Gatorade, ate the apple, and demolished my double burger before heading out on the trail. I tucked the Gummy Clusters away for later.

Leaving the trailhead at 4:30 p.m., I began my hike into the mountains. Lone Pine had been hot during the day, now, in elevation the evening air was getting cold. The loose, sandy trail made walking difficult, especially with my heavy backpack. I moved slowly, tired and a little stressed about finding a place to camp before the elevation started.

I spent too much time looking off-trail for flat spots. Two miles in, the trees began to grow thicker, I suddenly came across a sandy clearing with a rabbit. Seeing it hopping around so carefree made the area feel safe and inviting. I liked the idea of sharing this little clearing with the rabbit, so I decided this was where I would camp.

I used my newly acquired ice axe to scrape and level the sandy ground, making a flat spot for my tent.

For dinner, I made a Frito taco bowl with dehydrated pea-protein “beef” and dried refried beans. It hit the spot after a long day. With a full belly, a newly leveled tent spot, and a rabbit for company, I crawled into my tent. Too tired to write, I fell asleep around 8 p.m. with a bit of daylight still lingering. I had a long way to go, but at least I was back in the mountains.

I woke up at 3 a.m. to a dark, chilly morning. I was camped on the Cottonwood Pass Trail, 2.6 miles from its junction with the PCT at Mile 751.4. Nearly 1,200 feet lower in elevation. I opened one of my tent vestibules to help ventilate the condensation before burrowing back into my quilt for a little more rest.

At 4:15 a.m., I woke to the howling of coyotes. It made me smile. I love being in nature. Still tucked into my quilt, I reached for my headlamp in the top pocket of my backpack, where I always keep it. Although I normally sleep with it around my neck, I knew exactly where to find it.

While getting breakfast ready, I also pulled out my snacks for the day. I try to eat my heavier, bulkier foods first to lighten my pack. As I was packing up, I spotted Spicy Bite (Southern California) hiking toward town. He graciously offered to carry out my trash. Everything was inside a Ziploc bag, including half a family-size bag of Fritos that I had hiked in. I slipped the bag into his backpack without making him take it off. One less thing to carry!

A little after 7 a.m., I was hiking across a green, grassy meadow covered in frost. The sun hadn’t yet risen over the mountains, and the air grew colder as I approached a stream. After crossing it, I looked up at the steep mountainside ahead. I checked my map, trying to figure out where the trail passed through the snow-covered pass above. I began preparing myself for the climb.

The trail entered a pine forest and began to switchback.

The sun began to warm the earth. Chipmunks scurried over the white boulders and fallen logs along the trail. At the last switchback before the top, I stopped for a break. I was above 11,000 feet and beginning to notice a slight headache coming on. After five minutes, I pushed on to the top and finally rejoined the PCT.

Just minutes later, I stopped at the first trickle of flowing water. My headache was slowly developing, and my stomach wasn’t feeling right. I needed to hydrate and give myself time to adjust before continuing farther into the Sierras. I spent the next hour and a half sitting beside the trail at 11,132 feet, drinking water and trying to stay warm. My headache improved slightly, but I still wasn’t feeling quite right.

Pushing to higher elevations can be dangerous. In 2022, I had been just a few days behind a young hiker who lost her life on Forester Pass due to altitude-related issues. That experience had stayed with me and reminded me not to take the mountains lightly.

As I packed up, I noticed another hiker continuing past the Lone Pine exit. It was Diva (Tennessee), who had already built up his trail legs after hiking the Appalachian Trail and then the Florida Trail over the past year. He was planning to continue north to Kearsarge Pass.

We hiked together to the frozen Chicken Spring Lake, where we met a group of local high school students and their teachers. The students were about to graduate and were on a 20-day backpacking trip.

Having company lifted my spirits, even though I still wasn’t feeling 100%. Before leaving the lake, I noticed my feet were feeling swollen and my shoes were getting tight. With the altitude already on my mind, it was another thing I was paying attention to. I took an ibuprofen, collected a little more water, and said goodbye as Diva continued north.

As I climbed over the next pass and entered Kings Canyon National Park, the landscape opened up to beautiful views. A meadow stretched out to my left, while the trail wrapped around the mountainside. To my right, the majestic pink and white rock walls of Cirque Peak towered above me at 12,900 feet.

I stopped for lunch and noticed dark clouds gathering in the distance. I moved more snacks into my hip-belt pocket. With most of my elevation gain behind me for the day, I wasn’t too concerned about being caught in a storm while exposed on a climb, though I did pick up my pace.

As thunder rumbled in the dark clouds, I hurried to the next water source. I found a few flat spots nearby and collected water, letting my gravity filter do its work while I set up my tent. With the storm moving in, I wanted to get settled before it arrived.

Happy with my setup, I was about to put my gear inside when I looked up. Above my tent, I saw one dead tree leaning against another, with yet another dead tree on the opposite side. With thunder sounding closer, I quickly decided to move my tent. I relocated to a completely different spot a short distance away.

Another hiker, Scott (New Zealand), came down the trail. He was planning to continue a few more miles before setting up for the night. I finally threw my gear into my tent just as it started to hail.

Small hailstones continued falling steadily. I occasionally bumped the sides of my tent to knock off the accumulating ice. It was only 5 p.m., but I was happy to be inside and dry, with enough water for the night.

I boiled water and mixed half a Mountain House pepperoni pizza meal with half a chicken alfredo meal.

After the hail stopped, the golden sun came streaming through the pine trees. Tiny white pieces of hail were scattered across the ground, contrasting with the beautiful green landscape.

I brushed my teeth and placed my bear can behind a large fallen tree as the sun dipped below the mountains.

Crawling into my tent, I thought about how far I had come. I was proud of myself for making it this far on the trail and stepping into the high-altitude Sierras. It had taken bravery and strength to venture this far into the wilderness on my own. Even with the cold, I was genuinely happy to be surrounded by such a beautiful place. I felt grateful to be there, with a deep respect for the mountains and the wilderness I am fortunate enough to experience.

At 3:45 a.m., my alarm pulled me out of sleep. I wanted to eat up any food that required cooking today so that tomorrow, when I pushed over Forrester Pass, I could just grab bars and snacks and keep moving.

After boiling water, I made oatmeal and a mix of hot cocoa and coffee.

Part of the reason I struggle to hike with others is that I’m a slower hiker and take longer to get out of camp. I get frustrated when I’m the last one to leave, and that frustration can often affect my attitude throughout the day.

This morning, I realized that taking my time getting out of camp was a form of self-care. When I give myself time to get everything done without rushing, I’m happier and more relaxed. I also know I’ve taken care of everything at camp and haven’t forgotten any chores.

At 7 a.m., I continued my hike. The trail descended toward Rock Creek, where it opened into a beautiful grassy valley surrounded by snow-covered mountains. Water was flowing everywhere. It was a completely different landscape from the desert I’d been hiking through the past two months.

As I approached Rock Creek, I passed established campsites, a few with hikers still packing up. The creek was rushing, and the morning air was cold.

The stepping stones used later in the year were covered in inches of rushing water. Looking downstream, I spotted an old fallen tree with no bark. It would likely be slippery with the frost that morning. A little farther down, I saw a newer-looking fallen tree across the creek. Thinking this might be an option, I started climbing onto it, but some of the bark slipped off, revealing a smooth, slippery surface. Looking farther along the log, I noticed more sections where the bark was missing.

I hiked back upstream, past the trail, but didn’t find any better options for a dry crossing. I felt most comfortable getting my shoes wet, hoping that if I crossed on the rocks, I could at least keep the rest of my clothing dry.

I tried stepping across the rocks first. As soon as both feet were on the slippery rocks, I decided it would be better to just cross the creek. Plunging my legs into the freezing current, the water reached my knees. Even part of my rolled-up pants got wet as I climbed out on the other side.

I met a NOBO JMT hiker named Will (California), who had taken off his shoes to cross the creek and was putting them back on.

At the top of the next hill, I took a break on a boulder with a view, dreaming of French toast with syrup, maybe even peanut butter.

Scott from the day before walked up while I was taking a break. This was when we officially met.

Hiking through a rocky boulder field, I heard black-capped Chickadees chirping something that sounded like the word “cheeseburger.” Every time I heard it, I couldn’t help but laugh and found myself getting hungry!

Reaching Crabtree Meadow, I crossed Whitney Creek using a log. I felt a little uneasy about my balance with my backpack and how high the log was above the creek, but I made it across. I was proud of myself!

After crossing Whitney Creek, I passed the first trail junction leading to Mount Whitney

The PCT climbed through exposed switchbacks with some great views. I stopped halfway up to apply sunscreen.

Passing the second junction to Whitney, I was slightly tempted to take it, but continued hiking the PCT. I didn’t have enough food to add the extra day and a half, and the last forecast I’d read was predicting snow in a few days.

I picked my way through some patchy snow before arriving at Wallace Creek. After hiking up and down the creek looking for a dry crossing, I couldn’t find one. This time I took off my shoes, socks, calf compression sleeves, and gaiters before crossing.

The creek was cold, the rocks were slippery, and the water was high enough to get the bottom of my shorts a little wet.

I dried my feet with a bandanna and let them air out for a bit before putting everything back on.

Clouds started rolling in as the afternoon continued. I needed to push to get as close to Forrester Pass as I could.

As I approached the climb up Big Horn Plateau, the clouds grew closer, and I could hear thunder rumbling. Seeing a cluster of trees about 300 feet off the trail, I crossed a small creek to take a break, eat a snack, and assess the storm.

With a snack in my stomach, most of the dark clouds had moved off to the south, though I could still hear thunder rumbling in the distance. I ventured back to the trail, still cautious as I headed toward a treeless area at 11,056 feet, with a metal bear canister in my backpack.

As I climbed onto Big Horn Plateau, it began to snow. The clouds obscured some of the panoramic views of the Sierra mountains, though I could still see much of the surrounding landscape. I was exhausted and cold, and I wasn’t particularly happy about being out there in the weather. But stopping wasn’t even a consideration.

My last obstacle for the day was Tyndall Creek. One of the trail notes mentioned a more manageable crossing about a half-mile upstream. As I started heading upstream, a hiker on the other side spotted me and came down to the creek.

I didn’t see him right away and couldn’t hear him over the rushing water. He pointed toward the spot where the trail crossed and told me to just cross there.

Again, I took off my shoes, socks, gaiters, and calf compression sleeves before stepping into the frigid water.

There were a few small groups of hikers scattered among the campsites. I found a semi-flat, semi-secluded spot and set up camp. I made dinner and got ready for bed. Tomorrow was going to be an early start. I was a little nervous about Forrester Pass. I had 5 miles to the top and didn’t want to be plunge-stepping down the north side in the afternoon. I was glad I wouldn’t have to cross Tyndall Creek in the morning.

At 3:00 a.m., I woke to bright stars in the cold morning sky. I mixed coffee into cold water and packed snacks into my pockets. After packing up camp, I took off my puffy and hit the trail at 4:40 a.m.

Hiking in the early morning light, I didn’t need my headlamp. The colors in the sky were stunning.

The trail crossed small snowmelt creeks, weaving in and out of stream crossings. At one point, the melting snow had created a stream right across the trail.

My feet crunched over the frozen ground, and I slipped a bit on a patch of ice. I realized I needed to be more careful crossing snow bridges, especially with rushing water underneath.

The only person I saw that morning was Scott, who passed me. I was still hoping to find a hiking buddy for the pass, and I was glad everything was so frozen.

As I continued climbing, I kick-stepped up increasingly steep icy fields, trying to avoid the boulders that were beginning to melt and weaken the snow in the morning sun.

As I looked up in awe at 13,897-foot Junction Peak next to it, Forrester Pass, at 13,120 feet, loomed ahead like a boss. I could see two people crossing over its scary icy chute above.

Just then, I heard someone hiking on the ice behind me. I was surprised to see Scott again. He had taken a break off trail, and I must have passed him at some point.

I told Scott we should hike together up and over the pass, and he agreed. I’m sure he did it just so I would feel more comfortable hiking it. Once we were out of the valley bowl, the switchbacks were mostly clear. They received afternoon sun each day.

As I made my way toward Forrester Pass, I felt a mix of intimidation and excitement. The climb ahead was daunting, and the icy terrain made me nervous, especially the chute. I was more worried about crossing the chute than the north side, although it would turn out that I probably should have been more concerned about the other side. Still, I felt confident in my ability to tackle the challenge. Reaching Forrester Pass would be a huge milestone, both physically and personally. It was the highest point on the PCT, and I was excited to see what it would take to get there.

When we finally reached the ice chute, I watched Scott scoot across the iced-over, foot-shaped holes in the ice. I questioned whether I should take the time to put on my microspikes. I hadn’t worn them yet on the trail.

My heart started to race. I secured my microspikes to the bottom of each shoe and exchanged one of my trekking poles for my ice axe. I crossed the ice chute one foot at a time, never looking down and maintaining three points of contact. Just like that, I was on the rocky trail on the other side.

Thrilled to be over the ice chute, I hollered down into the valley below. Just a few more switchbacks to the highest point on the PCT!

As I attempted to take a drink of my coffee, I discovered that the liquid around the lid had frozen, and there were chunks of ice inside the bottle. Thirsty as I was, I laughed. How often do you get a frozen coffee with a view like this?

As I crested the pinnacle of the PCT, Forrester Pass, I hollered at the top of my lungs in excitement. We stopped to take in the view and take photos on the blisteringly cold pass. Afterward, we decided it was too icy to glissade, so we would hike down instead. There was more snow on the north side because it received less sunlight during the early spring months. I was glad I had my microspikes. Scott didn’t have any.

Unable to see the trail, we followed footprints as we switchbacked down the ridge, eventually ending up farther out than we should have. Unaware that we were that far off trail, I told Scott I would probably be fine from there, and he picked up his pace.

Following the footprints, we ended up at the edge of the ridge. Scott climbed down over the snow and rocks and continued on. Dark, foggy clouds began rolling in. I hesitated, wondering whether I should backtrack alone to look for the trail or attempt to follow him. I was still reluctant to use my ice axe.

Just then, Scott slipped and slid, landing in a pile of rocks. He yelled out that he was hurt.

Thankfully, Yak and Gezelle were already below us and rushed over to help. I tossed them my first-aid kit.

I decided it was unsafe to try to climb down with my heavy, off-balance backpack, so I took it off and lowered it down using my bear line.

By the time I safely reached the bottom, Scott had already continued hiking on his own. The girls were packing up the first-aid kit and shouldering their packs as two young hikers who had just glissaded down the pass hiked toward us.

They had been hiking with the girls and were fast hikers. We were really concerned about Scott, even though he had an SOS button. The guys hiked ahead to try to catch up with him.

The three of us women continued hiking downhill, passing stunning teal-blue alpine lakes and crossing streams alongside the roaring Bubbs Creek. We were surrounded by some of the most epic views of beautiful white granite peaks.

At Mile 784, at an elevation of 10,961 feet, I took off my microspikes. Eventually, most of the snow was gone, replaced by puddles along the rocky trail and the occasional large snowdrift that I had to plunge-step through.

Below 10,000 feet and back in the trees, the girls stopped for a break while I continued hiking. When I reached Bubbs Creek, I noticed a downed tree upstream. I decided to take my shoes off and wade across instead. Waterfalls cascaded everywhere, and the creek was rushing with so much water. Along the trail, I also spotted a young buck.

two guys from earlier, who were taking a break on the side of the trail. They told me Scott was still alive and hadn’t bled out. This was where they had caught up to him, and he was still hiking at a good clip. I was happy to hear the news and relieved that he was doing okay.

With my early start, my quilt had some condensation on it when I packed up. I considered stopping to dry it out, but noticed a cooler breeze and clouds starting to roll in from the west. I wondered if we might get some precipitation.

I continued down the switchbacks toward Vidette Meadow. I was exhausted by the time I reached the trail junction, where there was a bear box. I found a spot a little farther off the trail and set up my tent around 12:30 p.m.

I collected water and filtered 1.75 liters. After making my Chicken and Vegetable Rice Mountain House meal and having cocoa coffee, I settled in for a nap from 1:00–2:30 p.m. While I napped, my body heat helped dry the condensation from my quilt.

After waking, I decided to stay at the lower elevation rather than climb higher because the weather was uncertain. I spent the afternoon blogging and relaxing at camp. I was almost out of food, and after eating a big lunch, I settled for some candy and made tea. Around 7 p.m., I fell asleep again.

At 11:30 p.m., I woke to the sound of precipitation hitting my tent. It was barely sleeting. I checked the tension on my tent, then curled back into my quilt.

In the morning, my tent was covered in a very thin layer of sleet that had slightly melted on contact and stuck to the tent. One guyline had slipped out of place, and a corner of the tent was sagging under the weight. The ground was dusted with tiny sleet pellets and snow.

Before putting on my shoes, I slipped a twist-tie snack bag over each foot. If my feet got wet in these temperatures, I might have trouble keeping them warm. I wanted to do everything I could to keep them dry.

Around 7:00 a.m., I left camp, making fresh tracks on the trail.

Not long after starting, I arrived at a wide stream feeding into the roaring Bubbs Creek. I picked my way across its multiple channels, stepping on fallen logs and large rocks while using my trekking poles for balance.

I arrived at a trail junction, turned right, and began climbing out of the canyon. As I gained elevation, the trees began to thin, and the terrain gave way to solid rock. As I switchbacked across the cliffs, I had a beautiful view of Kings Canyon and could see the valley I had hiked down the day before. Forrester Pass itself was hidden behind a mountain.

The Kearsarge Pinnacles, along with East and West Vidette Mountains, towered over the valley, dusted with snow. The scenery was so beautiful that it hardly looked real, with every detail highlighted by the white snow.

Crossing flowing streams and seasonal snowmelt, I was glad I had put the bags over my feet that morning. My shoes got damp, but my feet stayed dry and warm.

There was a brief relief from the climbing as I passed the first trail junction toward Bullfrog Lake and Kearsarge Pass. The plateau was flat and sandy, with a few scattered pine trees. I could see unfamiliar mountains towering in the distance and hear what sounded like military jets booming overhead, though I couldn’t see a single one.

I took the second trail junction, adding 7.6 bonus miles up and over Kearsarge Pass to the trailhead. As I climbed, the trees became increasingly sparse. Near the cutoff leading back down to Bullfrog Lake, I noticed a ranger’s note posted on a rock. It was titled “Bear Info” and warned hikers that bears in the area had gotten food from people and would continue trying to get more. It reminded hikers never to leave food unattended and to keep all food and scented items secured.

The note also included some rather unusual advice about what to do if you encountered a bear!

At 10:00 a.m., I reached Kearsarge Pass at 11,760 feet after hiking five miles and gaining 2,200 feet in elevation.

Inside the Sierras, the skies were partly cloudy with patches of blue. On the Highway 395 side, the sky was cloudy and gray, and I could see a storm blowing in from the north. I was relieved that I had beaten the storm to the pass.

I met a hiker named Crush who was hiking back over the pass. Just then, it started to lightly snow, and it continued throughout the rest of my hike. I was cold as I began the long switchbacks down toward Onion Valley. Big Pothole Lake was covered in ice, its surface a deep blue. On the other side of the lake, three people were practicing self-arresting with their ice axes on the snow.

Passing Gilbert Lake, I noticed patches of green grass and scattered trees around the partially frozen lake. The three men I’d seen practicing with their ice axes passed me on the trail. I was too shy to ask for a ride, thinking they would be long gone by the time I reached the trailhead.

As the trail continued downhill, I had views of waterfalls cascading down the steep valley walls. In the final mile, the parking lot came into view. I could still see the three men, so I picked up my pace, hoping they were still at the trailhead. If they were, I was going to ask for a ride.

When I reached the parking lot, I hurried over to where the three men were sitting in their vehicles with the windows down. Thankfully, they had been waiting for me!

Frank and his sons had permits for Mount Whitney on Friday, weather permitting, and had been practicing with their ice axes at elevation. They were heading to Lone Pine in their two vehicles and offered me a ride.

I stopped at the post office in Lone Pine to pick up my new base layer. Frank and his sons then dropped me a few blocks away at the gear store, where I repacked my backpack.

I was hoping to get a bunk at The Hostel California in Bishop. The hostel was first come, first served, and with cellphone service issues in the area since the windstorm back at Kennedy Meadows South, I wasn’t able to secure a spot ahead of time.

I hiked to the north edge of town and stuck out my thumb, hoping to catch a ride toward Bishop.

Someone pulled over, but they were only going as far as Independence. I thanked them but decided to hold out for a ride all the way to Bishop.

Mark, from Tehachapi, picked me up. He was heading past Bishop to retrieve his family’s RV at June Lake. His family had been camping there with their trailer when they decided to drive into Yosemite the previous day. It ended up snowing, and Tioga Road closed, leaving them unable to get back to their trailer. They had to drive home to Tehachapi, and Mark was now making the trip back to retrieve the RV.As we approached Bishop, I called The Hostel California to check for a bunk. Unfortunately, they were full. Not sure where to go that wouldn’t cost me over three bills, I asked Mark to drop me off at Smart & Final.

I picked up a bagged salad, a pint of ice cream, a gallon of milk, and a box of cereal for dinner and breakfast. Then I started walking down the snowy streets of Bishop.

Hotel prices were high because the Mule Days festival had just taken place. Heartbroken that the hostel was full and unsure whether I knew any other hikers in town, I started walking down the snowy main street, going from motel to motel asking for a hiker discount.

By my third stop, I was struggling to hold back tears. If only I knew someone in town I could split a room with. The snowy weather had left the streets nearly empty.

After leaving my fourth motel, I was considering stealth camping when an angel named Erine pulled over. She had seen me at the store earlier and noticed me wandering down the street. She was a local teacher.

She asked where I was headed. Not really sure myself, I explained that I was looking for a motel that might offer a discount for hikers.

When we arrived at the motel, Erine, the angel of a woman, paid for my room. She also made sure I got my backpack and groceries inside quickly because of the precipitation.

Before leaving, she gave me her phone number in case I needed anything while I was in Bishop and gave me a hug. Then she was gone.

After taking a shower and doing some laundry, I spiderwebbed my bear line around the room. There weren’t many good places to tie it off, but I managed to create a makeshift clothesline.

I spent some time blogging before eating half the salad for dinner. I also brushed the caps of my two Smartwater bottles, which I had been carrying since Mexico. It’s a chore I try to do in every town.

I fell asleep in a soft, warm bed with the TV on.

The skies had cleared up in town. You could still see fresh snow covering the mountains, with clouds beginning to move back over them. I spent my morning eating cereal, drinking coffee, and blogging. It felt good to have time to catch up without feeling so rushed and to have a little less stress on my shoulders.

I received a group message and found out that Google was in town staying at the Motel 6. He offered to split his bed with me for the night.

When checkout time came, I walked down the road with my backpack to the Motel 6. Google and two other hikers had been there for days, and their gear was scattered around the room. I threw my backpack in a corner away from everyone else’s gear.

There was a group wanting to go to Keough’s Hot Springs outside of town. I was unsure if I wanted to go, still having chores to do before leaving town. With snow still predicted in the mountains that day, I had no excuse not to go.

With no rideshares available, we started walking down the road and eventually found a hitch who even offered to take us the few miles off the main road to the hot springs.

Once there, we entered the bathhouse. There was a large, warm pool for swimming and a smaller, hot, shallow pool for soaking. The warm pool had no roof, offering a glimpse of the surrounding scenery. Dark storm clouds hung over the Sierra peaks, making being in the warm water feel even better.

After spending a few hours at the pool, most of it soaking in the hot water and enjoying a pretzel Google shared with me, the group walked the mile back to Highway 395 to hitch a ride to Bishop.

The storm had given the desert valley a beautiful, moody feel, with clouds draped over the White Mountains in front of us.

After returning to the motel with my resupply box, I set it aside to deal with tomorrow. I still hadn’t decided how many days of food I would need for the next stretch. With several challenging mountain passes ahead, I wanted to take some time to make sure I was prepared for what was coming. I spent the rest of the evening relaxing in the room while the others headed out for pub trivia and bowling. It was nice to have a quiet evening before heading back into the mountains.
