#8347. The Savage Model 99 Is Still One of the Greatest Lever Action Rifles of All Time

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 2:47 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

In the early years of the twentieth century whitetail populations were making a comeback and deer hunting was a growing sport. Lever action rifles ruled the woods for at least the first half of the century and beyond. Even in my early years of hunting in the late sixties, lever actions seemed to fill the gun rack in every deer camp. The most popular was the Winchester Model 94, usually in .30-30, and this rifle dominated the deer woods.

While the 94 was the gun for the masses, the gun guys, the hunters who “knew stuff” had a different pick. The Savage Model 99 rifle was often their choice. While the tube magazine on the Winchester and Marlin lever actions limited the rifles to use with blunt nose bullets, with its rotary box magazine the Savage 1899 could use pointed bullets for a ballistic advantage. It was also strong enough to handle powerful cartridges using that newfangled smokeless powder.

The lever action Model 99 featured an internal hammer and many advanced features like a cocking indicator and even a counter to show the number of cartridges in the magazine. It was simply a more sophisticated design than the other lever actions. The introduction of the .250 Savage cartridge for the Model 1899 in 1915 ushered a new world of deer cartridges. At the time most cartridges barely broke the 2,000 ft/s barrier, but this one hit 3,000 ft/s with an 87-grain bullet. The savviest hunters recognized the advantage over the big, slow, hard kicking, straight walled black powder cartridges that were still in vogue at that time.

Five years later in 1920, Savage changed the name to the Model 99 and introduced the .300 Savage, a cartridge that at the time matched the ballistics of the .30-06. With these advanced cartridges the Savage Model 99 rifle became the thinking man’s deer rifle.

Prior to WWI bolt action rifles were almost unheard of for deer hunting and the Savage Model 1899 lever action rifle led the industry in innovation and performance. Even after the war when bolt action sporting rifles began to emerge into the market, the Savage remained one of the most popular hunting rifles on earth.

So how did this rifle come to be? As with many of our great firearms in history it was because of one visionary, Arthur William Savage.

Savage was born in Kingston, Jamaica on May 13, 1857. He received an education in the United States and in England, which was probably designed to have him follow in his father’s footsteps and become a government bureaucrat.

But Savage wanted adventure, so as soon as they handed him his diploma he hightailed it for Australia to look for black opals and interesting experiences, as Teddy Roosevelt called them.

At some point he was captured by aborigines and held captive for a year. One version of the story says that his captors demanded a ransom, but his family refused to pay. The historian was unable to provide details as to why, but I suspect that Christmas dinners with his family were a bit tense after his escape.

Savage explored the wild Australian interior in a covered wagon. Later he became the owner of the largest cattle ranch down under. He married Annie Bryant while there and eventually had four sons and four daughters. After eleven years in Australia, Savage returned to Jamaica at the age of 34 to grow coffee.

Savage loved firearms and machinery. He was always tinkering on new ideas. He improved the British service rifle. Among his inventions was a torpedo that he sold to the government of Brazil. He also developed the recoilless rifle and the detachable rifle magazine. Savage soon recognized that the future was in America, not Jamaica.

He traveled to New York where he took a job managing the Utica Belt Line railroad in Utica, NY. In his spare time he developed a hammerless, lever-action rifle. He patented the design in 1893 with an unwavering belief that this gun would lead the way into a new world of firearms. With a few investors, he founded Savage Arms in 1894.

It took courage to open a gun company in a field that was dominated by Winchester, Remington, and Colt, but Savage recognized that the newly invented smokeless powder was about to change the rifle world and that the low-pressure, big-bore, rifle cartridges were going to become obsolete. Europe was already using smokeless powder in some cartridges and here in America in 1892, the Army had adopted the .30-40 Krag as our first smokeless powder cartridge. Some sporting rifles were chambered for this new cartridge and Savage could see the future was with small bore, high velocity smokeless powder cartridges.

The first Savage rifle was the Model 1892, although it never got into production. Savage built it to compete for a military contract, which ultimately went to the .30-40 Krag bolt action. Savage continued to work on the design and it evolved into the Model 1895. That rifle won a contract to supply rifles for the New York National Guard. But politics prevailed, as it always has and probably always will in NY, so the contract was cancelled. The poor soldiers were sent off to fight in the Spanish-American war with obsolete single-shot trapdoor Springfield rifles.

With the Model 1895 rifle Savage also introduced a new cartridge, the .303 Savage, a bottle-necked, .30-caliber (.311 diameter bullet) cartridge designed for smokeless powder. Savage created the cartridge with a military contract in mind, but that never developed so he turned to the sporting rifle market.

The Model 1895 rifle, which was reported to have been built by Marlin Firearms Company for Savage, was revolutionary as it was designed to use modern, bottle-necked, smokeless powder cartridges and could handle pointed bullets. The new, smaller .303 Savage cartridge had to compete with the physically larger and more popular cartridges of the day, like the .45-70 Government. At the time the public still equated killing power with physical size and convincing them that technology would allow this diminutive cartridge to perform better than those claiming brute size as their biggest asset would define the struggle for success.

Proving he understood marketing as well as design, Savage started running testimonials in his catalog about the effectiveness of the new cartridge. In those days the catalog not only displayed the products, but it was a source of information for hunters and gun owners in the era before internet, television, or radio. Savage recognized that and ran first person accounts of one-shot kills with the .303 Savage on moose, deer, caribou, sheep and even grizzly bears. But the clincher was from an Alaska resident who killed a whale with a single shot from the .303 Savage.

The Model 1895 evolved into the Model 1899 rifle. That went on to become of the most successful hunting rifles in history. On March 22, 1960 Savage presented the NRA with the one millionth rifle made. No one is quite sure about the total sales number, but it would continue to be made for 38 more years.

A little known fact is that Savage did much of the development work on his early rifles using the machinery at the Remington factory. It’s said that he offered his lever action design to Remington.

Remington and Winchester were owned by the same company at the time and it’s thought that Remington was being locked out of the lever action market by upper management. Winchester didn’t want the competition. It’s what led to Remington developing their semi-auto and pump action line of hunting rifles.

But if you think about it, if those in charge had a little more foresight and had allowed Remington to dip a toe into the lever action market the Model 99 would have had the Remington name on it and Savage Arms might never have existed.

Savage was a restless soul and in 1904 he sold his interest in the company to a group of businessmen and headed west to California. There he started the Savage Tire Company, which grew to employ as many as 2,000 people at one time. It’s said that he invented the radial tire. Later he would have citrus groves, drill for oil and mine for gold. He died in 1938 at the age of 81. His was a life well lived and his lasting legacy would be the Model 99 rifle.

Savage Arms of course continued on without Arthur Savage and by the 1920s it became the largest firearms company in the free world.

In what was probably the start of a Savage tradition, the Model 1899 and 99 rifles were made in a dizzying number of models and configurations. At one time there were more than 700 SKUs of the Savage Model 110 rifle. I am not sure the Model 99 matched that, but it sure tried. Entire books have been written about the various models of this rifle.

There were octagon and half octagon barrels. Carbines and rifles with barrel lengths from 20 inches to 26 inches. The military musket had a 28-inch barrel. The 1895 had a 30-inch tube. There was even a model with a .410 shotgun barrel. Some had a saddle ring and others had deluxe wood. Still others had deluxe wood and fancy engraving. Some rifles had cut checkering and many had no checkering at all. In later years as they tried to cut production costs and ultimately ruined the rifle, some of those stocks had pressed in rifling. Some 99s had straight levers and grips, but most had curved levers with a pistol grip. The forends ranged from Schnabel to a plane Jane straight forend. Some rifles were take-down and others were not. Some came with tang mounted peep sights and others with a very innovative scope mount. It wasn’t until 1950 that Savage started drilling and tapping the rifles at the factory for scope mounting. Some carbine models had a barrel band, but most models did not. The rotary magazine was at some point modified to remove the counting window and the traditional brass was replaced with steel. In 1965 they introduced the “C” model which featured a detachable “clip” magazine. (Yes, they called it a clip. No matter what they tell you on the internet, “clip” was a commonly used term to describe removable magazines in sporting rifles for decades.)

The Savage Model 99 features a hammerless design with a much faster lock time than a traditional lever action. The lever is connected to the breech bolt by a long, wide, curving piece of metal. This gives the rifle a distinctive profile when the action is open. The breech block locks into the action as the lever is closed creating a strong action design that is suitable to modern high-pressure cartridges. While it’s not often noted, the Model 99 action is a controlled round feed design. That means that the extractor captures the cartridge as it exits the magazine and holds it against the bolt face during its travel into the rifle’s chamber. There is a cocking indicator in the form of a pin that sticks out of the top of the action when the gun is cocked and recedes inside when it is not.

The stock is two pieces with separate forend and buttstock. The bottom of the metal action is rounded which makes this rifle a delight to carry in your hand.

The safety is a small catch on the right side of the lever. Pushed forward it locks the lever shut and prevents the rifle from firing. Pulled back it is in the fire position. This is opposite of the more common safeties seen on modern rifles where back is safe and forward is fire. Shooters were smart enough to know the difference back then. Sometime in the mid-sixties a top-tang shotgun style safety was added on some models, but the traditional style lever locking safety remained more popular.

The .303 Savage was the first cartridge introduced in 1895. It was more or less a ballistic twin to the .30-30 introduced the same year. These were the first sporting cartridges designed for smokeless powder and it’s debatable which one was first. The .303 Savage is a handloading-only deal now, but I have always wanted one just because. My friend Lane Pearce just bought one and called to gloat. I hate him.

The .22 Savage High Power was next. It was designed by Charles Newton and introduced in the Model 99 in 1912. It was based on the .25-35 Winchester cartridge necked down to take a .228-diameter bullet. With a 70 grain bullet and a muzzle velocity of 2,800 ft/s it was marketed for big game. In fact, Savage ran testimonials of foolish feats like a 400-pound Tiger taken by Reverend H. R. Caldwell with .22 Savage Hi-Power. Or W.M.D. Bell, the demigod of inadequate rifle cartridges, shooting a Cape buffalo with one. I am sure that probably sold some guns and no doubt got some people killed. But, the truth won out and the cartridge lost favor. Not saying I don’t want one; I do, but not for hunting big game.

Next came the Newton designed .250-3000, which is simply called the .250 Savage today. Newton designed it with a 100 grain bullet, but the marketing folks at Savage wanted that “3000” in the name and reduced the bullet weight to 87 grains to achieve that goal. Terminal performance was erratic until the ammo companies finally listened to Newton and made ammo with a 100 grain bullet. Sadly, he never saw it come to fruition as he died just before the ammo was introduced.

The .300 Savage is the flagship. It was 1920 and the war was over. The doughboys coming home had seen the .30-06 in action and they were impressed. Savage knew that the cartridge would not work in the Model 99, so they created one that would. They shortened the .30-06 case, made a short neck and a sharp shoulder so they could pack all the powder possible into the case. The result was a 150-grain bullet at 2,700 ft/s. That matched the sporting load for the .30-06 at the time. (The velocity was later increased in the .30-06 to 2,900 ft/s.) The .300 Savage soon became one of the most popular hunting cartridges and many gun makers other than Savage chambered it in their rifles. In addition to my Savage Model 99 I have a Remington Model 760 pump action and a Remington Model 722 bolt action rifle in .300 Savage, two of the more popular rifles in that cartridge.

When the military was looking for a new short action cartridge they looked at the .300 Savage. In the end they modified the case to create the 7.62 NATO which was introduced to the sporting world as the .308 Winchester. The pity is that the .308 killed off the .300 Savage. It wasn’t long before the .308 was outselling the .300 even in the Model 99. I don’t think any major rifle maker offers the .300 Savage today. Not even Savage.

The Model 99 has been chambered for a lot of cartridges over its life span. See the list below. In addition to the .303 and .22 HP my bucket list includes a .358 Winchester and the very rare .284 Winchester. The trouble is, the folks I have encountered selling them are asking prices that make a gun writer question his career choices.

The cartridges chambered in the Savage Model 1899 and Savage Model 99 over the years.

The early guns were not clearly marked with model information and trying to figure out which model a gun is can drive a Baptist Deacon to a hard drink. Mine is a 99-EG as far as I can figure out. It was made in 1936 and has smooth, non-checkered wood, which adds a premium to the value. But, it hardly matters now. At some point the gun was drilled and tapped for a scope, which detracts from the collector’s value but adds to the “usability” of the rifle. There is a Weaver 1.5-4.5 Wide Angle scope on it with Weaver mounts. This TV screen scope was introduced in 1973 to compete with the Redfield Widefield scopes and it makes me think it was added to the rifle in the mid-seventies. Sling swivels have also been added and at some point a skilled craftsman repaired a cracked stock. Which means this is no collector’s rifle. That suits me fine; I bought it to hunt with, just as Arthur Savage intended.

My rifle has what I think is the 1926 style front sight ramp that is integral with the barrel. I have to wonder what that would cost to reproduce today. The rifle sold in 1939 for $47.00. So I am guessing that when mine was made three years earlier it was maybe $45.00? That barely buys a box of ammo today.

It was a sad day in 1998 when I got a call from the marketing director of Savage to inform me that the Model 99 was being discontinued. I had a gun on loan for an article which with that call changed its focus. The gun was shabbily made and unlike the elegant 99s of the past, it felt like a dead fence post in my hands. The truth is, if this is all they could produce, it was time to stop. I should have bought it at the very good price he offered, but I didn’t. I returned it to Savage and mourned the end of a dynasty.

Read Next: The 300 Savage: A (Nearly) Perfect Mid-Range Hunting Cartridge

Savage experienced some hard times shortly after the Model 99 was dropped. Ron Colburn took over the company and made a drastic move to drop every single product except the Model 110. Everybody said he was crazy, but he saved the company. Savage today is healthy, vibrant, and is building good guns.

Once Ron’s brilliance became apparent and Savage began growing and prospering we all asked when he would bring back the Model 99.

“Never,” he said with his Irish brogue. “It’s just too expensive to make because it requires a lot of hand fitting of parts. Besides, the market isn’t there anymore. I wish it wasn’t true, I dearly love the rifle, but that’s reality.”

Ron never wavered from that until the day he retired.

The same question came up with the current owners during a press conference in early 2020 and the Savage folks provided the same answer almost by rote.

Most recently I contacted Jessica Treglia, Sr. Brand Manager at Savage Arms. Here is what she said:

“We have no current plans to bring back the M99. The cost to manufacture this gun would push the price above $1500 and would not be well received by our customers and thus the volume would not cover the cost to tool up to make the rifle. That said, a lever action is not in our current plan but is on the long term one. It will not, however, be a M99.”

I am of the belief that this “conventional” wisdom is wrong. Much has changed since the Model 99 stopped production. The “high cost due to the labor of hand fitting the parts” is no longer an excuse.

With modern computer controlled machinery and new manufacturing techniques Savage can reduce greatly the labor cost. I am of the belief that the rifle could be made and sold at a price the market will accept.

The question is simply that, the market. Is it there to support a return of the rifle? Sadly, I think the answer is no. The world has moved on. There is a bit of a nostalgic return of interest in lever-action rifles and surprisingly some movement to using them as self-defense rifles in states with harsh gun laws. But, that is aimed at the more traditional lever actions. Sure, a lot of the diehard gun guys would buy a new Model 99 if it was properly made. But, sadly, there just are not enough of us to justify the cost of bringing it back. However, with Savage’s new ownership … who knows what the future might hold.

#8342. Zero In Hagerstown, MD

Today’s post is a brief memo covering my emotions heading into my final 4 day hiking stint. Mostly I am praying I don’t forget anything!

It was difficult to write up the posts from the last two days. The problem was two-fold. Nerves for the upcoming 4 days on trail and ensuring I had thought of everything so I could attempt the 4 state challenge on Sunday. It would be easy to forget a single thing, like my power bank which I rarely take with me. I was also trying to give myself a mental break, but writing the blog posts takes mental energy, so I was battling a catch-22.

The latest weather outlook looks almost too promising to be true. It looks like it will be reaching 60 degrees each of the upcoming 4 days. Overnight lows are hovering around 40 degrees. That would be a miracle given the time of year. Sunday does look cloudy, so catching the sunset at Pen Mar park might fall a bit flat, but if I am successful with my 4 state challenge attempt nothing will be able to dull the ceremony.

Perhaps the only item of note is that I have been unable to restock my whole milk powder, all the stores in the area have run out and there seems to be a supply issue. I am making a plan to replace my usual breakfast and dinner with something else, probably granola bars and beef sticks. Not ideal, but hopefully a minor change that I can negotiate without impacting my 4 state challenge attempt.

I’ll wrap up the post here, its only been two days since my last zero. There isn’t anything to report that you haven’t read at least a few times already.

Hopefully the butterflies in my stomach are more due to the 3 cups of coffee rather than actual nerves that will seep over into my hiking time tomorrow.

#8335. ‘My Knees Got Weak.’ Bowhunter Arrows 222-Inch Buck After Almost Leaving His Stand Twice

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Carpenter Enos Miller climbed into a 15-foot ladder stand overlooking a cut beanfield the afternoon of Nov. 11. It was a private farm in Northern Ohio that he’d hunted a few times.

“I had a ladder stand in a grown-up fence row along the edge of a bean field,” Miller tells Outdoor Life. “I got in the stand and started getting ready to hunt when a train suddenly appeared rolling along the tracks not too far away … I figured it would go by, but it stopped. I thought no deer was going to come by with a train nearby.”

Reluctantly, Miller stayed on his stand despite the disruption, remaining patient and watchful. Then a woodchipper started working very close to his stand.

“I figured that was it for the hunt,” says the 35-year-old from Ashland County. “I gathered my gear for leaving the stand again. But 400 yards away on the far side of the field I spotted a deer, and through my binoculars I saw it was a nice buck.”

“I decided to stay in my stand and just watch the deer to learn more about it and maybe hunt a different spot another day.”

Miller monitored the giant buck for 90 minutes as it chased a half-dozen does around far across the field.

As evening approached, a small buck and a doe appeared on the field edge near Miller. And that seemed to get the attention of the distant buck.

“The does he’d been after across the field were gone, and the big buck started toward me and the little buck and doe,” Miller says. “I knew he was a good buck, but even with binoculars I couldn’t see all the kicker points. I never thought he’d come to me, but he just walked straight in.”

Miller says the buck never looked at him, and when the deer stepped behind some nearby brush, he drew his compound.

“I grunted to stop him, and he looked up at me broadside at 11 yards.”

Miller released his arrow and the buck took off. The moment was recorded on trail cam footage — the first time his camera had ever captured the buck.

The buck ran 80 yards and piled up. Miller texted his wife and brother that he’d shot a good buck, but he still didn’t realize how exceptional the deer was yet. He waited about 10 minutes before climbing down from his stand

“When I walked up to recover him my knees got weak, I started counting points, and I thanked the Lord.”

Soon his family arrived to load the buck into a vehicle and take photos of Miller and his huge whitetail.

The field-dressed buck weighed 190 pounds, and a taxidermist aged it at 5 1/2.

Two days later Miller brought his buck to Toby Hughes, a Buckmasters official who scored the nontypical 29-point deer at 222 4/8 inches. The buck has 27-inch main beams with an inside spread of 20 inches.

Miller’s deer is the 20th buck Hughes has measured at 200 inches or better this year (all but one has been taken in Ohio). Miller’s whitetail also is the 201st buck over 200 inches that Hughes has measured for Buckmasters, almost all of which came from Ohio.

Hughes is just one of over three dozen Buckmasters scorers in Ohio, so the number of bucks scoring 200 inches or more coming from the Buckeye State is truly remarkable.

Read Next: ‘Over the Moon.’ Ohio Hunter Shoots 215-Inch Buck from Her Favorite Stand on Her Birthday

“God blessed me with this remarkable buck,” Miller says. “I put in a lot of hours hunting. But to think I almost climbed down out of this stand twice that afternoon … There’s no doubt in my mind that God was watching out for me.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8334. American Climber Dies at 5,800m After Summiting Mera Peak

Richard Carlos Skidmore, 66, of the United States, was found dead in his tent on Mera Peak on Sunday, November 22, after summiting the 6,476m mountain the previous morning.

According to Solukhumbu district police, he reached the summit at approximately 10am on Saturday, November 21, and descended to the high camp at around 5,800m, where he spent the night.

He was found dead the next morning. Past Mera Peak climbers have suggested on social media that it was unusual to overnight in the high camp after summiting so early in the day.

His body was evacuated by helicopter to Kathmandu for an autopsy. The cause of death remains undetermined. An initial report raised the possibility of altitude-related illness or a cardiac event, but neither has been established medically.

Skidmore previously summited Lobuche Peak in 2024, according to the Everest Chronicle and the Italian Alpine Club’s publication Lo Scarpone. So he had previous Himalayan climbing experience above 6,000m.

The Himalayan Database does include Mera Peak, but its coverage of popular trekking peaks is selective. However, it notes that since 2018, four people have died on Mera Peak, not counting Skidmore.

#8332. New Route on Classic Tien Shan Peak

Three Polish climbers have established a new line on the north face of one of the Tien Shan’s best-known climbing peaks.

Michal Makowski, Robert Paluch, and Rafal Zając took 11 hours to climb their new 850m line on 4,777m Free Korea Peak, in Kyrgyzstan’s northern Tien Shan Mountains. They then spent a further 11 hours returning to base camp.

They named the route The Polish Barber, after pioneer American climber Henry Barber, who soloed the wall in 1976. Their line shares the first 250–300m with Barber’s, Makowski told ExplorersWeb.

“According to the information I have, [the remaining 600-650m] was previously unclimbed and had never been attempted by another team,” said Makowski. “We reached the summit ridge and then began rappelling down from there.

Corona Hut, the team’s base camp. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Free Korea Peak — Pik Svobodnaya Koreya in Russian — was already famous among mountaineers in the former Soviet Union because of its 900m north wall. in 2018, Russian climber Valery Balesin suggested that it was no longer possible to find new lines on this well-known face, although advances in steep ice-climbing techniques have clearly opened new possibilities.

“My friends and I researched Free Korea Peak and realized that part of the north face still has unclimbed terrain,” said Makowski.

On the Polish Barber route. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Their route was almost completely ice climbing. The crux came midway up the wall, when they faced a particularly steep section of ice that they graded at Alpine Ice 4+. The rest of the route was 3/3+. Fortunately, “protection on the Polish Barber was really good, except for the last 150m, where ice was pretty thin and crusty,” said Makowski.

Climbing by moonlight. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

Despite its former popularity, they had the valley almost to themselves during their week in camp. There was just one Russian group doing a tourist trek up nearby Corona Peak.

A scrambly section on Free Korea Peak. Photo: Polski Związek Alpinizmu

#8331. Same Clothes, New Country- Camino Day 49

I woke up around 6:30 AM, and since breakfast wasn’t until 7:30, I had time to relax with a cup of instant coffee and take in the misty ocean view.

Every morning, we share the exact same joke. One of us asks, “What do you want to do today?” and the answer is always, “Let’s take a long walk.” The follow-up question is, “What should I wear?”—which is equally funny since we only own one set of clothes.

Today was a big milestone: the day we finally catch a ferry out of Spain and cross into Portugal.

After a great buffet breakfast at the hotel, we started hiking at 8:30 AM. The fog was still rolling in off the water, leaving the ocean barely visible as waves crashed against the rocks below us. Through the mist ahead, a massive church suddenly appeared—the nearly 900-year-old Monastery of Oia, nestled right along the coast in a picturesque little town.

Most of the morning followed dirt paths running parallel to the main coastal highway, occasionally merging onto a wide, yellow-paved shoulder. Reaching the end of the peninsula, we took a shortcut through a quiet town and arrived at the ferry dock around 12:30 PM.

While waiting, I grabbed a squid sandwich and a café con leche, while Knockerz ordered tea and some rather overcooked chicken fingers. Our ferry reservation wasn’t until 4:00 PM, so we hoped to snag space on an earlier boat.

Soon after our food arrived, a small ferry pulled up—a boat small enough to hold only about six passengers. Fortunately, only two other people were heading across, so we hopped right on.

Once we stepped ashore in Portugal, I checked my phone and realized we had gained an hour in a new time zone. Better yet, we had officially made it to a new country, bringing a whole new culture and language to explore.

Right on the waterfront stood a striking cathedral that felt noticeably different from the churches in Spain. Inside, decorative tiles lined the ceiling alongside carved wood, model boats hung from a side altar, and an intricate retablo resembled a tree growing out of a saint.

Since Portugal is famous for port wine, I stopped at a local bar to try a 10-year-old tawny port. It was sweet with a distinct licorice-syrup flavor. Next, I ordered a local red wine that arrived in a massive glass filled all the way to the brim—for just $1.50. Most places only pour a small portion, so I was shocked by the value.

While chatting with fellow southbound Camino hikers we had met on the boat, I accidentally knocked over and spilled my glass. As we wiped up the mess, the waiter walked right back out and refilled my glass completely free of charge.

Because Knockerz hadn’t eaten her stale chicken fingers on the ferry, she ordered a fresh meal at the table. Our entire bill came out to just €8. Soon, a group of pilgrims from Quebec joined our table for a lively conversation before we set off to find a grocery store and our apartment.

Along the way, we got distracted by an empty, historic church filled with centuries-old artwork. This town is truly stunning.

When we finally tried to locate our apartment, we couldn’t quite find the entrance—and immediately experienced how warm and laid-back Portugal is. Several locals stopped to help us search. One man—who looked like he worked for the local water company—asked for our host’s phone number, called them directly, and stayed with us until we reached the door. When we thanked him in Portuguese, he pointed to the Camino bracelet on his wrist and smiled, “Bom Caminho.” We were completely blown away by the kindness.

After dropping our packs and heading back out for dinner, I noticed the side wall of our apartment actually shared a wall with the back of an old church altar (important detail for tomorrows story!). We picked up a few groceries, sat down in the main square with a couple of glasses of wine, and looked out over the white-capped water separating Spain from Portugal. Even though we thoroughly loved Spain, we couldn’t be happier to finally be in Portugal.

#8328. The 6 Best Survival Movies of All Time, Plus 2 We Hate

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 5:02 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Rarely do we tout ways to spend time inside on the couch. But we’re a versatile bunch, and everyone needs rest and relaxation every now and then.  If you’re going to have a movie night, of course we’re going to recommend films with an outdoor component—particularly ones that involve a bit of a suckfest. In this sense, survival movies are always the right choice.

Good survival movies, much like the best survival books, are hard to beat. But survival movies are really easy to screw up, too. With that in mind, we had to include a couple that really missed the mark. These movies and the stories they tell are good for a lesson on what not to do and how nature doesn’t work.

Good survival movies require a few key components. Without checking these boxes, a survival movie won’t survive this room of tough critics.

After mulling over the countless options, our editors picked their favorite survival movies of all time based on how realistic, engaging, and stomach-churning they are. Disclaimer: spoilers ahead.

In 1972 a flight carrying 45 people, including an Uruguayan rugby team, crashed in the remote Andes mountains at high elevation, surrounded by nothing but snow, rock, and sky. After two months of facing the elements, an avalanche, and starvation, only 18 passengers survived—largely by eating the deceased members of their group. Eventually, two of the survivors set out on a journey to find help, one that would take them 38 miles over a 15,000-foot peak. Alive is based on that true story. It’s a film that makes you consider what you would do to survive, and is a great example of the most important survival asset: the will to live. —Scott Einsmann

Based on the harrowing (and true) survival story of Yossi Ghinsberg, Jungle features Daniel Radcliffe as the adventurous backpacker, anxious to see the unseen and explore the unexplored. A shady guide offers to take him deep into the Bolivian jungle where the group is separated and Ghinsberg must find his own way out of the rainforest. Natural horrors abound and, eventually, our hero is forced to cut open his own forehead. I found the movie dark, realistic, and skin crawling. The film keeps you invested with every fresh hell Ghinsberg faces, and provokes disturbing thoughts about human nature, adventure tourism, and the lengths you might go to escape the mundane in pursuit of an authentic and extraordinary life. —Ashley Thess

The story of Hugh Glass’ 200-mile odyssey is one of the great frontier survival tales of all time. Who cares if Leonardo DiCaprio’s portrayal of the story is largely fictionalized. The film’s scenery is stunning, the action is thrilling, and the bear mauling scene is brutal and iconic. If nothing else, DiCaprio does a good job portraying how horrible the whole experience must have been. If you want a more realistic retelling of Hugh Glass’ adventure, try reading Lord Grizzly. —Alex Robinson

The Way Back is one of my favorite survival movies. It’s based on the true story of a few Soviet prisoners who escaped a Siberian Gulag and adapted from the book, The Long Walk, and follows the characters as they walk south across the Gobi desert, communist China, and ultimately arrive in India. I know how bad it hurts to trek around the mountains of Alaska for two weeks at a time with all the modern backpacker’s comforts. This is an unfathomable story of persistence and survival. —Tyler Freel

I’ve seen a number of mountaineering documentaries and docudramas (go watch The Alpinist now if you haven’t seen it yet) but the one that lives rent-free in my head is Touching the Void. It reenacts the true story of two climbers, Joe Simpson and Simon Yates, who were attempting a first ascent of the West Face of Siula Grande in Peru. They do make it to the top, but things go downhill (literally and figuratively) after that: bones are broken, storms ensue. At what appears to be the crux of the story, Yates is forced to cut the rope during a belay (lest both men be killed) and Simpson crashes through the glacier into a deep crevasse.

Yates, believing his partner dead, returns to their basecamp. But Simpson isn’t dead, simply too deep in the crevasse to call up to the surface for help. I won’t spoil what happens next but it’s a survival maneuver that still has me in awe 20 years later. —Laura Lancaster

Based on the true story and #1 New York Times Bestseller by the same title, Unbroken follows Olympic track star Louis Zamperini as he goes from fame and glory in the 1936 Berlin games to the lowest lows a human can endure. After enlisting in the Army Air Corps at the onset of World War II, Zamperini’s plane goes down over the vast South Pacific, forcing him and two comrades, Phil and Mac, to survive for weeks in a pair of scarcely-supplied life rafts floating over shark-infested waters in enemy territory. They survive by fishing with hooks baited with seagull meat, catching rainwater, and wrestling small sharks out of the water with their bare hands. After 47 days adrift, during which time Mac dies, Zamperini and Phil aren’t exactly rescued. Instead, their salvation comes in the form of capture, imprisonment, and torture by the Japanese military.

What makes Zamperini’s story one of the best survival tales of all time? Just when the harrowing wilderness survival component comes to an end, the real hell begins. —Katie Hill

Based on a true story, Into the Wild is one of the worst survival movies, and not least because the hero dies. From a survival standpoint, Chris McCandless was horribly unprepared to survive independently on the Alaskan landscape, and he would have probably succumbed earlier had he not found the bus. Also, he wasn’t in a very remote area. My family used the bus as our hunting camp until 1972, and would drive their pickup trucks out there. Unfortunately for McCandless, he decided to turn back too late, and unfortunately for the bus, it was forever turned into a tourist trap and, ultimately, removed from the landscape. –Tyler Freel

This prequel to Predator is set in 1719 and only worth watching if you can suspend disbelief. Cool sci-fi movie? Definitely. Interesting characters? Sure. Accurate survival movie? Absolutely not.

The protagonist, Naru, is a Comanche teen desperate to prove herself a hunter and warrior among male peers. The script, however, has her perform all kinds of super-human stunts. Naru spooks a buck she’s trying to kill with a tomahawk and proceeds to run alongside it at top speed through the timber. While chasing the deer, Naru’s dog somehow trips a double long spring foothold trap with the tip of his tail. The running buck sounds like a galloping horse, complete with a whinny and bull elk chirps. Any outdoorsman can spot the problems. Later, Naru attempts to kill a mountain lion by using herself as live bait, a creative interpretation of big-cat hunting tactics. Worst of all, she manages to outrun and outswim a charging grizzly bear—which can cover 20 yards in a single second—while calf-deep in a river.

The movie is set in the northern Great Plains but shows sweeping landscapes of the Canadian Rockies. (Since I don’t know what kind of tech the Predator was working with, I’ll overlook how his thermal imaging was foiled by metabolization of a flower that “cools the blood,” rendering a warm human body invisible to heat signatures in mere seconds.) I can set aside realism to entertain most any sci-fi world, but this movie takes place on Planet Earth, with real wildlife and real people. Indeed, the whole plot hinges on human resourcefulness. True wilderness survival is in the details, and Prey gets them all wrong. —Natalie Krebs

Research has shown that we get hooked on survival movies and shows because we experience adrenaline-inducing situations without the negative emotions of being in immediate danger. In this sense, watching survival movies is sort of like seeing great white sharks from inside an underwater cage or soaring in a skydiving simulator. Plus, survival movies usually come with a healthy dose of character development, nail-biting action, and occasional romance.

While it’s difficult to track down the first survival movie, films like The Sea Beast (1926), Five Came Back (1939), and Lifeboat (1944) are some of the earliest survival films ever made. The Sea Beast was the first movie ever based on Herman Melville’s Moby Dick. Five Came Back is about an airplane with a varied mix of passengers that crashes in the Amazon. Lifeboat is about, well, a lifeboat, and its passengers who must survive on emergency rations in the wake of a Nazi U-boat torpedoing and sinking their passenger ship.

Survival movies can teach us many lessons. We can learn from the mistakes protagonists make that land them in difficult situations and we can learn from their ingenuity that ultimately leads to their rescue. These movies also inform us how to gear up for our next adventure (or, at least, what to look for in the best survival kits). We can also consider our own actions in similar situations and learn a thing or two about our own abilities and morals. How far would you go to survive in these situations?

Nothing compares to a really good survival movie. They can take you from the highest highs to the lowest lows and back again, all within a few hours. The next time you’re trying to choose between the same old horror flicks or true crime docs, take a walk on the wild side and pick one of our favorite survival movies instead. Ration your popcorn accordingly.

Katie Hill is a contributor for Outdoor Life where she covers outdoor news, hunting, and conservation in the West. She was born and raised on the East Coast but relocated to Missoula, Montana, in 2019 to earn her master’s degree in environmental journalism.

#8326. Day 40: The Meditation After the Party

I awoke at 9 am to the sound of an alarm clock that wasn’t mine. It was the sound I dreaded to hear.

My Italian brother was getting back on the Camino while my Argentinian brother took a rest day.

I walked my Italian brother out, hugging him multiple times and not wanting to let go. We met on the first day of the Camino, and over the course of the journey, he had become one of the most important people in my life.

Hug after hug after hug, neither of us wanting it to be the last.

The longer the goodbye went on, the more emotional he became.

Tears streamed down his face right before he put on his sunglasses to cover his eyes.

My Argentinian brother and I packed up our backpacks and leisurely left the place we were staying. On the way out, the Venezuelan woman who ran the property told us to come back because “es tu casa”.

We went directly to the cathedral plaza. With our other pilgrim friends, we soaked up the sun knowing it’d be the last day we would see it due to the weather forecast being full of rain over the next few days.

Others were staying, but we all didn’t want to move from that spot and have to say any more goodbyes.

But my Argentinian brother and I had a mission to go on: our meditation in the woods.

Together, we walked forty minutes to a park outside of the city.

There, I was able to process the immensity of emotions and thoughts that Santiago, my birthday, the goodbyes, and thoughts of the future brought.

I realized some powerful things about what was next for my life.

And in all of it, my brother was by my side.

As the afternoon progressed, we kept moving spots to where the sun was. The shade was cold but the sun felt like a warm blanket of comfort.

We watched the clouds slowly swallow the sun. I was getting cold, but my brother was lying on the ground with no desire to leave. In Spanish, he said,

The day together was exactly what we both needed. We were held by nature in a way we couldn’t frequently experience on the Camino.

Every good thing must come to an end. Other pilgrim friends I hadn’t seen in a while made it to Santiago, and it was time to meet them.

My brother and I walked arm in arm through the city to the apartment the dads rented. It took us to the side of the city we hadn’t yet explored, and it was beautiful. Old stone streets, large parks with open spaces, old trees, and views of the city.

The old tree that overlooked the cathedral called to me. Without a care what the locals thought, I ran to the tree and hugged it like it was a long-lost family member.

It was so refreshing to see the dads and catch up as we walked around town to a restaurant for dinner.

Two more pilgrims joined us, and it was a great meal, but I could feel my body shifting and a cold coming on.

We went to the pilgrim pub, Momo, for one drink and then walked back to the cozy apartment to rest much earlier than the night before.

On the comfy couch, my Argentinian brother and I watched videos together from the night before.

So much joy filled my heart reflecting on the past 48 hours.

#8325. Ukrainian Solos New Route in Gokyo Valley, Near Everest

Mykyta Balabanov of Ukraine has soloed a new route on the northeast face of Nepal’s Phari Lapcha (6,017m). Also known as Machhermo Peak, it sits 24km west of Everest in the Gokyo Valley.

Balabanov is still trekking back to Lukla, but has given ExplorersWeb some details and photos about this impressive new route.

The Ukrainian set off on his own up an unclimbed line on the northeast face on November 19. He made two bivouacs on the way up and one more on the descent.

“The level of difficulty is hard to tell, as always when solo, but I’d say it was around 80° on ice, M5+, 5c on rock, and lots of poor snow!” Balavanov said.

He estimates the new route is 1,200m long, and spans some 850 vertical meters from base to summit.

Rough topo of Balabanov’s solo route on the northeast face of Phari Lapcha. Photo: Mykyta Balabanov

Another side of Phari Lapcha saw action just one day before Balabanov, when U.S. guide Bennie Lieber led Canadian Emma Deguara up the Stone(d) Yeti route on the south face.

“I was not related to that climb, as I climbed alone after approaching from a different valley,” Balabanov explained. “However, I saw their tracks on the summit and spotted their bivy place at the bottom of the south face.”

Lieber and Deguara made the second ascent of their chosen route. “While the technical grade (M3, AI2+) is easily digestible, the room for mistakes, due to exposure, is zero,” Lieber said.

Mykyta Balabanov and fellow Ukrainian Mikhail Fomino opened a new route on Ama Dablam in 2024, but they first became widely known for their new route on the Southeast Ridge of Annapurna III in 2021.

#8323. High Winds Thwart Latest Everest Attempt

The Russian team has returned to Everest, aiming to reach at least 7,500m, but high winds have again dashed their plans.

The three-man team of Andrei Vasiliev, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov set off yesterday and reached their cached tent at 5,600m. As leader Vasiliev explained, they expected strong winds at summit altitude until Wednesday, but they intended to keep themselves in lower areas, where the wind speed was acceptable.

However, the wind was too strong to go higher than 5,600m today, so they decided to stay put. MountainClub.com, which has access to the team’s InReach tracker, has posted a partial view of the climbers’ current location:

We asked Jost Kobusch of Germany (also planning to climb the West Ridge of Everest this winter) about that location. He confirmed that they are midway up the vertical mixed section leading from the Khumbu Glacier to the Lho La Pass.

This is the expedition’s fourth foray up the mountain. According to Vasiliev, it is also their last chance to acclimatize before a potential summit push. The Russians’ climbing permit is reportedly valid until December 15.

Their future progress will depend on the wind. Multi-model forecasts indicate that winds are expected to decrease on Thursday but not to disappear. The chart by High Asia Avalanche Watch, which compares seven different weather models, predicts that winds will not drop below 45kph at the summit (on Thursday and Friday). They will then pick up again.

Wind speed at the summit of Everest over the next two weeks, by High Asia Avalanche Watch.

#8321. Black Friday Deals On Kifaru Backpacks Will Save You Hundreds

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 24, 2026 5:42 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

I’ve tried a number of hunting packs over the last few years, but my current favorite is Kifaru’s 22Mag. #8320. What Happens When You Come Home From Your Thru-Hike?

This guest post was written by Amy “Yardsale” Johnson. Full bio at the end.

There are endless articles, Youtube videos, books, and discussions about preparing for trail. But what happens when we come back home?

This question receives far less guidance. But for many hikers, “coming home” is one of the hardest parts.

I thru-hiked the Appalachian Trail and hiked 1600 miles of the Continental Divide Trail, and I have felt the challenge of spending months on trail and then coming home to a “society” that feels so at odds with the trail experience.

How might we be able to carry the gifts of the trail with us and create a life and a world that is more deeply alive?

For months, we sleep beneath the stars, watch the Milky Way move across the sky, catch the sunrise, and feel the cool breeze of dawn, brimming over with possibility as birds greet the day. As we walk, carrying everything we need on our backs, demands and expectations fall away.

There is no one left to impress, and no one left to be, other than our raw, unfiltered, beautiful selves. In this rawness and vulnerability, we meet each other. We walk, we talk, we sleep under the stars. We bear witness to one another’s stories, we hold one another up. We watch as the first flowers open in the spring, and then as the first leaves turn crimson, set on fire in the fall.

Somewhere along the way, we find deep connection with ourselves, with others, and with nature. We find more space, more color, more life. For the first time, perhaps, we have eyes to see.

We return to our jobs and routines, and the demands and expectations return too. Suddenly, how people interact with us is dependent on how we look, how much money we make, or where we’re from.

We’re expected to work, probably a lot of hours, cooped up indoors, often without a break. With little appreciation, perhaps little financial reward, and little connection to how our work might benefit people or nature. The core of who we are, which had emerged so quietly on trail, sometimes feels buried once again.

I believe the connection and community that we experience on trail open up an incredible possibility. In returning home, we have a chance to carry these gifts with us. We have a chance to create a kinder and more beautiful world: a world with more space, more color, and more life. I believe we can start by rewriting our own lives, step by step, and then by participating in the rewriting of our own small corner of the world. And maybe, this journey is the most important trail of all.

We can begin by considering how we can incorporate small aspects of our experience into our everyday life (and then more, and more, until our lives are more and more aligned).

After all, it’s one step after another that allowed us to hike for hundreds, or thousands, of miles. Are our everyday lives really so different from the trail? Isn’t the natural world all around us? The birds, the flowers, the trees, right outside our windows? The people around us every day, are they not people just like people on trail?

And if people on trail can form supportive, inclusive, beautiful communities, why shouldn’t we also be able to do that in our daily lives?

We all have the power to act, to respond, to create something in this world. On trail, we persevere in the face of conditions that we have no control over: difficult weather, trail conditions, and the physical limitations of our body. Truly, we are small and weak in the face of the power and beauty of Mother Nature.

But we have the power to respond, participate, and persevere. And as it turns out, that power ends up taking us pretty far. From Georgia to Maine, or Mexico to Canada, or any distance that we so choose.

Similarily, in society, we are all small and weak in the face of many of the powerful forces around us. Our culture, the economy, the govermment, and the people around us are all forces that we have little control over. But just like on trail, we always have the power to respond, to participate, and to persevere. And I believe that power can take us pretty far. To a better world, I venture to imagine.

I think we can get there by leaning into the same things that we leaned into on trail: community, perseverance, connection with nature, trust in ourselves, and other people.

Before hiking, I never thought I could hitchhike, that I could navigate remote wilderness on my own, or that I could literally walk from Georgia to Maine. I never imagined that I could break so many “rules” of our society. But I did, and what I found through that vulnerability was deep connection, deep love, faith, and an abiding confidence.

Might it be that we are playing by unspoken rules in our everyday lives that might be holding us back from true confidence, true connection, and new possibilities?

What might happen if we start to question them, and connect anyway?

When we have an urgent need on trail, oftentimes we have no choice but to ask for help. So like it or not, we swallow our pride, get over our fears, and ask some stranger if they can help. Usually, they do, and gladly.

What might happen if we began to do that in our everyday lives? What might happen if we offered that same level of support to others?

What new possibilities might break open, once we begin to realize that the trail was never separate from society, nor was the end of our hike the end of our journey.

What might the next steps look like in practice?

Let’s start with a few questions to consider. Everyone has their own unique experience on trail, and chances are, no two hikers’ “return-home journeys” will be the same. Journaling is one tool that I’ve found helpful in processing my experiences.

What did you experience on trail? How did you feel, and what new perspectives did you find? What aspects of your hike contributed to feeling the way you did?

What perspectives or ways of being did you find on trail that you want to hold onto? What are some small steps you can take to incorporate those perspectives or ways of being into your daily life?

Additionally, below are some of the small practical changes that I’ve found helpful. Have your own practices? Add them to the conversation.

1. Spend the first half hour or more of the day doing something just for you: outside and active is even better. This can set the tone for the entire day. The day belongs to you, to nature, to life itself. Everything else fits into that deeper truth.

4. Get outside regularly. It could be day hikes, weekend backpacking trips, or just taking in the sunlight for 5 minutes from your porch.

5. Along the lines of getting outside: connect with God, the universe, nature, or a sense of meaning beyond yourself.

6. Stay in touch with trail friends, and find like-minded people at home. Going on the post-trail journey with people “who get it” is immeasurably valuable.

7. Find ways to have the authentic relationships like the ones you found on trail. How might you be able to give and receive help more freely, and be yourself without reserve, with the people closest to you?

8. Find joy in the small things. Maybe it’s a fresh bouquet of flowers from the farmers market, the sunlight streaming in through the window, or the birds chirping.

9. Think about how your work can contribute to others, to yourself, and to nature. Start to think about how you might be able to negotiate your work, or your relationship with work, to find better work-life balance, and/or a deeper sense of meaning and contribution. It doesn’t all have to be better all at once. Small steps in the right direction is all that’s needed.

10. Learn about money. Things like budgeting, the time value of money, how debt works, saving and investing. Like it or not, our world runs on money. Money is one of the major keys to more freedom in our world. Use it as a tool to create more space, joy, abundance, and freedom for yourself, your community, and nature.

11. Acceptance. Accepting things we can’t control allows us to have more peace. And we can lean into that which we can control: our response, which is powerful. Just like on trail.

Amy “Yardsale” Johnson is a long-distance hiker, writer, speaker, retreat host, and founder of the Blue Compass Institute. She creates writing and experiences that break open possibilities so that people can create more deeply thriving and connected lives. Her work can be found at https://www.bluecompassinstitute.com, or follow along on Instagram @bluecompassinstitute.
